Pseudo-Kähler structure on the SL ⁡ ( 3 , ℝ ) \mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R}) -Hitchin component and Goldman symplectic form
Nicholas Rungi, Andrea Tamburelli

Abstract
. The aim of this paper is to show the existence and give an explicit description of a pseudo-Riemannian metric and a symplectic form on the SL ⁡ ( 3 , ℝ ) \SL(3,\mathbb{R}) -Hitchin component, both compatible with Labourie and Loftin’s complex structure. In particular, they give rise to a mapping class group invariant pseudo-Kähler structure on a neighborhood of the Fuchsian locus, which restricts to a multiple of the Weil-Petersson metric on Teichmüller space. By comparing our symplectic form with Goldman’s 𝝎 G \boldsymbol{\omega}_{G} , we prove that the pair ( 𝝎 G , 𝐈 ) (\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G},\mathbf{I}) cannot define a Kähler structure on the Hitchin component.



1.
Introduction

Historically, the deformation space
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
of convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures on a smooth, closed and orientable surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 2
, was the very first example of a higher rank Teichmüller space parameterizing geometric structures on the surface (see
[
Wie18
]
for a survey on this topic). It generalizes the notion of hyperbolic structures and, because of this, it contains a copy of Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{h}}(\Sigma)
. In the early nineties, Goldman proved that
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
is homeomorphic to a cell of real dimension
-8\chi(\Sigma)
(
[
Gol90
]
) and by using works of Koszul (
[
Kos65
]
,
[
Kos68
]
), it was shown (
[
Gol90
]
) that
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
embeds as an open submanifold in the representation space
\mathrm{Hom}\big(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\SL(3,\mathbb{R})\big)/\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
. Three years later, Choi and Goldman (
[
CG93
]
) proved that
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
is indeed homeomorphic to a connected component Hit
{}_{3}(\Sigma)
of the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-character variety, found by Hitchin using techniques from Higgs bundles theory (see
[
Hit92
]
for a more general result). It consists of representations obtained as deformation of the
Fuchsian
ones, i.e. those arising as holonomy of a hyperbolic structure, and nowadays it is called the
Hitchin component
. In the early 2000s, Labourie (
[
Lab07
]
) and Loftin (
[
Lof01
]
) proved independently, using the theory of hyperbolic affine spheres and harmonic maps in symmetric spaces, that the space
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
can be endowed with a mapping class group invariant complex structure
\mathbf{I}
. Such a complex structure comes from the identification between
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
and the total space of the holomorphic bundle of cubic differentials over
\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{h}}(\Sigma)
.

A natural question, after the parameterization by Labourie and Loftin, is to look for a Riemannian metric (or symplectic form) on
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
that gives rise to a Kähler structure, extending the Weil-Petersson metric, once coupled with
\mathbf{I}
. The first attempt was made by Loftin (
[
Lof01
]
) with the Goldman symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}
(
[
Gol84
]
), defined using the algebraic description
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)\cong
Hit
{}_{3}(\Sigma)
found by Choi and Goldman. Nowadays, it is still unclear whether
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\mathbf{I}\cdot,\cdot)
is a Riemannian metric. Later on, three more Riemannian metrics on the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component were defined: one by Darvishzadeh and Goldman (
[
DW96
]
), one by Li (
[
Li16
]
) and another by Bridgeman-Canary-Labourie-Sambarino (
[
Bri+15
]
) called
pressure metric
(defined also on much more general spaces). Regarding the first two it has been shown that they restrict to a multiple of the Weil-Petersson metric on Teichmüller space, which is totally geodesic in Hit
{}_{3}(\Sigma)
with respect to the metric found by Li. As far as pressure metric is concerned, very little is known and this is partly due to its complicated expression (
[
LW18
]
,
[
Dai19
]
). In all three cases the relation with Labourie and Loftin’s complex structure is unknown.

Recently, Kim and Zhang (
[
KZ17
]
), using various notions of positivity for holomorphic bundles on Kähler manifolds, have succeeded in showing the existence of a Kähler metric on Hit
{}_{3}(\Sigma)
, which restricts to a multiple of the Weil-Petersson one on the Fuchsian locus. Even if this metric is natural, namely invariant under the action of the mapping class group, the relation of its complex structure with the one found by Labourie and Loftin is still mysterious (see the discussion in
[
Lab17
, §1.2 and §1.3]
).

In this paper we study the symplectic and pseudo-Riemannian geometry of Hit
{}_{3}(\Sigma)
using a different approach coming from the theory of symplectic reduction in an infinite-dimensional context. Recall that a
pseudo-Riemannian
metric
\mathbf{g}
on a smooth
n
-manifold
M
is an everywhere non-degenerate, smooth, symmetric
(0,2)
-tensor. Let
\mathbf{I}
be a complex structure on
M
, then
(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{I})
is a
pseudo-Hermitian structure
if

\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{I}X,\mathbf{I}Y)=\mathbf{g}(X,Y),\qquad\forall X,Y\in T_{p}M,p\in M\ .

The
fundamental 2-form

\boldsymbol{\omega}
of a pseudo-Hermitian manifold
(M,\mathbf{g},\mathbf{I})
is defined by:

\boldsymbol{\omega}(X,Y):=\mathbf{g}(X,\mathbf{I}Y),\qquad\forall X,Y\in T_{p}M,p\in M\ .

A pseudo-Hermitian manifold
(M,\mathbf{g},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega})
is called
pseudo-Kähler
if the fundamental
2
-form is closed, namely if
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\omega}=0
. In this case the corresponding metric is called
pseudo-Kähler
.

The main result of the paper is the following:

Theorem A
.

There exists a bi-linear, alternating and closed
2
-tensor
\boldsymbol{\omega}
on
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
such that
\mathbf{g}(\cdot,\cdot):=\boldsymbol{\omega}(\mathbf{I}\cdot,\cdot)
defines an indefinite symmetric bi-linear form. Moreover, the triple
(\mathbf{g},\boldsymbol{\omega},\mathbf{I})
gives rise to a mapping class group invariant pseudo-Kähler structure on a neighborhood of the Fuchsian locus in the Hitchin component, and it restricts to a multiple of the Weil-Petersson Kähler metric on Teichmüller space. The signature of the pseudo-Riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}
in the above neighborhood is
(6g-6,10g-10)
.

There is a natural well-defined action of the mapping class group MCG
(\Sigma)
on the spaces
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
and
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
for which the monodromy map is an equivariant isomorphism (see Theorem
2.2
). Therefore, one gets an induced pseudo-Kähler structure on a neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{h}}(\Sigma)}
of Teichmüller space in
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
. The quotient
\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
of the deformation space by the mapping class group is a complex orbifold and it is smooth at generic points (
[
Lof01
, Proposition 4.1.2]
). The above discussion together with Theorem
A
implies the following:

Corollary B
.

There exists an orbifold neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{M}_{g}}
of the moduli space of Riemann surfaces of genus
g\geq 2
inside
\mathcal{C}(\Sigma)
endowed with a pseudo-Kähler orbifold structure. Such a structure restricts to a multiple of the Weil-Petersson orbifold Kähler structure on
\mathcal{M}_{g}
.

The tensors
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\mathbf{g}
will be explicit and defined on the whole
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
but, because we cannot exclude that
\boldsymbol{\omega}
might be degenerate outside the Fuchsian locus, the triple
(\boldsymbol{\omega},\mathbf{g},\mathbf{I})
defines a-priori a pseudo-Kähler structure only on a neighborhood of it. We will also give an interpretation of our pseudo-Kähler structure in terms of hyperbolic affine spheres.

The above theorem is inspired by a similar result obtained in the case of maximal globally hyperbolic anti-de Sitter three-manifolds (
[
MST21
]
), where the authors developed part of the techniques the we used in our work (see Section
1.2
).

The identification between
\mathcal{B}(S)
and the holomorphic vector bundle of cubic differentials over Teichmüller space induces a natural circle action on
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
that corresponds to the rotation along the fibres on
Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)\big)
, i.e.
e^{i\theta}\cdot([J],q):=([J],e^{-i\theta}q)
. The pseudo-Kähler structure we found in Theorem
A
behaves well with respect to this circle action:

Theorem C
.

Let
\rho
be a fixed area form on
\Sigma
, then the circle action on
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
is Hamiltonian with respect to
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and it satisfies:

(1.1)

\Psi_{\theta}^{*}\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g},\quad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{R}\ .

The Hamiltonian function is given by:

H(J,q):=\frac{2}{3}\int_{\Sigma}f\bigg(\frac{||q||^{2}_{g_{J}}}{2}\bigg)\rho,

where
f:[0,+\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
is the smooth function defined by (
2.13
) and
g_{J}(\cdot,\cdot):=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
is a Riemannian metric on the surface.

In the theorem above, we improperly used the notion of "Hamiltonian action" since we know that the symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}
is non-degenerate only on a neighborhood of the Fuchsian locus in
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
. Nevertheless, the relation (
1.1
) and the moment map equation defined by
H(J,q)
continue to hold on the whole Hitchin component.

By work of Goldman, the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
has a natural symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}
. One of the main open question about the geometry of
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
is whether the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
that the Hitchin component inherits from the Labourie-Loftin parameterization is compatible with Goldman symplectic form and the pair
(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G},\mathbf{I})
gives rise to a Kähler structure on the Hitchin component. By comparing our symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}
with
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}
, we give a negative answer to the above question:

Theorem D
.

The symplectic forms
\boldsymbol{\omega}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}
coincide on the Fuchsian locus along all directions, tangent and transverse to the Fuchsian locus. In particular, even if the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
were compatible with
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}
, the metric
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\mathbf{I}\cdot,\cdot)
would be indefinite with signature
(6g-6,10g-10)
, thus the pair
(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G},\mathbf{I})
cannot define a Kähler structure on the Hitchin component.

1.1.
Strategy of the proofs

In this section we explain with some details the techniques used to construct the Hitchin component as an infinite dimensional symplectic quotient. Throughout the exposition we will state other fundamental and non-immediate results needed for the proof of Theorem
A
, Theorem
C
and Theorem
D
. On the one hand the overall strategy follows the lines of the anti-de Sitter case (
[
MST21
, §1.7]
), but on the other we encountered more difficulties during some steps of the proof that will be explained on a case-by-case basis (see also Section
1.2
for a more general discussion).

The genus one case
:

The case when
\Sigma=T^{2}
was studied by the authors in a previous work (
[
RT24
]
). Let us give a brief summary of what has been proven, as the methods used for the genus one case will later be a key part of the general case. Let
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the space of (almost) complex structures on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
compatible with the standard area form
\rho_{0}=\mathrm{d}x\wedge\mathrm{d}y
, namely all the endomorphisms
J:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
J^{2}=-\mathds{1}
and for which
\{v,Jv\}
is a positive basis, whenever
v\neq 0
. For any such
J
, let
g_{J}^{0}(\cdot,\cdot):=\rho_{0}(\cdot,J\cdot)
be the associated scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. There is an identification between this space and the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, so that the action of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
by Möbius transformations results in an action by conjugation on
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. We introduced an explicit family of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariant pseudo-Kähler metrics
(\hat{\mathbf{g}}_{f},\hat{\mathbf{I}},\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f})
, parametrized by a smooth function
f:[0,+\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
, on a vector bundle
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
over
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, whose fibre over a point
J
is given by
\{A\in\End_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{2},g_{J}^{0})\otimes T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\ A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J,\ A(X)Y=A(Y)X\}
, where
A(X)
is a trace-less and
g_{J}^{0}
-symmetric endomorphism of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, for any
X\in T\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Each element of the pseudo-Kähler structure preserves the complement of the zero section in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, which can be identified with the deformation space
\mathcal{B}_{0}(T^{2})
. Moreover, under the above identification the mapping class group action on
\mathcal{B}_{0}(T^{2})
corresponds to the restricted
\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})<\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
minus the zero section (see Theorem 3.13 in
[
RT24
]
).

The general case
:

Now let
\Sigma
be a smooth closed connected and oriented surface of genus
g\geq 2
. The crucial step in moving from the genus one case to the higher genus case, consists in the following construction, which will be developed in full details in Section
4.1
. Let
\rho
be a fixed area form on
\Sigma
, then for any (almost) complex structure
J
on
\Sigma
, let
g_{J}:=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
be the associated Riemannian metric. Now consider the space formed by pairs
(J,A)
, where
J
is an (almost) complex structure on
\Sigma
compatible with the given orientation and
A
is a
1
-form with values in the bundle of trace-less and
g_{J}
-symmetric endomorphisms such that
A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J
and
A(X)Y=A(Y)X,\ \forall X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
. This space, denoted by
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, is of infinite dimension and it carries a pseudo-Kähler structure as its analogue
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. In fact, one can choose an area-preserving linear isomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
T_{x}\Sigma
, which induces an identification between
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and
D^{3}\big(\mathcal{J}(T_{x}\Sigma)\big)
. Since the pseudo-Kähler metric on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariant, the induced structure does not depend on the chosen area-preserving linear isomorphism. Then, one can (formally) integrate each element of the pseudo-Kähler structure on
\Sigma
, evaluated on first-order deformations
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
. Slightly more in detail, let
P
be the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-frame bundle over
\Sigma
whose fibres over a point
x\in\Sigma
are linear maps
F:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to T_{x}\Sigma
such that
F^{*}\rho_{x}
is the standard area form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let us define the fibre bundle

P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P\times D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\SL(2,\mathbb{R})$}\right.}

where
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
acts diagonally on the two factors. The space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
can be identified with the space of smooth sections of such fibre bundle. Hence, as explained above, one can introduce the following formal symplectic form

(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big):=\int_{\Sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big)\rho

and the formal pseudo-Riemannian metric

(\mathbf{g}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big):=\int_{\Sigma}\hat{\mathbf{g}}_{f}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big)\rho

where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}
and
\hat{\mathbf{g}}_{f}
are both induced on each fibre of
T^{\text{vert}}P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
. Here we denoted by
T^{\text{vert}}P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
the vertical sub-bundle of
TP\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
with respect to the projection map
P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)\to\Sigma
. Similarly, a complex structure
\mathbf{I}
is obtained on the infinite-dimensional space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
of smooth sections, by applying point-wise
\hat{\mathbf{I}}
, which is defined on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. It should be noted that the symplectic form
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}
and the pseudo-Riemannian metric
\hat{\mathbf{g}}_{f}
both depend on the choice of a smooth function
f
, as they arise from the construction on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. In particular, the expression for
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
and
\mathbf{g}_{f}
combined with
\mathbf{I}
effectively gives us a (formal) family of pseudo-Kahler metrics on the space of smooth sections
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. Instead, we are interested in inducing such structures on a certain submanifold, whose elements
(J,A)
will be identified with the set of embedding data of hyperbolic affine spheres in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see Section
2.5
). In order to do so, a particular choice of the function
f
appearing in the expression of
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
and
\mathbf{g}_{f}
has to be made. Let
F:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be the unique smooth function such that
ce^{-F(t)}-2te^{-3F(t)}+1=0
, where
c
is a constant depending only on the topology and the area of
(\Sigma,\rho)
. Let us define a new metric in the same conformal class of
g_{J}
by the formula
h:=e^{F(t)}g_{J}
, where the function
F
is computed in
||A||^{2}_{g_{J}}
(the norm of the tensor
A
with respect to
g_{J}
) divided by
8
. Then, imposing equations (
HS
) on the pair
(h,A)
we get a
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-invariant submanifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
of the space of smooth sections
(J,A)
, whose quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
by
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
, is a smooth manifold of dimension
16g-16+2g
. This will be a consequence of a simple application of Moser’s trick in symplectic geometry, of the particular choice of the function
f
in terms of
F
, and finally of the existence and uniqueness of hyperbolic affine sphere immersions in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see the discussion in Section
2.5
for more details). It turns out that such a manifold is not diffeomorphic to the deformation space of convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures, as its dimension exceeds that of
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
by
2g
. As we shall see later, the tangent space to this manifold splits as the
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal direct sum of the tangent space to
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
and the tangent to the orbit of harmonic vector fields (Lemma
6.1
). For this reason, the further (finite-dimensional) quotient of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
by
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cong H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
gives us the desired deformation space.

The candidate for the tangent space to the Hitchin component
:

As explained in the previous paragraph, in order to actually obtain the Hitchin component from the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, and thus induce a pseudo-Kähler structure
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
on it, we need to perform two quotients: the first by
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
and the second by
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
. The idea is to define a distribution
\{W_{(J,A)}\}_{(J,A)}
of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-invariant subspaces inside the tangent space to
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
. Each vector space
W_{(J,A)}
of this distribution will be defined by a system of partial differential equations and will be point-wise isomorphic to the tangent space of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
. The first result that conceals a number of technical difficulties, developed in Section
4.3
, shows, using an argument from the theory of elliptic operators on compact manifolds, that the dimension of each
W_{(J,A)}
is bounded below by the expected dimension of the quotient manifold.

Theorem E
.

Let
(J,A)
be a point in the infinite-dimensional space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
. Let
W_{(J,A)}
be the vector space of solutions of the following system:

(1.2)

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\big(\divr\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\big)=0\\
\mathrm{d}\big(\divr\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)\circ J+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}_{0}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\circ J\big)=0\\
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\divr\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0\end{cases}

where
\beta(\bullet):=\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle
is a
1
-form and
\dot{f}_{0}=-\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle
is a smooth function on
\Sigma
. Then,
\dim W_{(J,A)}\geq 16g-16+2g
.

The second difficult statement, which will be consequence of the above theorem, also involves a large number of technical details (carried out in Section
4.4
). It allows us to identify each subspace
W_{(J,A)}
with the tangent space to the first quotient space
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
at the point
(J,A)
.

Theorem F
.

For every element
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, the vector space
W_{(J,A)}
is contained inside
T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
and it is invariant by the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
. Moreover, the collection
\{W_{(J,A)}\}_{(J,A)}
defines a
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-invariant distribution on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
and the natural projection
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\to\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
induces a linear isomorphism

\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}\pi:W_{(J,A)}\longrightarrow T_{[J,A]}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)

In particular, we can restrict the pseudo-Kähler structure
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
from the ambient space to the finite dimensional manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
. Since the pseudo-metric
\mathbf{g}_{f}
is not positive-definite, it is not immediate that it is still non-degenerate when restricted to the subspaces
W_{(J,A)}
. This will be the biggest issue to be addressed and will be discussed in the last paragraph of this section. At this point, one can proceed by performing the finite-dimensional quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)/H
, where
H:=\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cong H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
. Such a quotient is isomorphic to
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
(see Section
6.1
), hence to the Hitchin component. In particular, there is a
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal decomposition
W_{(J,A)}=V_{(J,A)}\oplus S_{(J,A)}
, where
V_{(J,A)}
is the tangent to
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
and
S_{(J,A)}
is a copy of
H
(Lemma
6.2
).

Theorem G
.

The
H
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is free and proper, with complex and symplectic
H
-orbits. Moreover, the pseudo-Kähler structure
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
descend to the quotient which is identified with
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
. Finally, the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
induced on the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component coincides with the one found by Labourie and Loftin.

The relation with moment maps and symplectic reduction

While Theorem
E
and Theorem
F
can be proven with self-contained arguments, it is not clear how to obtain the differential equations (
1.2
) defining the subspace
W_{(J,A)}
. In fact, their origin must be sought in the context of moment maps and symplectic reductions, but in an infinite-dimensional context. For this reason, we will briefly explain how to characterize the subspaces
W_{(J,A)}
in these terms and how the presence of isotropic vectors for
\mathbf{g}_{f}
generates further difficulties.

In a previous paper, where we studied the same problem on the torus, we showed that the action of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is Hamiltonian with respect to the symplectic form
\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}
and we computed explicitly the moment map
\widehat{\mu}:D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\to\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})^{*}
(
[
RT24
]
). A general theorem of Donaldson (see Section
5.1
), allows us to promote the previous result to a Hamiltonian action of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, with respect to the symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
. In this case, the moment map
\boldsymbol{\mu}
associates to each pair
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
an element in the dual Lie algebra of Hamiltonian vector fields on the surface. It turns out that to obtain an honest moment map
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for the action of the group of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms, one has to add a scalar multiple of the area form
\rho
. At this point, it can be shown that the submanifold
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1}(0)
intersected with the set
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}=\{(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\ |\ d^{\nabla}A=0\}
(see Section
2.5
and Section
5.2
) is equal to
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
. Inspired by classical symplectic reduction theory, one is tempted to induce the pseudo-Riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}_{f}
and the symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
on the quotient
\big(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1}(0)\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}\big)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
. The issue is that, in our case, the tangent space
T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
is not a Hilbert space, and there is no canonical way to identify the
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to the
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-orbit inside
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
with the
\mathbf{I}
-invariant distribution tangent to the finite dimensional manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
. Despite that, by imitating the reduction in the positive-definite case, we are able to give a characterization of the subspace
W_{(J,A)}
as follows:

Theorem H
.

For any
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, the vector space
W_{(J,A)}
is the largest subspace in
T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
that is:

\bullet

invariant under the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
;

\bullet

\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to the orbit
T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)
.

The proof of this theorem will be developed in Section
5.3
and it is independent of the other results. On the other hand, it serves as a motivation for defining the subspace
W_{(J,A)}
as the solution of a system of partial differential equations (
1.2
).

Regarding the non-degeneracy of the pseudo-metric.

Theorem
F
together with Theorem
G
allow us to induce the pseudo-Kähler structure
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
from the infinite-dimensional manifold
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
to the Hitchin component, but, a-priori, it may be degenerate. We will show in the proof of Theorem
A
(Section
4.2
) that, at least on the Fuchsian locus, there are no non-zero degenerate vectors. As for the tangent directions to points away from
\mathcal{F}(\Sigma)
, the analysis becomes very complicated. On the one hand, we know the exact expression of
\mathbf{g}_{f}
, but on the other hand, the model
V_{(J,A)}
of the tangent space to the Hitchin component is described by very complicated PDEs (Remark
4.9
), whose solution is far from being explicit. The idea, is to look for a subspace of
T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
(possibly of infinite dimension) whose elements have a treatable description for our purpose. This is the tangent space
T_{(J,A)}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
to the set of pairs
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
satisfying the Codazzi-like equation
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0
for hyperbolic affine spheres (see (
HS
)). We will show that the set
T_{(J,A)}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
contains the tangent space to the
\Diff(\Sigma)
-orbit, which in turn splits as a direct sum of three subspaces (Lemma
3.1
). Then, using the relation between the PDEs describing
V_{(J,A)}
and the theory of symplectic reduction, the following
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal decomposition of
T_{(J,A)}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
can be obtained:

V_{(J,A)}\overset{\perp_{\mathbf{g}_{f}}}{\oplus}S_{(J,A)}\overset{\perp_{\mathbf{g}_{f}}}{\oplus}T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\overset{\perp_{\mathbf{g}_{f}}}{\oplus}\mathbf{I}\Big(T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\Big)\ .

The existence of the moment map
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
for the action of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
and an explicit calculation allow us to conclude that
\mathbf{g}_{f}
restricted to the Hamiltonian orbit is non-degenerate. Moreover, using a highly non-trivial integration by parts (Proposition
5.12
) we prove that
\mathbf{g}_{f}
is non-degenerate even when restricted to the subspace
S_{(J,A)}
. Finally, using the relation
\mathbf{g}_{f}(\mathbf{I}\cdot,\mathbf{I}\cdot)=\mathbf{g}_{f}(\cdot,\cdot)
one gets the following equivalence:
\mathbf{g}_{f}
is non-degenerate on
T_{(J,A)}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
if and only if it is non-degenerate on
V_{(J,A)}
(Corollary
6.6
).

The relation with Goldman symplectic form

In
[
Gol90a
]
, Goldman gave a gauge theoretic formula for his symplectic form on the
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-character variety: by identifying a Hitchin representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
with the holonomy of a flat connection
\nabla
, which is nothing but the Blaschke connection of the
\rho
-equivariant affine sphere, the pairing

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\dot{\nabla},\dot{\nabla}^{\prime})=\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\dot{\nabla} \wedge\dot{\nabla}')-\frac{1}{3}\tr(\dot{\nabla})\wedge\tr(\dot{\nabla}')

defines a non-degenerate symplectic form on the Hitchin component. The techniques we develop in Section
5.2
will allow us to compute explicitly the variations
\dot{\nabla}
in terms of variations
\dot{J}
and
\dot{A}
of the embedding data of the corresponding affine spheres, at least at points on the Fuchsian locus, in
all
directions, tangent or transverse to the Fuchsian locus. By direct inspection, we show that
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\mathbf{I}\cdot,\cdot)
coincides with our pseudo-Kähler metric
\mathbf{g}
at the Fuchsian locus in all directions, thus showing that the pair
(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G},\mathbf{I})
cannot induce a positive definite Kähler metric on the Hitchin component.

1.2.
Comparison with the anti-de Sitter case

As mentioned earlier, part of the techniques we use to construct the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component as a symplectic quotient and the definition of the pseudo-Kähler metric are based on a previous work (
[
MST21
]
), where the authors defined a para-hyperkähler structure on the deformation space of maximal globally hyperbolic anti-de Sitter
3
-manifolds, denoted with
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{GH}(\Sigma)
. Such a deformation space can be identified with a maximal component in the
\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
character variety, which consists entirely of discrete and faithful representations. In particular, such a space is parameterized by two copies of Teichmüller space (
[
Mes07
]
,
[
KS07
]
) and it is isomorphic to the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
(
[
KS07
]
).

As can be seen, the first major difference lies in the fact that
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
cannot be isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)\times\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
, since its real dimension is equal to
16g-16
. This does not allow, unlike the anti-de Sitter case, to define a natural para-complex structure
\mathbf{J}
that together with
\mathbf{I}
gives rise to another para-complex structure
\mathbf{K}:=\mathbf{I}\mathbf{J}
. Moreover, the parameterization
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{GH}(\Sigma)\cong T^{*}\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
as a holomorphic vector bundle, gives rise to a complex symplectic structure on
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{GH}(\Sigma)
, which is missing for the Hitchin component. This is the reason why with our construction we only obtain a pseudo-Kähler metric.

The different descriptions as holomorphic vector bundles over
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
lead to different computations along the way. In fact, in the
\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
setting one has to work with a pair given by a complex structure and a holomorphic quadratic differential on
\Sigma
, the real part of which corresponds to the second fundamental form of the immersion as a maximal surface in AdS
3
-manifolds, namely it is an endomorphism of
T\Sigma
. In our case, the real part of a holomorphic cubic differential is, up to the contraction with the metric, an End
(T\Sigma)
-valued
1
-form. On the one hand, the additional
1
-form part makes the analysis more difficult, but on the other we still succeed in obtaining similar results in regard to some key steps in the construction (Proposition
5.10
and Proposition
5.12
).

The presence of other two moment maps in the AdS seeting (
[
MST21
, Theorem 6.5]
), allowed the authors to obtain
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{GH}(\Sigma)
as the quotient of an infinite-dimensional space by the group of all symplectomorphisms of the surface isotopic to the identity. In our case, not knowing whether the equation
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0
can be interpreted as a moment map, we had to resort to the use of two quotients, which led to further difficulties developed in Section
6.1
. It is also worth mentioning that since the PDEs defining the distribution tangent to the deformation space are much more complicated in our setting, it was necessary to employ a deep analysis of the associated differential operators (Section
4.3
).

Finally, the most relevant part: the pseudo-metric is non-degenerate on the deformation space. In
[
MST21
]
, the authors were able to identify the three symplectic forms they defined on
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{GH}(\Sigma)
with already known symplectic forms (thus non-degenerate), in terms of the various parameterizations given above. In our setting, we know that, at least on the Fuchsian locus, our symplectic form coincides with Goldman’s one both on horizontal and vertical directions. Away from the Fuchsian locus, the relation is still unknown but we nonetheless gain some new information about Goldman’s symplectic form, as explained in the introduction.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
The
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component

We briefly introduce the Hitchin component for the group
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
and we explain the topological type of the associated character variety. For a more detailed and general discussion on Hitchin components and character varieties see
[
Gol84
]
,
[
Hit92
]
,
[
Fra06
]
,
[
FG06
]
and
[
Col+19
]
for a survey.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed, connected smooth and oriented surface of genus
g\geq 2
and consider the space Hom
(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\SL(3,\mathbb{R}))
of all representations from
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
to
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
. This set has a topology induced by the inclusion

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),

\displaystyle\ \SL(3,\mathbb{R}))\hookrightarrow\SL(3,\mathbb{R})^{2g}

\displaystyle\rho\longmapsto\big(\rho(a_{1}),\dots,\rho(b_{g})\big)

where
a_{1},\dots,b_{g}
are generators of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
subject to the relation
\prod_{i=1}^{g}\big[a_{i},b_{i}\big]=1
. There is a natural action of
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
on this space given by conjugation: for
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
and
P\in\SL(3,\mathbb{R})

(2.1)

(P\cdot\rho)(\gamma):=P^{-1}\rho(\gamma)P\ .

In order to get a Hausdorff quotient space, one needs to restrict to the
completely reducible
representations, i.e. those
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
which split as a direct sum of irreducible representations. If we denote with Hom
{}^{+}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\SL(3,\mathbb{R}))
the space of such representations, the quotient space

\mathfrak{R}\big(\Sigma,\SL(3,\mathbb{R})\big):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathrm{Hom}^{+}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\SL(3,\mathbb{R}))$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\SL(3,\mathbb{R})$}\right.}

is called the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-
character variety
.

Theorem 2.1

(Hitchin
[
Hit92
]
)
.

The topological space
\mathfrak{R}\big(\Sigma,\SL(3,\mathbb{R})\big)
has three connected components: the one containing the class of the trivial representation, the one consisting of representations whose associated flat
\mathbb{R}^{3}
-bundles have non-zero second Stiefel-Whitney class and the one consisting of representations connected to those arising as uniformization. Moreover, the third one is contained in the smooth locus of
\mathfrak{R}\big(\Sigma,\SL(3,\mathbb{R})\big)
and it is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{-8\chi(\Sigma)}
.

The most interesting component in the above list is the last one, which will be denoted by Hit
{}_{3}(\Sigma)
throughout the discussion. It can also be defined as the connected component of
\mathfrak{R}\big(\Sigma,\SL(3,\mathbb{R})\big)
containing the
Fuchsian representation

\tau\circ j
, where
j:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is discrete and faithful and
\tau
is the unique (up to conjugation) irreducible representation from
\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
to
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
. In particular, the composition
\tau\circ j
induces an inclusion of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{rep}}(\Sigma)
in
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
, whose image is called the
Fuchsian locus
and it will be denoted by
\mathcal{F}(\Sigma)
.

There is a geometric interpretation for representations in
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
, found by Choi and Goldman, which we now recall. A
(properly) convex

\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-
structure
on
\Sigma
is a pair
(\phi,M)
, where
\phi:\Sigma\to M
is a diffeomorphism (called the
marking
) and
M\cong\Omega/_{\Gamma}
is a (properly) convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-surface, namely
\Omega
is a (bounded) convex subset of
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
preserved by a properly discontinuous action of a discrete subgroup
\Gamma<\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
.
One can define an equivalence relation on such pairs:
(\phi_{1},M_{1})\sim(\phi_{2},M_{2})
if and only if there exists an element
\Psi\in\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)
such that the map
\phi_{2}\circ\Psi\circ\phi_{1}^{-1}:M_{1}\to M_{2}
is a projective isomorphism. The
deformation space of (properly) convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures
is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\Sigma):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\{(f,M)\ \text{convex}\ \mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}-\text{structure on}\ \Sigma\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)$}\right.}

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{0}(\Sigma):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\{(f,M)\ \text{properly convex}\ \mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}-\text{structure on}\ \Sigma\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)$}\right.}\ .

When
g\geq 2
these two spaces coincide and therefore every convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structure is actually properly convex (
[
Kui53
]
,
[
Ben60
]
). To any equivalence class of convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures on
\Sigma
there is an associated class of representations
[\rho]
, with
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
. In particular, this association defines the so-called
monodromy map

\mathfrak{hol}:\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)\to\mathfrak{R}\big(\Sigma,\SL(3,\mathbb{R})\big)
whose image is contained in the space of discrete and faithful representations.

Theorem 2.2

(
[
Gol90
]
,
[
CG93
]
)
.

The map
\mathfrak{hol}:\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)\to\mathfrak{R}\big(\Sigma,\SL(3,\mathbb{R})\big)
induces an isomorphism between
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
and
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
. In particular, any deformation of a Fuchsian representation
\tau\circ j:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
can be realized as the holonomy of a convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structure on
\Sigma
.

2.2.
Hyperbolic affine spheres

Here we briefly introduce the theory of hyperbolic affine spheres in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see
[
LM16
]
,
[
NS94
]
) and their relation with convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed surface of genus
g\geq 2
with universal cover
\widetilde{\Sigma}
and let
f\!:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an immersion with
\tilde{\xi}\!:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
a transverse vector field to
f(\widetilde{\Sigma})
. This means that for all
x\in\widetilde{\Sigma}
we have a splitting:

T_{f(x)}\mathbb{R}^{3}=f_{*}T_{x}\widetilde{\Sigma}+\mathbb{R}\tilde{\xi}_{x}\ .

Let
D
be the standard flat connection on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and suppose the structure equations of the immersed surface are given by:

(2.2)

\displaystyle D_{X}Y

\displaystyle=\nabla_{X}Y+h(X,Y)\xi

\displaystyle D_{X}\xi

\displaystyle=-S(X)

where
\nabla
is a torsion-free connection on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
called the
Blaschke connection
,
\xi
is the
affine normal
of the immersion (see
[
Lof01
, §3.1]
for example),
h
is a metric on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
called the
Blaschke metric
and
S
is an endomorphism of
T\widetilde{\Sigma}
called the
affine shape operator
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
N
be an immersed hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with structure equations given by (
2.2
). Then
N
is called a
hyperbolic affine sphere
if
S=-\mathrm{Id}_{TN}
.

The properties of the global geometry of hyperbolic affine spheres were conjectured by Calabi (
[
Cal72
]
) and proved by Cheng-Yau (
[
CY77
]
,
[
CY86
]
) and Calabi-Nirenberg (with clarifications by Gigena (
[
Gig81
]
) and Li (
[
Li90
]
,
[
Li92
]
)). The most important result (stated only in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
but true in arbitrary
\mathbb{R}^{n}
) is the following:

Theorem 2.4

(Cheng-Yau-Calabi-Nirenberg)
.

Given a constant
\lambda<0
and a convex, bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there is a unique properly embedded hyperbolic affine sphere
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
with affine shape operator
S=\lambda\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{TN}
and center
0
asymptotic to the boundary of the cone
\mathcal{C}(\Omega):=\{(tx,t)\ |\ x\in\Omega,t>0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. For any immersed hyperbolic affine sphere
f:N\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, properness of the immersion is equivalent to the completeness of the Blaschke metric, and any such
N
is a properly embedded hypersurface asymptotic to the boundary of the cone given by the convex hull of
N
and its center.

We can use the above theorem to describe a
\Diff(\Sigma)
-equivariant one-to-one correspondence between convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures and hyperbolic affine spheres. In fact, given a convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structure
\phi:\Sigma\to M\cong\Omega/\Gamma
, where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is bounded, there exists a unique hyperbolic affine sphere
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
asymptotic to the boundary of the cone
\mathcal{C}(\Omega)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
(Theorem
2.4
). Such a hyperbolic affine sphere
\mathcal{H}
is invariant under automorphisms of
\mathcal{C}(\Omega)
, seen as a subgroup of
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
. The restriction of the projection
\pi:\mathcal{C}(\Omega)\to\Omega
induces a diffeomorphism of
\mathcal{H}
onto
\Omega
. By equivariance, the tensor
h
and the connection
\nabla
descend to the quotient
\Omega/\Gamma\cong M
. Viceversa, given an embedding of the universal cover
\widetilde{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
as a
\widetilde{\Gamma}
-equivariant hyperbolic affine sphere, with
\widetilde{\Gamma}\cong\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, one gets an identification of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
with a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, via the developing map. Then, Theorem
2.4
implies that
\widetilde{\Sigma}
is asymptotic to a cone over
\Omega
. The action of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
on
\widetilde{\Sigma}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
corresponds to an action of a group
\Gamma<\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
, isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
, on the domain
\Omega
so that
\Sigma\cong\Omega/\Gamma
.

Let
f:\!(\widetilde{\Sigma},h,\nabla)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an immersed hyperbolic affine sphere, where
h
is the Blaschke metric and
\nabla
is the Blaschke connection. If
\nabla^{h}
denotes the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
h
, then
\nabla=\nabla^{h}+A
, where
A
is a section of
T^{*}(\Sigma)\otimes\End(T\Sigma)
called the
Pick form
. In particular, for every
X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
the quantity
A(X)
is an endomorphism of
T\Sigma
.

Definition 2.5
.

The
Pick tensor
is the
(0,3)
-tensor defined by

(2.3)

C(X,Y,Z):=h(A(X)Y,Z),\quad\forall X,Y,Z\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)\ .

Corollary 2.6
.

If
f:\!(\widetilde{\Sigma},h,\nabla)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is an immersed hyperbolic affine sphere, then the Pick tensor is totally symmetric, namely in index notation
C_{ijk}
we have

C_{ijk}=C_{\sigma(ijk)},\quad\forall\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}\ .

In particular, this is equivalent to the requirement that the endomorphism
A(X)
is
h
-symmetric for all
X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
and

(2.4)

A(X)Y=A(Y)X,\quad\forall X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)\ .

Theorem 2.7

(
[
BH13
, Lemma 4.8]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed oriented surface of genus
g\geq 1
. Let
h
be a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
and
J
be the induced (almost) complex structure. Suppose that a
(1,2)
tensor
A
and a
(0,3)
tensor
C
are related by
A=h^{-1}C
. Assume further that the tensor
C
is totally symmetric. Then,
A(X)
is trace-free for all
X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
if and only if
C
is the real part of a complex cubic differential, which can be expressed as
q=C(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)-iC(J\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
. If this holds, then the following are equivalent:

\bullet\ \mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{h}}A=0
;

\bullet\ C
is the real part of a holomorphic cubic differential
q=C(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)-iC(J\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
;

\bullet\ (\nabla^{h}_{JX}A)(\cdot)=(\nabla^{h}_{X}A)(J\cdot),\forall X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
.

The embedding data of hyperbolic affine spheres in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
can be described in terms of the Blaschke metric
h
and the Pick form
A
satisfying the following equations:

(HS)

\begin{cases}K_{h}-||q||_{h}^{2}=-1\\
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{h}}A=0\ ,\end{cases}

where
q=C(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)-iC(J\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
is the holomorphic cubic differential determined by the Pick tensor
C
,
K_{h}
is the Gaussian curvature of the Blaschke metric
h
and
A=h^{-1}C
is the associated End
{}_{0}(T\Sigma,h)
-valued 1-form. Moreover, for any tangent vector fields
X,Y,Z
on
\Sigma
the exterior-derivative
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{h}}A
is the
\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,h)
-valued 2-form

(2.5)

\big(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla^{h}}A\big)(X,Y)Z=\big(\nabla^{h}_{X}A\big)(Y)Z-\big(\nabla^{h}_{Y}A\big)(X)Z\ ,

where End
{}_{0}(T\Sigma,h)
denotes the vector bundle of
h
-symmetric and trace-less endomorphisms of the tangent bundle.

Remark 2.8
.

Notice that the second equation in (
HS
) is invariant under conformal change of metric. In fact, it is equivalent to require that the cubic differential
q=C(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)-iC(J\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
is holomorphic with respect to the complex structure defined by the conformal class of
h
. For this reason, in the following discussion, we will use either the tensor
A
or
C
according to which is more convenient.

Viceversa, every pair
(h,C)
satisfying (
HS
), with
h
a Riemannian metric and
C
a totally symmetric
(0,3)
-tensor equal to the real part of a
h
-cubic differential, i.e. a cubic differential that is holomorphic for the conformal class of
h
, represents the embedding data of a hyperbolic affine sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(
[
Wan90
]
,
[
Lof01
]
,
[
BH13
]
). Considering that such a correspondence is natural by the action of
\Diff(\Sigma)
, we introduce the space parametrizing the embedding data of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
-equivariant hyperbolic affine spheres in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as:

(2.6)

\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left\{(h,C)\ \Bigg|\ \parbox{190.00029pt}{$h$ is a Riemannian metric \\
$C$ is the real part of a $h$-cubic differential \\
equations (\ref{Gausscodazzi}) are satisfied}\right\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)$}\right.}

Thus, according to the above discussion, we obtain the following result:

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed surface of genus
g\geq 2
, then there exists a MCG
(\Sigma)
-invariant homeomorphism between
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
, given by the embedding data of the unique equivariant hyperbolic affine sphere.

Because of this identification, for the rest of the discussion we will equivalently use one of the two pieces of notation in Proposition
2.9
to denote the deformation space of convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures, hence the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component.

2.3.
Wang’s equation

Here we discuss the relation between the hyperbolic affine sphere immersion
f:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the conformal geometry of the surface. In particular, it is possible to rewrite the structure equations (
2.2
) in terms of a local holomorphic coordinate on the surface. We also see, how these equations can be solved whenever a certain integrability condition is satisfied (see
[
LM16
]
and
[
Wan90
]
).

Since we are interested in equivariant hyperbolic affine spheres, we can pick a parametrization
f\!:\Delta\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
\Delta
is a simply-connected domain in
\mathbb{C}
biholomorphic to the open unit disk. Let
z=x+iy
be a local conformal coordinate with respect to the Blaschke metric
h
, so that
h=e^{\psi}|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}
, where
|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}
is defined as the symmetric product between
\mathrm{d}z
and
\mathrm{d}\bar{z}
. After some algebraic manipulations (see
[
LM16
, §2.1.5]
and
[
RT24
, §2.2]
), the structure equations (
2.2
) with
S=-\mathrm{Id}_{T\Sigma}
can be rewritten as a system of ODEs

(2.7)

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\mathbf{F}=A\cdot\mathbf{F}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}\mathbf{F}=B\cdot\mathbf{F}\ ,\end{cases}

where
A
an
B
are
3\times 3
matrices completely determined by the Blaschke metric and the Pick tensor (see Definition
2.5
), and
\mathbf{F}
is the frame field of the hyperbolic affine sphere.

Proposition 2.10

(
[
Wan90
]
)
.

Given an initial condition for
\boldsymbol{F}
at
z_{0}\in\Delta
, there exists a unique solution to the system (
2.7
) as long as the following two equations are satisfied:

(2.8)

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\bar{z}}\psi+\frac{1}{2}|Q|^{2}e^{-2\psi}-\frac{1}{2}e^{\psi}=0\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}Q=0\ ,\end{cases}

where
Q
is as smooth function on
\Delta
completely determined by the Pick tensor of the immersion. In particular, if equations (
2.8
) hold, the expression
q=Q\mathrm{d}z^{3}
gives rise to a holomorphic cubic differential on
\Delta
.

Remark 2.11
.

Attention must be given to the factor
\frac{1}{2}
that has been placed in front of the term
|Q|^{2}e^{-2\psi}
in (
2.8
) and that does not appear in
[
RT24
, Theorem 2.10]
. In fact, as we shall see shortly, through this rescaling of the cubic differential we will be able to rewrite the first equation of the above system in terms of a vortex-type equation defined on the surface.

Now let
(\Sigma,J)
be a closed Riemann surface with genus
g\geq 2
. By the well-known Poincaré-Koebe Uniformization Theorem we can pick a Riemannian metric
g_{0}
of constant curvature
k_{0}
on
\Sigma
which is compatible with the initial complex structure
J
. Let
H^{0}(\Sigma,K^{3})
be the holomorphic sections of the tri-canonical bundle over
(\Sigma,J)
, namely the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space of holomorphic cubic differentials. It is easy to see, using the Riemann-Roch Theorem, that this space has complex dimension equal to
5g-5
. If
z=x+iy
is a local holomorphic coordinate on
(\Sigma,J)
, then we can define a norm on
H^{0}(\Sigma,K^{3})
, given by:

||q||_{g_{0}}^{2}:=|Q|^{2}e^{-3\phi}\ ,

where
q=Q\mathrm{d}z^{3}
and
g_{0}=e^{\phi}|\mathrm{d}z|^{2}
in this local coordinate.

Theorem 2.12

(
[
Wan90
]
)
.

Pick the metric
g_{0}
so that its Gaussian curvature is equal to
-1
. Let
h=e^{u}g_{0}
be a Riemannian metric in the same conformal class as
g_{0}
and
q\in H^{0}(\Sigma,K^{3})
, then
\psi=u+\phi
satisfies the first equation of (
2.8
) if and only if the metric
h
satisfies:

(2.9)

K_{h}-||q||^{2}_{h}=-1\ ,

where
K_{h}
is the Gaussian curvature of
h
and
||q||^{2}_{h}=||q||_{g_{0}}^{2}e^{-3u}
.

Lemma 2.13
.

In the setting of the previous theorem, the metric
h
satisfies equation (
2.9
) if and only if the function
u:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the following semi-linear elliptic equation

(2.10)

\Delta_{g_{0}}u+2||q||^{2}_{g_{0}}e^{-2u}-2e^{u}+2=0

Proof.

This is an easy application of the formula for the curvature
K_{h}=e^{-u}(k_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g_{0}}u)
under conformal change of metric
h=e^{u}g_{0}
. In fact, since
g_{0}
can be chosen so that
k_{0}=-1
, we get

K_{h}-||q||_{h}^{2}+1=-e^{-u}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-u}\Delta_{g_{0}}u-e^{-3u}||q||_{g_{0}}^{2}+1\ .

Multiplying the right-hand side of the equation above by the factor
-2e^{u}
, we have the following equivalence

-e^{-u}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-u}\Delta_{g_{0}}u-e^{-3u}||q||_{g_{0}}^{2}+1=0\iff\Delta_{g_{0}}u+2||q||^{2}_{g_{0}}e^{-2u}-2e^{u}+2=0\ .

∎

The original approach used by Wang to study existence and uniqueness of the solution to (
2.10
) (and thus to (
2.9
)) was the theory of elliptic operators between Sobolev spaces (
[
Wan90
, §4]
). About ten years later, Loftin simplified a lot the original argument, deducing the following stronger result:

Lemma 2.14

(
[
Lof01
]
)
.

Let
(M,\tilde{g})
be a smooth compact Riemannian manifold and let
\tilde{\varphi}
be a smooth non-negative function on
M
. Then, the equation

(2.11)

\Delta_{\tilde{g}}u+\tilde{\varphi}(p)e^{-2u}-2e^{u}+2=0

has a unique smooth solution.

In the end, combining all the results together we get the following important consequence:

Corollary 2.15

(
[
Wan90
,
Lof01
]
)
.

A hyperbolic affine sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with center
0
which admits a properly discontinuous action of a discrete group
\Gamma<\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
, so that the quotient is a closed oriented surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 2
, is completely determined by a conformal structure on
\Sigma
and a section
q\in H^{0}(\Sigma,K^{3})
. Moreover, all such hyperbolic affine spheres are determined in this way.

2.4.
Labourie and Loftin’s parametrization

The core of this section is to use the results presented in Sections
2.2
and Section
2.3
to describe the parameterization, found by Labourie and Loftin, of the space of convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures on
\Sigma
. It should be emphasized that the same result found in
[
Lof01
]
and
[
Lab07
]
was proven using slightly different techniques, and in our case, we will follow Loftin’s approach more.

Let
\pi:Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)\big)\to\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)
be the holomorphic vector bundle of cubic differentials over Teichmüller space. The fibre over an equivalence class
[J]\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)
is the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space of holomorphic sections of the tri-canonical bundle. Given any pair
([J],q)\in Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)\big)
, we have an embedding
\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
as a (equivariant) hyperbolic affine sphere whose Pick tensor and Blaschke metric are completely determined by
[J]
and
q
(Corollary
2.15
). In particular, by the argument in Section
2.2
, we get a family of convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures on
\Sigma
in the same
\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)
-orbit. Conversely, if we start with an equivalence class of convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures, by Theorem
2.4
we get an (equivariant) embedding
\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
as a hyperbolic affine sphere, which is equivalent to a pair
([J],q)
as above. In the end, the main result is the following:

Theorem 2.16

(
[
Lof01
]
,
[
Lab07
]
)
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)\to Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)\big)
be the map which associates to each equivalence class of convex
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{2}
-structures the pair
([J],q)
described above. Then,
\Phi
is an homeomorphism.

There is a pull-back action of MCG
(\Sigma)
on
Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)\big)
given by:

[\psi]\cdot([J],q):=([\psi^{*}J],\psi^{*}q)\ .

It is well defined as it does not depend on the chosen representative in
[\psi]\in\textrm{MCG}(\Sigma)
. Moreover, the pair
([\psi^{*}J],\psi^{*}q)
still defines a point in
Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)\big)
as
\psi^{*}q
is holomorphic with respect to
\psi^{*}J
if and only if
q
is
J
-holomorphic. Thanks to Theorem
2.2
, taking into account the action of MCG
(\Sigma)
on
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
and
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
, one gets the following remarkable consequence:

Corollary 2.17

(
[
Lof01
]
,
[
Lab07
]
)
.

The space
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
carries a mapping class group invariant complex structure, denoted with
\mathbf{I}
.

2.5.
A conformal change of metric

The next step now is to introduce a conformal change of metrics on the surface that allows us to find an equivalent description of
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
which will be crucial for the symplectic reduction. In order to do this, we need to fix an area form
\rho
on the surface. Then, using the so-called Moser’s trick in symplectic geometry we will obtain a different model of
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
as the quotient of an infinite dimensional space by
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
.

First, we introduce the function that will allow us to make the conformal change of metric.

Lemma 2.18
.

There exists a unique smooth function
F:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(2.12)

ce^{-F(t)}-2te^{-3F(t)}+1=0,\qquad F(0)=\log\absolutevalue{c},

where
c:=\frac{2\pi\chi(\Sigma)}{\mathrm{Vol}(\Sigma,\rho)}
is a strictly negative constant depending only on the topology and the area of the surface. Moreover, if the function
f:[0,+\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
is defined as

(2.13)

f(t):=-\Bigg(\int_{0}^{t}F^{\prime}(s)s^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathrm{d}s\Bigg)t^{\frac{1}{3}}

then it is smooth and it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

f(0)=0
;

(2)

f^{\prime}(t)<0
for all
t>0
;

(3)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}f(t)=-\infty\ .

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of the smooth function
F
follows from a standard application of the implicit function theorem to
G(t,y):=ce^{-y}-2te^{-3y}+1
. In particular,
G\big(0,F(0)\big)=0
implies that
F(0)=\log|c|
. Using the formulas for the derivative of the function
y=F(t)
in terms of the derivatives of
G(t,y)
, we obtain that:

F^{\prime}(t)>0\ \forall t\geq 0,\qquad F^{\prime\prime}(t)<0\ \forall t\geq 0,\qquad\lim_{t\to+\infty}F(t)=+\infty\ .

From the definition it is clear that
f
is smooth,
f(0)=0
and it only attains non-positive values. The fundamental theorem of calculus implies that

f^{\prime}(t)=-\Big(F^{\prime}(t)+\frac{t^{-\frac{2}{3}}}{3}\int_{0}^{t}F^{\prime}(s)s^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathrm{d}s\Big)\ ,

hence
f^{\prime}(t)<0
for all
t>0
. The behavior of
f
at infinity can be obtained by using the explicit expression

F(t)=\ln\left(\frac{(4t)^{\frac{1}{3}}}{\sqrt[3]{1+\sqrt{1+\frac{\zeta}{t}}}+\sqrt[3]{1-\sqrt{1+\frac{\zeta}{t}}}}\right)\ ,t\neq 0

that can be derived from the functional equation in the statement, which is a cubic equation in
e^{-F(t)}
, and where
\zeta=\frac{2}{27}|c|
.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
f:[0,+\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
and
F:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be the functions defined above. Then,

(1)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(t)=-F^{\prime}(t)+\frac{f(t)}{3t},
for all
t>0
;

(2)

1-f(t)+3tf^{\prime}(t)>0,
for all
t\geq 0
;

(3)

f^{\prime}(t)
is monotonically increasing for any
t>0
.

Proof.

The first identity can be obtained by computing the derivative of the function
f(t)
. In fact,

(2.14)

f^{\prime}(t)=-\Big(F^{\prime}(t)+\frac{t^{-\frac{2}{3}}}{3}\int_{0}^{t}F^{\prime}(s)s^{-\frac{1}{3}}\mathrm{d}s\Big)=-F^{\prime}(t)+\frac{f(t)}{3t},\quad\forall t>0

Regarding the second identity, we need to use the explicit expression of
F(t)
found in the proof of Lemma
2.18
. Hence, for any
t>0
, we have

F(t)=\ln\bigg(\frac{(4t)^{\frac{1}{3}}}{g(t)}\bigg),\qquad g(t):=\sqrt[3]{1+\sqrt{1+\frac{\zeta}{t}}}+\sqrt[3]{1-\sqrt{1+\frac{\zeta}{t}}},\quad\zeta=\frac{2}{27}|c|\ .

This implies,

F^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{3t}-\frac{g^{\prime}(t)}{g(t)}\ .

In the end, combining
(1)
with the explicit expression for
F^{\prime}(t)
, we get

\displaystyle 1-f(t)+3tf^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=1-f(t)+3t\Big(-F^{\prime}(t)+\frac{f(t)}{3t}\Big)

\displaystyle=1-f(t)+3t\Big(-\frac{1}{3t}+\frac{g^{\prime}(t)}{g(t)}+\frac{f(t)}{3t}\Big)

\displaystyle=3t\frac{g^{\prime}(t)}{g(t)}.

By using the classical theory of the study of a real function of one variable, we deduce that
g(t)
is strictly positive and monotonically increasing for every
t>0
. Moreover, at
t=0
, we have
1-f(0)+3\cdot 0\cdot f^{\prime}(0)=1>0
.

Using equation
(1)
in the statement, we have

(2.15)

f^{\prime\prime}(t)=-F^{\prime\prime}(t)+\frac{1}{3t^{2}}\big(f^{\prime}(t)t-f(t)\big)\ .

Using the definition of
f(t)
, the new function
G(t):=f^{\prime}(t)t^{\frac{2}{3}}-f(t)t^{-\frac{1}{3}}
is equal to zero when
t=0
and its derivative is given by

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\big(f^{\prime}(t)t^{\frac{2}{3}}-f(t)t^{-\frac{1}{3}}\big)^{\prime}

(Equation (
2.14
))

\displaystyle=\Big(-t^{\frac{2}{3}}F^{\prime}(t)-\frac{2}{3}f(t)t^{-\frac{1}{3}}\Big)^{\prime}

(Equation (
2.14
))

\displaystyle=-F^{\prime\prime}(t)t^{\frac{2}{3}}-\frac{2}{3}F^{\prime}(t)t^{-\frac{1}{3}}-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}(t)t^{-\frac{1}{3}}+\frac{2}{9}f(t)t^{-\frac{4}{3}}

\displaystyle=-F^{\prime\prime}(t)t^{\frac{2}{3}}>0,\quad\forall t>0\ .

This implies that
G(t)\geq 0
for any
t\geq 0
, hence, by using (
2.15
),
f^{\prime}(t)
is monotonically increasing for any
t>0
.
∎

Recall that, in general, given a (0,3)-tensor
C
and a Riemannian metric
g
on
\Sigma
, one can define
A:=g^{-1}C
to be the associated (1,2)-tensor, namely a
1
-form with values in End
(T\Sigma)
. Suppose also that
C
is totally symmetric, then according to Theorem
2.7
the tensor
C
is the real part of a cubic differential if and only if the endomorphism part of
A
is trace-less. Let us introduce the following space:

\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}_{0}(\Sigma):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left\{(g,C)\ \Bigg|\ \parbox{190.00029pt}{$g$ is a Riemannian metric on $\Sigma$ \\
$C$ is the real part of a $g$-cubic differential \\
$\big(h:=e^{F\big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\big)}g,A:=g^{-1}C\big)$ satisfy (\ref{Gausscodazzi})}\right\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)$}\right.}

Notice that the map sending the pair
(g,C)
to
(h,A)
, where
h=e^{F\big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\big)}g
and
A=g^{-1}C
, induces a MCG
(\Sigma)
-equivariant map from
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}_{0}(\Sigma)
to
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
. There exists an inverse map constructed by sending the pair
(h,A)
satisfying (
HS
) to the pair
(g,C)
where
g=e^{-F\big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\big)}h
and
C:=gA
. Since all the process is invariant by the action of
\Diff(\Sigma)
, we get the following:

Lemma 2.20
.

The correspondence described above induces a MCG
(\Sigma)
-equivariant isomorphism between
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}_{0}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
.

Let
\rho
be the area form fixed at the beginning of the discussion, then for any (almost) complex structure
J
the pairing
g_{J}:=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
defines a Riemannian metric on the surface. Let us introduce the space

\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left\{(J,C)\ \Bigg|\ \parbox{190.00029pt}{$J$ is an (almost) complex structure on $\Sigma$ \\
$C$ is the real part of a $J$-cubic differential \\
$\big(h:=e^{F\big(\frac{||q||^{2}_{g_{J}}}{2}\big)}g_{J},A:=g_{J}^{-1}C\big)$ satisfy (\ref{Gausscodazzi})}\right\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Symp_{0}(\Sigma)$}\right.}

Proposition 2.21
.

The map sending the pair
(J,C)
to
(h=e^{F\big(\frac{||q||_{g_{J}}^{2}}{2}\big)}g_{J},A=g^{-1}_{J}C)
induces a MCG
(\Sigma)
-equivariant homeomorphism between
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
and
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

The proof is based on the so-called Moser’s trick in symplectic geometry. Since this argument is standard in contexts similar to ours, we will only give an idea of how it is applied (for more details see
[
Hod05
, §3.2.3]
). Moser’s stability theorem (
[
MS17
, Theorem 3.2.4]
) claims that given a family of cohomologous symplectic forms
\omega_{t}
on a closed manifold, there exists a family of diffeomorphisms
\phi_{t}
such that
\phi_{0}=\text{Id}
and
\phi_{t}^{*}\omega_{t}=\omega_{0}
. For a closed surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 2
, given two area forms
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
of the same total area, one can apply Moser’s stability theorem to the family
\rho_{t}:=(1-t)\rho+t\rho^{\prime}
and deduce that there exists
\phi\in\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)
such that
\phi^{*}\rho^{\prime}=\rho
. In particular, for any
\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)
-equivalence class
[g,C]
in
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
, there exists a representative of the form
(g_{J},C)
. Finally, if one has a family of diffeomorphisms
\psi_{t}
with
\psi_{0}=\text{Id}
and
\psi_{1}^{*}\rho=\rho
, by applying Moser’s stability again to
\rho_{t}:=\psi_{t}^{*}\rho
one can deform
\psi_{t}
to a family of symplectomorphisms
\phi_{t}
such that
\phi_{0}=\text{Id}
and
\phi_{1}=\psi_{1}
. Combining it all together, it has been shown that

\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)=\Diff_{0}(\Sigma)\cap\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)\ .

∎

3.
The Weil-Petersson Kähler metric on Teichmüller space

In this section we briefly recall the definition of the group of (Hamiltonian) symplectomorphisms of a closed oriented surface of genus
g\geq 2
and their corresponding Lie algebras. Next, we briefly describe the construction of the Weil-Petersson Kähler metric on Teichmüller space using the theory of symplectic reduction, which inspires our construction for
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
.

3.1.
The Lie algebra of the group of (Hamiltonian) symplectomorphisms

Let
\rho
be a fixed area form on a closed surface
\Sigma
of genus
g\geq 2
. The group
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
is given by those diffeomorphisms
\phi
isotopic to the identity and such that
\phi^{*}\rho=\rho
. Thanks to Cartan’s magic formula:

\mathcal{L}_{X}\rho=\iota_{X}\mathrm{d}\rho+\mathrm{d}(\iota_{X}\rho)

and the fact that
\mathrm{d}\rho=0
, we obtain the following identification for the Lie algebra of
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
:

\mathfrak{S}(\Sigma,\rho)=\{X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)\ |\ \mathrm{d}(\iota_{X}\rho)=0\}\cong_{\rho}Z^{1}(\Sigma)\ ,

where the last isomorphism is given by the identification of
\Gamma(T\Sigma)
with the space of
1
-forms
\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)
, and
Z^{1}(\Sigma)
denotes the space of closed
1
-forms. A symplectomorphism
\phi
is called
Hamiltonian
if there is an isotopy
\phi_{\bullet}:[0,1]\to\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, with
\phi_{0}=\text{Id}
and
\phi_{1}=\phi
, and a smooth family of functions
H_{t}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\iota_{X_{t}}\rho=\mathrm{d}H_{t}
, where
X_{t}
is the infinitesimal generator of the symplectomorphism
\phi_{t}
. Let us denote by
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
the group of Hamiltonian symplectomorphisms, which is a normal subgroup of
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
(
[
MS17
, §3.1]
). The Lie algebra of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
can be characterized as:

\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)=\{X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)\ |\ \iota_{X}\rho\ \text{is exact}\}\cong_{\rho}B^{1}(\Sigma)\ ,

where
B^{1}(\Sigma)
is the space of exact
1
-forms on
\Sigma
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\rho
be a fixed area form and
J
be a complex structure on
\Sigma
, then any
X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
has a unique decomposition

(3.1)

X=V+W+JW^{\prime}\ ,

where
W,W^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)
and
\mathrm{d}(\iota_{V}\rho)=\mathrm{d}(\iota_{JV}\rho)=0

Proof.

Let
\rho
be a fixed area form on
\Sigma
and consider the induced isomorphism

\displaystyle\Gamma(T

\displaystyle\Sigma)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle X\mapsto\iota_{X}\rho\ .

For any (almost) complex structure
J
on
\Sigma
we get a Riemannian metric
g_{J}:=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
. Hodge theory for compact Riemannian surfaces implies the existence of a decomposition

\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)=\mathrm{d}\big(C^{\infty}(\Sigma)\big)\oplus\mathrm{d}^{*}\big(\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)\big)\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma)\ ,

where
\mathrm{d}^{*}
is the
L^{2}
-adjoint of the exterior differential and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma)=\{\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)\ |\ \mathrm{d}\alpha=\mathrm{d}^{*}\alpha=0\}
is the space of harmonic
1
-forms. In particular, for any
X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
we have a unique decomposition
\iota_{X}\rho=\mathrm{d}f+\mathrm{d}^{*}\omega+\alpha
, with
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma),\omega\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)
and
\alpha\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Sigma)
. Since each element of the decomposition is a
1
-form, there must exist three vector fields
V,W,\widetilde{W}
such that
\mathrm{d}f=\iota_{W}\rho,\mathrm{d}^{*}\omega=\iota_{\widetilde{W}}\rho
and
\alpha=\iota_{V}\rho
, which implies that
X=V+W+\widetilde{W}
. Now notice that
\iota_{W}\rho
is exact, hence
W\in\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)
. Since
\alpha
is harmonic we have
\mathrm{d}(\iota_{V}\rho)=\mathrm{d}^{*}(\iota_{V}\rho)=0
, but the term in between can be written as
\mathrm{d}(\iota_{V}\rho\circ J)
, which implies that
\mathrm{d}(\iota_{JV}\rho)=0
. Finally, in order to end the proof, we only need to show that
\widetilde{W}=JW^{\prime}
for some
W^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)
. This follows from the fact that
\iota_{\widetilde{W}}\rho=\mathrm{d}^{*}\omega=\ast_{g_{J}}\circ\mathrm{d}\circ\ast_{g_{J}}\omega=(\mathrm{d}\circ\ast_{g_{J}}\omega)\circ J
, where
\ast_{g_{J}}
denotes the Hodge-star operator with respect to
g_{J}
. Since
\mathrm{d}\circ\ast_{g_{J}}\omega
is an exact
1
-form, there exists a vector field
W^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)
such that
\iota_{W^{\prime}}\rho\circ J=\iota_{\widetilde{W}}\rho
, hence
\widetilde{W}=JW^{\prime}
. ∎

Because of the close connection with harmonic
1
-forms, the vector fields
V
on
(\Sigma,J)
for which
\mathrm{d}(\iota_{V}\rho)=\mathrm{d}(\iota_{JV}\rho)=0
will be called
harmonic
. The space of harmonic vector fields on
\Sigma
will be denoted with
\mathfrak{h}_{J}
and it is a Lie subalgebra of
\mathfrak{S}(\Sigma,\rho)
. Moreover, there is a splitting

(3.2)

\mathfrak{S}(\Sigma,\rho)=\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)\oplus\mathfrak{h}_{J}

as infinite-dimensional vector spaces. Let
\psi\in\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, then there exists a family of symplectomorphisms
\{\psi_{t}\}
with
\psi_{1}=\psi
and
\psi_{0}=\text{Id}
. Denote with
X_{t}
the vector field which generates the isotopy, namely
\partial_{t}\psi_{t}=X_{t}\circ\psi_{t}
. Then one has a well-defined map called the
Flux homomorphism

(3.3)

\displaystyle\text{Flux}:\Symp_{0}(

\displaystyle\Sigma,\rho)\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\{\psi_{t}\}\mapsto\int_{0}^{1}[\iota_{X_{t}}\rho]\mathrm{d}t

Lemma 3.2

(
[
MS17
]
)
.

The Flux homomorphism is surjective and it induces an isomorphism

(3.4)

{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)$}\right.}\cong H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})\ .

We end the discussion in this section by introducing two non-degenerate pairings:

(3.5)

\displaystyle\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{S}}:{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$B^{1}(\Sigma)$}\right.}

\displaystyle\times Z^{1}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle([\alpha],\beta)\ \ \longmapsto\int_{\Sigma}\alpha\wedge\beta

\displaystyle\langle\cdot|\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}:{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$Z^{1}(\Sigma)$}\right.}

\displaystyle\times B^{1}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle([\alpha],\beta)\ \ \longmapsto\int_{\Sigma}\alpha\wedge\beta\ .

Thanks to the identifications
Z^{1}(\Sigma)\cong_{\rho}\mathfrak{S}(\Sigma,\rho),B^{1}(\Sigma)\cong_{\rho}\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)
and the isomorphism
between
B^{2}(\Sigma)
and
\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)/Z^{1}(\Sigma)
induce by the differential
\mathrm{d}
, we get

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$B^{1}(\Sigma)$}\right.}\subset\mathfrak{S}(\Sigma,\rho)^{*},\quad B^{2}(\Sigma)\cong_{\mathrm{d}}{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$Z^{1}(\Sigma)$}\right.}\subset\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)^{*}\ .

Remark 3.3
.

Observe that, since the above pairings are defined on infinite dimensional vector spaces
\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{W}
, the notion of non-degeneracy we are referring to is the one that sometimes in the literature is called
weakly non-degenerate
, namely the induced map
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}^{*}
is injective.

Using the standard property of the contraction operator
\iota
with respect to the wedge product, for any vector field
V
and any
1
-form
\alpha
on
\Sigma
, one has

(3.6)

\iota_{V}\alpha\rho=\alpha\wedge\iota_{V}\rho\ .

Moreover, if
V
is Hamiltonian, namely
\iota_{V}\rho=\mathrm{d}H
for some smooth function
H
, we get

(3.7)

\langle\alpha,\mathrm{d}H\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{\Sigma}\alpha\wedge\mathrm{d}H=\int_{\Sigma}\alpha(V)\rho\ ,

where
[\alpha]\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)/Z^{1}(\Sigma)
.

3.2.
Teichmüller space as a symplectic reduction

Before starting the study of
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
, let us briefly recall the construction that has been done for Teichmüller space. For a more detailed exposition see
[
Don03
]
,
[
Tra18
]
and
[
MST21
, §4.2]
.

Let
\rho_{0}:=\mathrm{d}x\wedge\mathrm{d}y
be the standard area form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and consider the space

\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):=\{J\in\End(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ |\ J^{2}=-\mathds{1},\ \rho_{0}(v,Jv)>0\ \text{for some}\ v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}\}\ .

Such a space is a
2
-dimensional manifold and it is easy to see that
\forall J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, the tensor
g_{J}^{0}(\cdot,\cdot):=\rho_{0}(\cdot,J\cdot)
is a scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, with respect to which
J
is an orthogonal endomorphism.
By differentiating the identity
J^{2}=-\mathds{1}
, it follows that

T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=\{\dot{J}\in\End(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ |\ J\dot{J}+\dot{J}J=0\}\ .

Equivalently, the space
T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
can be identified with the trace-less and
g_{J}^{0}
-symmetric endomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. It carries a natural (almost) complex structure given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{I}}:T_{J}

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

\displaystyle\dot{J}\mapsto-J\dot{J}

Moreover, there is a natural scalar product defined on each tangent space

\langle\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime}\rangle_{J}:=\frac{1}{2}\tr(\dot J\dot J')\ ,

for every
\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The group
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
acts by conjugation on
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
: for
P\in\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
and
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
one defines
P\cdot J:=PJP^{-1}
. The same formula can be used to define the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on
T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as well.

Lemma 3.4
.

The pairing given by

\widehat{\Omega}_{J}(\dot{J},\dot{J}):=-\frac{1}{2}\tr(\dot JJ\dot J')

defines a symplectic form on
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, compatible with
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{J}
. In particular, the triple
(\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{J},\widehat{\mathcal{I}},\widehat{\Omega}_{J})
is an
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariant Kähler structure on
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Now let
P
be the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
frame bundle over
(\Sigma,\rho)
, namely the fibre over a point
x\in\Sigma
is given by those linear maps
F:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to T_{x}\Sigma
such that
F^{*}\rho_{x}=\rho_{0}
. The frame bundle
P
inherits the structure of an
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-principal bundle with the following action:
B\cdot(x,F):=(x,F\circ B^{-1})
, for
B\in\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
. Notice that any symplectomorphism
\psi
of
(\Sigma,\rho)
naturally lifts to a diffeomorphism
\hat{\psi}
of the total space
P
, by setting

\hat{\psi}(x,F):=(\psi(x),\mathrm{d}_{x}\psi\circ F)\in P\ ,

for every
(x,F)\in P
. Let us define the bundle

P\big(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P\times\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\SL(2,\mathbb{R})$}\right.},

where
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
acts diagonally on the two factors. Notice that a section of
P\big(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
induces an almost complex structure
J
on
\Sigma
which is compatible with
\rho
, i.e.
g_{J}(\cdot,\cdot):=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
defines a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
. The induced almost complex structure on
\Sigma
is fibre-wise defined on
T_{x}\Sigma
as:
F_{x}\circ J_{x}\circ F_{x}^{-1}
. It is easy to see that the section
J
is well-defined as if two pairs
\big((x,F),J_{x}\big)
and
\big((x,F^{\prime},J_{x}^{\prime})\big)
differ by the diagonal action of
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
, then they induce the same almost complex structure on
T_{x}\Sigma
. According to the above construction, let us introduce the space of almost complex structures on
\Sigma
:

\mathcal{J}(\Sigma):=\Gamma\big(\Sigma,P\big(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\big)\ .

Given any
J\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, a tangent vector
\dot{J}\in T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
identifies with a section of the pull-back vector bundle
J^{*}\big(T^{\text{vert}}P\big(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\big)\to\Sigma
, where
T^{\text{vert}}P\big(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
stands for the vertical sub-bundle of
TP\big(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)
with respect to the projection
\pi:P\big(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\big)\to\Sigma
. Equivalently,
\dot{J}
is a section of End
(T\Sigma)
that satisfies
\dot{J}J+J\dot{J}=0
. One can formally define a symplectic form on the infinite-dimensional manifold
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
by integrating fibre-wise that on
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. In other words,

(3.8)

\Omega_{J}(\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime}):=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\dot JJ\dot J')\rho\ .

Furthermore, one obtains a complex structure
\mathcal{I}
on
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, by applying point-wise
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}
which is defined on
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. At this point, the main goal is to explain that such a symplectic form and complex structure can be induced from the ambient
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
to Teichmüller space, using the theory of symplectic reduction. In the end, one succeeds in doing more, namely,
\Omega_{J}
and
\mathcal{I}
will be part of a Kähler metric on
\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)
which turns out to be a multiple of the Weil-Petersson metric. Let us briefly recall the definition of Hamiltonian action and moment map.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
G
be a Lie group, with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, acting on a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
by symplectomorphisms. We say the action is
Hamiltonian
if there exists a smooth function
\mu:M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

The function
\mu
is equivariant with respect to the
G
-action on
M
and the co-adjoint action on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, namely

(3.9)

\mu_{g\cdot p}=\Ad^{*}(g)(\mu_{p}):=\mu_{p}\circ\Ad(g^{-1})\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}\ .

(ii)

Given
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
, let
X_{\xi}
be the vector field on
M
generating the action of the
1
-parameter subgroup generated by
\xi
, i.e.
X_{\xi}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\text{exp}(t\xi)\cdot p|_{t=0}
. Then, for every
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
we have

(3.10)

\mathrm{d}\mu^{\xi}=\iota_{X_{\xi}}\omega=\omega(X_{\xi},\cdot)

where
\mu^{\xi}:M\to\mathbb{R}
is the function
\mu^{\xi}(p):=\mu_{p}(\xi)
.

A map
\mu
satisfying the two properties above is called a
moment map
for the Hamiltonian action.

Theorem 3.6

(
[
Don03
]
,
[
Tra18
]
)
.

Let
c:=\frac{2\pi\chi(\Sigma)}{\text{Vol}(\Sigma,\rho)}
, then the function

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mu:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)^{*}

\displaystyle J\ \longmapsto\ -2(K_{J}-c)\rho

is a moment map for the action of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
on
(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma),\Omega)
, where
K_{J}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Sigma)
is the Gaussian curvature of
g_{J}
.

Observe that, by the Gauss-Bonnet Theorem, the
2
-form
-2(K_{J}-c)\rho
is exact, according to the inclusion
B^{2}(\Sigma)\subset\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)^{*}
introduced in Section
3.1
. Because of property (i) in Definition (
3.5
), the subset
\mu^{-1}(0)\subset\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
is preserved by the action of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
. In particular, any variation
\dot{J}=\mathcal{L}_{X}J
, with
X
an Hamiltonian vector field and
J\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
, lies inside
\text{Ker}\mathrm{d}_{J}\mu
, which is identified with
T_{J}\mu^{-1}(0)
. In other words, the tangent space to the
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-orbit is entirely contained in the Kernel of
\mathrm{d}_{J}\mu
, for any
J\in\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
. Furthermore, by property (ii) in Definition
3.5
, for any
