J\in\mu^{-1}(0)
the space
\text{Ker}(\mathrm{d}_{J}\mu)
is identified with the
\Omega_{J}
-orthogonal to
T_{J}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot J\big)
, namely the tangent space to the
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-orbit. By using a geometric characterization of the elements in the
\Omega_{J}
-orthogonal to the orbit (
[
Don03
]
) one can induce a symplectic form on the quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma):=\mu^{-1}(0)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
. However, the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
is not isomorphic to Teichmüller space of the surface as it is a manifold of real dimension
6g-6+2g
. The further quotient of the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
by the group

(see Lemma
3.2
)

H:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)$}\right.}\cong H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})\ ,

can be identified with
\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)
(see
[
Don03
, §2.2]
). The
H
-orbits in
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
are complex and symplectic submanifolds (see
[
Don03
, §2.2]
and
[
Tra18
, Lemma 4.4.8]
), hence one gets an induced symplectic form on
\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)
given by:

\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{[J]}([\dot{J}],[\dot{J}^{\prime}])=\Omega_{J}(\dot{J}_{h},\dot{J}_{h}^{\prime})\ ,

where the vectors
\dot{J}_{h},\dot{J}_{h}^{\prime}\in\text{Ker}(\mathrm{d}_{J}\mu)
are lifts of
\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime}
that are
\Omega_{J}
-orthogonal to the
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
-orbit. If one further re-normalizes the lift
\dot{J}_{h}
so that it is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to the tangent space to the orbit, one recovers the classical description of the tangent space to Teichmüller space as the space of traceless Codazzi tensors (
[
Tro12
]
). In that case, the formula of Weil-Petersson metric is also recovered by choosing an area form
\rho
with
\text{Vol}(\Sigma,\rho)=-2\pi\chi(\Sigma)
, which means
c=-1
in Theorem
3.6
.

Proposition 3.7

(
[
BMS15
, §2.1]
)
.

Let
\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime}
be elements in
T_{[J]}\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)
, then the Weil-Petersson symplectic form and metric are respectively given by:

(3.12)

\big(\Omega_{\text{WP}}\big)_{[J]}(\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime})=-\frac{1}{8}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\dot J J\dot J')\mathrm{d}V,\qquad\big(G_{\text{WP}}\big)_{[J]}(\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{8}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\dot J\dot J')\mathrm{d}V\ ,

where
\mathrm{d}V
is the area form of the unique hyperbolic metric with conformal structure
J
.

Remark 3.8
.

One of the key facts of this construction is that any choice of a supplement
V
of
T_{J}\big(\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot J\big)
inside the Kernel of
\mathrm{d}\mu
and
\Omega_{J}
-orthogonal to
T_{J}\big(\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot J\big)
, provides a well-defined model for the tangent space to
\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)
, such that
(V,\Omega_{J}|_{V})
is symplectomorphic to
(T_{[J]}\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma),4\Omega_{\text{WP}})
.

3.3.
A formula for the differential of the curvature

Here we briefly explain how to derive a formula for the first variation of the curvature
K_{J}
, using the theory introduced in the previous section. That expression will be useful later, when we show how equations (
1.2
) come from a symplectic reduction argument (see Section
5.3
). We will follow closely the approach in
[
MST21
, §4.2]
.

Given any
B\in\mathrm{End}(T\Sigma)
and given a Riemannian metric
g
on
\Sigma
, we define the
divergence
of the endomorphism
B
as the
1
-form:

(3.13)

(\mathrm{div}_{g}B)(X):=\sum_{i}g\big((\nabla^{g}_{e_{i}}B)X,e_{i}\big)\ ,

where
(e_{i})_{i}
is a
g
-orthonormal frame of
T\Sigma
,
\nabla^{g}
is the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g
and
X
is a smooth vector field on the surface. We will denote, likewise, the divergence of a vector field
V
by
\mathrm{div}_{g}V
. Moreover, whenever there is a fixed almost complex structure
J
on the surface, the divergence will be taken with respect to
g_{J}=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)\equiv g
. Given that
J
is
\nabla^{g}
-parallel, namely
(\nabla^{g}_{X}J)Y=0
for all
X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
, one can deduce the following useful formula:

(3.14)

\mathrm{div}_{g}(JB)=-(\mathrm{div}_{g}B)\circ J

for any trace-less and
g
-symmetric endomorphism
B
. Another relation we will be using later is the following:

(3.15)

\mathrm{div}_{g}(X)=\mathrm{d}(\iota_{X}\rho)(v,Jv)

for any unit vector
v
.

Lemma 3.9

(
[
MST21
]
)
.

Let
X
be a vector field on
\Sigma
, then

\frac{1}{2}\tr\bigg(\dot J J\liederivative_XJ\bigg)=(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(X)-\mathrm{div}_{g}(JX)\ ,

where
\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J\big)(Y):=[X,JY]-J([X,Y])
for any
Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
.

By using the explicit expression of the moment map
\mu
in Theorem
3.6
and the above Lemma, one can prove the following result:

Proposition 3.10

(
[
MST21
]
)
.

Let
J
be any almost complex structure on
\Sigma
and
\rho
a fixed area form, then

\mathrm{d}K_{J}(\dot{J})\rho=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\big)\ ,

where
K_{J}
is the Gaussian curvature of
g_{J}\equiv g
.

4.
The pseudo-Kähler metric on the Hitchin component

This is the core section of the paper, in which the main result, namely Theorem
A
, will be proved (Section
4.2
). In particular, after defining an infinite-dimensional space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
starting from a general construction of Donaldson (Section
4.1
), we look for a specific distribution inside the tangent to a set
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
sitting inside
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. Each vector space
W_{(J,A)}
of this distribution is defined as the space of solutions to a system of PDEs. After studying in detail the above system of equations (Section
4.3
), we show that such a finite dimensional subspace
W_{(J,A)}
, can be identified (up to a further finite dimensional decomposition) with the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component (Section
4.4
). Finally, in Section
4.5
we generalize a previous result obtained in the case of the torus (
[
RT24
, §3.2]
).

4.1.
Construction of
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))

Here we use the notations introduced in Section
3.2
. Recall that
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the space of (almost) complex structures on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
compatible with the standard orientation. Now, consider the following space

(4.1)

D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})):=\{(J,C)\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times S_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ |\ C(J\cdot,J\cdot,J\cdot)=-C(J\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)\}\ ,

where
S_{3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the space of totally symmetric
(0,3)
-tensors. Any pair
(J,C)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
defines a unique pair
(J,q)
, where
q
is a
J
-holomorphic cubic differential on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In particular, there is a
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-equivariant isomorphism between
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and the holomorphic vector bundle of cubic differentials
Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}(T^{2})\big)
over Teichmüller space of the torus (
[
RT24
, Corollary 3.3]
).

Remark 4.1
.

Thanks to the relations between the tensor
C
and the
(1,2)
-tensor
A:=g_{J}^{-1}C
(see Theorem
2.7
), it is possible to describe
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
as the space formed by pairs
(J,A)
, where
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
A
is a
1
-form with values in the bundle of trace-less and
g_{J}
-symmetric endomorphisms of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J
and
A(X)Y=A(Y)X
for any
X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
. We will make repetitive use of this correspondence, using the tensor
C
or the Pick form
A
whichever is more convenient.

Because of the identification
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cong\mathcal{T}(T^{2})
, the space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
has the structure of a vector bundle over
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, whose fiber at a point
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a two dimensional real vector space, denoted with
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))_{J}
. Let
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
be a
g_{J}
-orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\{e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}\}
be its dual, then any element
A
in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))_{J}
can be written as
A=A_{1}e_{1}^{*}+A_{2}e_{2}^{*}
, where
A_{k}:=A(e_{k})
for
k=1,2
. Hence, we can introduce a scalar product on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))_{J}
by

(4.2)

\langle A,B\rangle_{J}:=\tr(A\wedge\ast_JB)(e_{1},e_{2})\ ,

or, more explicitly after expanding the wedge product and evaluating the
2
-form,

(4.3)

\langle A,B\rangle_{J}=\tr(A_{1}B_{1}+A_{2}B_{2})e_{1}^{*}\wedge e_{2}^{*}(e_{1},e_{2})=\tr(A_{1}B_{1}+A_{2}B_{2})\ .

Remark 4.2
.

Although the definition of the scalar product in (
4.2
) differs from that found in
[
RT24
]
, defined by integrating the
2
-form
\tr(A\wedge\ast_JB)
over the torus, the two are equivalent. In fact, one can always assume that the flat metric
g_{J}
is of unit area. Moreover, it is easy to check that relation (
4.3
) does not depend on the choice of basis.

By exploiting the definition in (
4.3
), the following relation can be deduced:

(4.4)

\langle AJ,BJ\rangle_{J}=\langle A,B\rangle_{J}\ ,

which is equivalent to

(4.5)

\langle AJ,B\rangle_{J}=-\langle A,BJ\rangle_{J}\ .

Lemma 4.3

(
[
RT24
, Lemma 3.6]
)
.

Let
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and let
\dot{A}:=g_{J}^{-1}\dot{C}
denotes the unique
(1,2)
-tensor such that
g_{J}(\dot{A}(X)Y,Z)=\dot{C}(X,Y,Z)
for all
X,Y,Z\in\Gamma(T\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Then, an element
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
belongs to
T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
if and only if

(4.6)

\dot{J}\in T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\tr\dot{A}(X)=\tr(JA(X)\dot J)\ \forall X\in\Gamma(T\mathbb{R}^{2}),\quad\dot{A}_{0}=\dot{\tilde{A}}_{0}+T(J,A,\dot{J})

where
\dot{A}_{0}
is the full trace-free part of
\dot{A}
, while the tensor
\dot{\tilde{A}}_{0}
is the trace-free part of
\dot{A}
independent of
\dot{J}
and
T(J,A,\dot{J})=A_{1}J\dot{J}Ee_{1}^{*}+2A_{2}J\dot{J}Ee_{2}^{*}
in a local basis dual to a
g_{J}
-orthonormal frame
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
, with
E=\mathrm{diag}(1,-1)
.

Notice that, the endomorphism part of the tangent vector
\dot{A}
at the point
(J,A)
is still
g_{J}
-symmetric, but its trace-part is completely determined by
\dot{J}
due to (
4.6
). In particular, there is a decomposition

\dot{A}=\dot{A}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\tr(JA\dot J)\mathds{1}\ ,

where
\mathds{1}
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix and the second term in the RHS of the above equation is the trace-part of
\dot{A}
, which will be denoted by
\dot{A}_{\text{tr}}
. There is an
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
given by

(4.7)

P\cdot(J,A):=(PJP^{-1},PA(P^{-1}\cdot)P^{-1})\ ,

where
P\in\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
and
A(P^{-1}\cdot)
has to be interpreted as the action of
P^{-1}
via pull-back on the
1
-form part of
A
. It is easy to show that the scalar product introduced in (
4.2
) is invariant under the above action. Hence, by differentiating the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action we get an induced linear isomorphism between
T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and
T_{P\cdot(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, which is given explicitly by

P\cdot(\dot{J},\dot{A})=(P\dot{J}P^{-1},P\dot{A}(P^{-1}\cdot)P^{-1})\\

where
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and
P\in\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
. We can define a similar scalar product on pairs
\dot{A},\dot{B}
as:

\langle\dot{A},\dot{B}\rangle_{J}:=\tr(\dot A_1\dot B_1+\dot A_2\dot B_2)\ .

In the following we will denote with
||\cdot||_{J}=||\cdot||
the norm induced by the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{J}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. In order to simplify the notation we define
||A||_{0}^{2}:=\frac{1}{8}||A||_{J}^{2}
.

Let
f:[0,+\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
be a smooth function such that
f(0)=0,\ f^{\prime}(t)<0
for each
t>0
and
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}f(t)=-\infty
. Then, we define the following symmetric bi-linear form on
T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

(4.8)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathbf{g}}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A});(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big):=\big(1-f(||A||_{0}^{2})\big)\langle\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{f^{\prime}(||A||_{0}^{2})}{6}\langle\dot{A}_{0},\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime}(||A||_{0}^{2})}{12}\langle\dot{A}_{\text{tr}},\dot{A}_{\text{tr}}^{\prime}\rangle

and the endomorphism
\widehat{\mathbf{I}}
of
T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

(4.9)

\widehat{\mathbf{I}}_{(J,A)}(\dot{J},\dot{A}):=(-J\dot{J},-\dot{A}J-A\dot{J})

where the products
\dot{A}J
and
A\dot{J}
have to be interpreted as a matrix multiplication. Matching these two objects together we get the following
2
-form:

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}(\cdot,\cdot)=\widehat{\mathbf{g}}_{f}(\cdot,\widehat{\mathbf{I}}\cdot)

which is given by:

(4.10)

\displaystyle(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A});(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big)=\big(f(||A||_{0}^{2})-1\big)\langle\dot{J},J\dot{J}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime}(||A||_{0}^{2})}{12}\langle\dot{A}_{\tr},\ast_{J}\dot{A}_{\tr}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime}(||A||_{0}^{2})}{6}\langle\dot{A}_{0},\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}J\rangle\ .

Theorem 4.4

(
[
RT24
]
)
.

The triple
(\widehat{\mathbf{g}}_{f},\widehat{\mathbf{I}},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f})
defines an
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariant pseudo-Kähler metric on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

Remark 4.5
.

We want to point out the difference of the coefficients in front of the two terms with
f^{\prime}
in both the symplectic form and the pseudo-Riemannian metric, from our previous work (
[
RT24
]
). This change is due to the fact that, in the case of genus
g\geq 2
, the cubic differential, hence the tensor
A
, is rescaled by a factor of
\sqrt{2}
(see also Remark
2.11
), so that the moment map we introduce in Section
5.2
will be closely related to the Wang’s equation for hyperbolic affine spheres in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Now let
\Sigma
be a closed smooth surface of genus
g\geq 2
. The next step is to perform a construction similar to that done for
\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
in Section
3.2
, so as to obtain an infinite-dimensional space, associated with
\Sigma
, and endowed with a (formal) pseudo-Kähler structure. Let
P
be the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-frame bundle over
(\Sigma,\rho)
introduced in Section
3.2
and consider the bundle

P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P\times D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\SL(2,\mathbb{R})$}\right.}\ ,

where
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
acts diagonally on the two factors. The fibre of
P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
over a point
x\in\Sigma
identifies with
D^{3}\big(\mathcal{J}(T_{x}\Sigma)\big)
, namely the space of pairs
(J_{x},A_{x})
where
J_{x}
is an almost complex structure on
T_{x}\Sigma
compatible with
\rho_{x}
, and
A_{x}
is an End
{}_{0}(T_{x}\Sigma,(g_{J_{x}})_{x})
-valued
1
-form such that
A_{x}(J_{x}\cdot)=A_{x}(\cdot)J_{x}
and
A_{x}(X)Y=A_{x}(Y)X,\ \forall X,Y\in\Gamma(T_{x}\Sigma)
. Since the pseudo-Kähler metric on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariant, each fibre
D^{3}\big(\mathcal{J}(T_{x}\Sigma)\big)
is naturally endowed with a pseudo-Kähler structure, still denoted with
((\widehat{\mathbf{g}}_{f})_{x},\widehat{\mathbf{I}}_{x},(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f})_{x})
, obtained by identifying
T_{x}\Sigma
with
\mathbb{R}^{2}
using an area-preserving isomorphism
F_{x}:T_{x}\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The space of smooth sections

D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)):=\Gamma\big(\Sigma,P(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})))\big)

is identified with the set of pairs
(J,A)
, where
J
is a complex structure on
\Sigma
, and
A
is an End
{}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})
-valued
1
-form such that
A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J
and
A(X)Y=A(Y)X,\ \forall X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
. Moreover, there is an identification between the tangent space to
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
at
(J,A)
and the space of sections of the vector bundle
(J,A)^{*}\big(T^{\text{vert}}P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)\big)\to\Sigma
, where
T^{\text{vert}}P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
stands for the vertical sub-bundle of
TP\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
with respect to the projection map
P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)\to\Sigma
. We can consider tangent vectors
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
at
(J,A)
as the data of (see Lemma
4.3
):

•

a section
\dot{J}
of End
(T\Sigma)
such that
\dot{J}J+J\dot{J}=0
, namely
\dot{J}
is a
g_{J}
-symmetric and trace-less endomorphism of
T\Sigma
;

•

an End
(T\Sigma,g_{J})
-valued
1
-form
\dot{A}
such that

(4.11)

\dot{A}=\dot{A}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\tr(JA\dot J)\mathds{1}\ ,

where
\mathds{1}
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix and
\dot{A}_{0}
is the trace-less part of
\dot{A}
. In particular, the trace-part of
\dot{A}
is uniquely determined by
\dot{J}
.

The infinite-dimensional space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
inherits a (formal) family of pseudo-Kähler structures, where the symplectic form is defined as

(4.12)

(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big):=\int_{\Sigma}\hat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big)\rho

and the pseudo-Riemannian metric is given by

(4.13)

(\mathbf{g}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big):=\int_{\Sigma}\hat{\mathbf{g}}_{f}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big)\rho\ ,

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{g}}_{f}
denote, respectively, the symplectic form and pseudo-Riemannian metric induced on the pull-back of the vertical sub-bundle inside
TP\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
as described above. Likewise we get a linear endomorphism

\mathbf{I}_{(J,A)}:T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\to T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))

obtained by applying pointwisely the endomorphism
\widehat{\mathbf{I}}
to a smooth section
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
(see
[
Koi90
, §2]
).

Remark 4.6
.

It is important to point out that the definition of each element of the pseudo-Kähler structure on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
is identical to that given in (
4.8
), (
4.9
) and (
4.10
), the only change is that now the elements
J,A,g_{J},\dot{J},\dot{A}
are all tensors and
f\big(||A||_{0}^{2}\big)
is a smooth function on
\Sigma
. Because of this similarity, in the remainder of the discussion we will make use of some relations proved in the previous work
[
RT24
]
and which we will recall when necessary. The general idea to keep in mind is that the identities for elements in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
can be interpreted as point-wise identities at the level of smooth sections inside
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. In both our setting and anti-de Sitter one the definition of the infinite-dimensional space follows the lines of a much more general construction given by Donaldson (
[
Don03
, §2.1]
), and for this reason the same phenomenon described above happens in either situation (
[
MST21
, Remark 4.9]
).

4.2.
Proof of Theorem A

The aim of this section is to summarize the strategy of the proof of Theorem
A
. We will present preliminary results, already stated in Section
1.1
and proved later in the paper, which will allow us to give a quite immediate proof of the main theorem mentioned above. The same approach was used in
[
MST21
, §4.4]
with the appropriate differences.

Recall from Section
2.5
that
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
is the quotient of the infinite-dimensional space

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho):=\left\{(J,C)\ \Bigg|\ \parbox{190.00029pt}{$J$ is an (almost) complex structure on $\Sigma$ \\
$C$ is the real part of a $J$-cubic differential \\
$\big(h:=e^{F\big(\frac{||q||^{2}_{g_{J}}}{2}\big)}g_{J},A:=g_{J}^{-1}C\big)$ satisfy (\ref{Gausscodazzi})}\right\}

by the action of
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, where
F
is the smooth function defined in Lemma
2.18
. The main idea is to define an
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-invariant distribution
\{W_{(J,A)}\}_{(J,A)}
inside
T\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, whose integral manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is the finite-dimensional quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
. Because of the very specific choice of the subspaces
W_{(J,A)}
, the further finite-dimensional quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)/H
, with
H:=\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
, is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
.

Definition 4.7
.

Given
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, define
W_{(J,A)}
to be the subspace of
T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
formed by those elements
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
satisfying the following system of equations:

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\big(\divr\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\big)=0\\
\mathrm{d}\big(\divr\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)\circ J+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}_{0}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\circ J\big)=0\\
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\divr\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0\end{cases}

where
\beta(\bullet):=\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle
is a
1
-form,
\dot{f}_{0}=-\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle
is a smooth function on
\Sigma
and
f
is the function given by (
2.13
). Moreover, all the expressions for
f,f^{\prime}
and
\dot{f}
are evaluated at
||A||_{0}^{2}=\frac{||A||^{2}_{J}}{8}
.

Remark 4.8
.

The third equation in the above system can be re-written as
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)J=(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)
, which is equivalent to the following:

(4.14)

\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,J\bullet)=\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}(\bullet)A(J\bullet)-\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}(J\bullet)A(\bullet)\ .

In fact, by
C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
-linearity, it is sufficient to perform the computation on a pair
\{X,JX\}
, for
X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(X,JX)J

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\wedge A)(X,JX)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(X)\cdot A(JX)-(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(JX)\cdot A(X)

which is exactly the right-hand side of (
4.14
) computed on
X
(as an End
{}_{0}(T\Sigma,g)
-valued
1
-form). This new form of the equation will be crucial to some key steps in our argument.

Theorem E
.

Let
(J,A)
be a point in
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, then

\mathrm{dim}W_{(J,A)}\geq 16g-16+2g\ .

The latter result will be a consequence of a detailed study of the system of equations defining
W_{(J,A)}
. The difficult part lies in computing the principal symbols of the matrix operator associated with the three equations in (
1.2
). It is then possible to conclude, using standard results from the theory of elliptic operators on compact manifolds.

Theorem F
.

For every element
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, the vector space
W_{(J,A)}
is contained inside
T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
and it is invariant by the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
. Moreover, the collection
\{W_{(J,A)}\}_{(J,A)}
defines a
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-invariant distribution on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
and the natural projection
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\to\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
induces a linear isomorphism

\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}\pi:W_{(J,A)}\longrightarrow T_{[J,A]}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)

Combining together Theorem
E
and Theorem
F
, we observe that the integral manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
has dimension equal to
16g-16+2g
and, for this reason, cannot be isomorphic to the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component. In fact, it is necessary to perform an additional (finite-dimensional) quotient of
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
by the group
H:=\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
isomorphic to
H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
(see Lemma
3.2
).

Theorem G
.

The
H
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is free and proper, with complex and symplectic
H
-orbits. Moreover, the pseudo-Kähler structure
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
descend to the quotient which is identified with
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
. Finally, the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
induced on the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component coincides with the one found by Labourie and Loftin.

Remark 4.9
.

The tangent space to the integral manifold
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
, i.e. the subspace
W_{(J,A)}
, decomposes as a direct sum
V_{(J,A)}\oplus S_{(J,A)}
, where
V_{(J,A)}
is the tangent space to the Hitchin component and
S_{(J,A)}:=\{\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)\ |\ X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma),\ \mathrm{d}(\iota_{X}\rho)=\mathrm{d}(\iota_{JX}\rho)=0\}
, namely the tangent space to the harmonic orbit (see Section
3.1
). Using the definition of
W_{(J,A)}
in terms of the system of equations (
1.2
), we get a similar description of the tangent space to the Hitchin component. In particular,
V_{(J,A)}
can be characterized as the subspace of
W_{(J,A)}
(see Section
6.1
) defined by the following system:

(4.15)

\begin{cases}\divr\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta=\mathrm{d}\gamma_{1}\\
\divr\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)\circ J+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}_{0}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\circ J=\mathrm{d}\gamma_{2}\\
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\divr\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0\end{cases}

for some
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
. In a more concise form:

(4.16)

V_{(J,A)}=\left\{(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\ \bigg|\ \parbox{150.00023pt}{$\alpha_{1}+i\alpha_{2}$ is exact \\
$\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\divr\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0$}\right\}

where
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are the
1
-forms in (
4.15
) defined by the LHS of the first two equations.

At this point, we have all the ingredients to present a concise proof of the main result of the paper.

Theorem A
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed oriented surface of genus
g\geq 2
. Then, there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{F}(\Sigma)}
of the Fuchsian locus in
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
, which admits a mapping class group invariant pseudo-Kähler metric
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
. Moreover, the Fuchsian locus embeds as a totally geodesic submanifold and the triple
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
restricts to a (multiple of) the Weil-Petersson Kähler metric of Teichmüller space.

Proof.

The tangent space
T_{[J,A]}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
can be identified with
W_{(J,A)}
(Theorem
F
), hence we can define a complex structure
\mathbf{I}
, and a pseudo-Riemannian metric
\mathbf{g}_{f}
by restriction from the infinite-dimensional space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. This definition does not depend on the representative in the
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-orbit by the invariance statement in Theorem
F
and the
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
-invariance of
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{g}_{f}
. It is immediate that
\mathbf{I}
is still compatible with
\mathbf{g}_{f}
and that the pairing
\mathbf{g}_{f}(\cdot,\mathbf{I}\cdot)
coincides with the
2
-form
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
restricted to
W_{(J,A)}
. Moreover, Theorem
G
allows us to induce the triple
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
on the quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)/H\cong\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
, in such a way that the induced complex structure is equivalent to the one found by Labourie and Loftin. Thanks to the
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
-invariance of
\mathbf{g}_{f}
and
\mathbf{I}
, it follows that the induced structure on
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
is invariant under the action of the mapping class group since it is isomorphic to
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)/\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
.

Notice that the Fuchsian locus
\mathcal{F}(\Sigma)
(see Section
2.1
and
2.2
) inside
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)\cong\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
corresponds to pairs
(J,A)
with
A=0
. According to Remark
4.9
, the tangent space to
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)
along the Fuchsian locus is isomorphic to
V_{(J,0)}
, and thus consists of pairs
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
such that
\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}=0
and
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\cdot,\cdot)=0
. The pseudo-metric restricted to
V_{(J,0)}
is equal to

(\mathbf{g}_{f})_{(J,0)}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A});(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big)=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime}\rangle\rho+\int_{\Sigma}\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\dot{A}_{0},\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle\rho

since the trace-part of
\dot{A}
is equal to zero according to relation (
4.11
). Notice that
\mathbf{g}_{f}
on
V_{(J,0)}
coincides with
4G_{\text{WP}}
along horizontal directions (
\dot{A}=0
) and it is negative-definite along vertical directions (
\dot{J}=0
). Because of the explicit description of
\mathbf{g}_{f}
, this is sufficient to conclude that the pseudo-metric is non-degenerate on arbitrary directions inside
V_{(J,0)}
as well. In particular, there must exist an open neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{F}(\Sigma)}
of
\mathcal{F}(\Sigma)
inside
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
in which
\mathbf{g}_{f}
is non-degenerate.

Finally, the Fuchsian locus is the set of fixed points of the circle action, that consists of isometries for
\mathbf{g}_{f}
by Theorem
C
(which is proved in Section
4.5
). Using a standard argument in (pseudo)-Riemannian geometry, this implies that the Fuchsian locus is totally geodesic.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

It is important to emphasize again that the triple
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
is defined over the entire Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
, but may be degenerate away from the Fuchsian locus. The main problem lies in the fact that the restriction of an indefinite metric on a subspace is not necessarily non-degenerate (as in the positive-definite case). Partial results have been obtained concerning the non-existence of degenerate vectors outside
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{F}(\Sigma)}
, which will be explained in detail in Section
6.2
.

4.3.
The system of equations

This section is devoted to the study of the system of equations defined by (
1.2
) and to the proof of Theorem
E
. More precisely, in Lemma
4.11
and Lemma
4.12
we study the induced connection on the endomorphism bundle and the associated exterior covariant derivative. Then, Lemma
4.13
allows us to compute the terms involving derivatives of order two of
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
in the first and second equation appearing in the system defining
W_{(J,A)}
. Further on, we explain how the space
W_{(J,A)}
can be seen as the kernel of a matrix of mixed-order smooth differential operators, which is proven to be elliptic (Lemma
4.14
). Finally, using the homotopy invariance of the Fredholm index for elliptic operators, we deduce the lower bound on the dimension of
W_{(J,A)}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
\nabla
be the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g_{J}
, then the induced connection

\widebar{\nabla}:\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))\longrightarrow\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))

does not admit any non-zero parallel section, where
\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})
denotes the real vector bundle of
g_{J}
-symmetric and trace-less endomorphisms of
T\Sigma
.

Proof.

Let
B\in\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))
such that
\widebar{\nabla}B=0
. Let
x_{0}\in\Sigma
be a fixed point and
x\in\Sigma
be arbitrary. Consider a path
\gamma:[0,1]\to\Sigma
with
\gamma(0)=x_{0}
and
\gamma(1)=x
. Let
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
be a basis of
T_{x_{0}}\Sigma
and denote with
\{e_{1}(t),e_{2}(t)\}
the basis of
T_{\gamma(t)}\Sigma
obtained by parallel transport
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
along the path
\gamma
. Then, if
b_{ij}(t)
denotes the
(i,j)
-th entry of
B_{\gamma(t)}
for
i,j=1,2
, we have
b_{ij}(t)=g_{J}\big(B_{\gamma(t)}(e_{j}(t)),e_{i}(t)\big)
. By differentiating the last identity with respect to the parameter
t
, we get:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}b_{ij}(t)=g_{J}\big(\underbrace{\big(\widebar{\nabla}_{\dot{\gamma}}B\big)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=0\end{subarray}}\big(e_{j}(t)\big)+B_{\gamma(t)}\big(\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}e_{j}\big),e_{i}(t)\big)+g_{J}\big(B_{\gamma(t)}\big(e_{j}(t)\big),\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}e_{i}\big)\ .

Since the basis
\{e_{1}(t),e_{2}(t)\}
has been obtained by parallel transport, we have
\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}}e_{j}=0
for any
j=1,2
. In particular, we deduce that each entry of
B
is constant along
\gamma
, hence
B_{x}=B_{\gamma(1)}=B_{\gamma(0)}=B_{x_{0}}
. Since
x\in\Sigma
was arbitrary, it follows that the endomorphism
B
is actually constant on the whole surface. At this point, it is enough to show that every section of
E:=\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})
admits at least one zero to conclude the proof. Since the real rank of
E
is equal to the real dimension of the surface, any section
B
is nowhere zero if and only if the Euler class
e\big(E\big)
is trivial in
H^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
. In our case, it can be shown (see for example
[
Tro12
, §2.4]
) that
E
is the realization of the holomorphic line bundle
K\otimes K
defined on
(\Sigma,J)
. In particular,
e\big(E\big)=c_{1}\big(K\otimes K\big)
, where
c_{1}
denotes the first Chern class of a complex vector bundle. Therefore,

\int_{\Sigma}e\big(E\big)=\int_{\Sigma}c_{1}\big(K\otimes K\big)=\text{deg}(K\otimes K)=2(2g-2)\neq 0\ .

The last chain of equalities implies that
e\big(E\big)
is not trivial in cohomology by Poincaré duality, and thus any such section
B
admits at least one zero.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\nabla
be the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g_{J}
, then the exterior covariant derivative

\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}:\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))\longrightarrow\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))

is surjective and its kernel has real dimension equal to
10g-10
.

Proof.

Recall that for
A\in\Omega^{1}\big(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})\big)
and for any
X,Y,Z\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
we have

\big(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A\big)(X,Y)Z=\big(\nabla_{X}A\big)(Y)Z-\big(\nabla_{Y}A\big)(X)Z\ .

In particular, if we define the
(0,3)
-tensor
C(X,Y,Z):=g_{J}(A(X)Y,Z)
, then
A\in\text{Ker}(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla})
if and only if
C
is the real part of a
g_{J}
-holomorphic cubic differential (Theorem
2.7
). The space of holomorphic cubic differentials on
(\Sigma,g_{J})
coincides with the space
H^{0}(\Sigma,K^{\otimes^{3}})
of holomorphic sections of the tri-canonical bundle, which is isomorphic (as a real vector space) to
\mathbb{R}^{10g-10}
by an easy application of Riemann-Roch Theorem for curves.

Concerning the surjectivity of
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}
, we will prove that its Co-kernel is trivial. Let us denote with
\ast_{J}
the Hodge-star operator defined on differential forms with respect to
g_{J}
, which can be extended to an isomorphism
\ast_{J}:\Omega^{k}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\Omega^{2-k}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))
. Let
\big(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\big)^{*}
be the formal adjoint of the exterior covariant derivative with respect to the
L^{2}
-inner product on
\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))
induced by
\ast_{J}
and integration over
\Sigma
. A standard computation shows that

\big(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\big)^{*}=-\ast_{J}\circ\ \mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\circ\ast_{J}:\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))\longrightarrow\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))\ .

Since Range
(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla})
is a closed subspace of
\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))
, we get that
\text{CoKer}(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla})=\text{Ker}\Big(\big(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\big)^{*}\Big)
. In particular, if
\alpha\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))
then

(
\ast_{J}
is an isomorphism)

\displaystyle\big(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\big)^{*}\alpha=0\iff

(
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\equiv\widebar{\nabla}
on
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}(\ast_{J}\alpha)=0\iff

\displaystyle\widebar{\nabla}(\ast_{J}\alpha)=0\ ,

where
\widebar{\nabla}
in the last equation is the induced connection on
\Omega^{0}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))
. According to Lemma
4.11
, the induced connection
\widebar{\nabla}
does not admit any non-zero parallel section, hence
\ast_{J}\alpha=0
, which implies
\alpha=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
and consider the following
2
-forms on the surface

\displaystyle\eta_{1}:=\mathrm{d}\bigg(\mathrm{div}_{g}\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\bigg)\ ,

\displaystyle\eta_{2}:=\mathrm{d}\bigg(\mathrm{div}_{g}\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)\circ J+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}_{0}\circ J+\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{\bullet}A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\bigg)

where
(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})\in T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, the function
f
is the one defined by (
2.13
),
\dot{f}_{0}=-\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle
, and
f,f^{\prime},\dot{f},\dot{f}_{0}
are computed in
||A||_{0}^{2}=\frac{||A||_{J}^{2}}{8}
. Then, the part involving second order derivatives of
(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})
in
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
is, respectively

(f-1)\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\big)+\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\mathrm{d}\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle,\qquad(f-1)\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\circ J\big)-\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\mathrm{d}\langle A,\big(\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\big)J\rangle\ .

Proof.

By using (
3.13
), we get the following equation involving the divergence of a smooth section of End
{}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})
multiplied by a smooth function
\varphi

(4.17)

\mathrm{div}_{g}\big(\varphi\dot{J}\big)(X)=\mathrm{d}\varphi(\dot{J}X)+\varphi\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\big)(X),\quad\forall X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)\ .

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bigg(\mathrm{div}_{g}\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)\bigg)=\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{d}f\circ\dot{J}\big)+\mathrm{d}f\wedge\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}+(f-1)\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\big)\ ,

and it is clear that
(f-1)\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\big)
is the only part involving second order derivatives of
\dot{J}
in the expression above. Regarding the other two terms in
\eta_{1}
, let us first define
\tau_{1}:=\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J
and
\tau_{2}:=-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta
, where
\beta=\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle
.

The differential of
\tau_{1}

Notice that the first order variation of
f\big(||A||_{0}^{2}\big)
is

(see Lemma 3.22 in
[
RT24
]
)

\displaystyle\dot{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{\prime}}{8}(\langle\dot{A},A\rangle+\langle A,\dot{A}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\ .

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\tau_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Big(\mathrm{d}\big(\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\big)\circ J\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{d}\bigg(\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\mathrm{d}f^{\prime}\circ J+f^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\circ J\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{d}\bigg(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{4}\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}A\rangle\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle+f^{\prime}\big(\langle\nabla_{J\bullet}A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle+\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\big)\bigg)\ ,

where in the last step we used that
\mathrm{d}f^{\prime}=\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{4}\langle A,\nabla_{\bullet}A\rangle
. The only interesting part, for our purpose, is the term containing
f^{\prime}\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle
. In particular,

\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{d}\bigg(f^{\prime}\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\bigg)=\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{16}\langle A,\nabla_{\bullet}A\rangle\wedge\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle+\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\mathrm{d}\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\ .

Again, the only part involving second order derivatives is the term
\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\mathrm{d}\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle
.

The differential of
\tau_{2}

To conclude the proof it must be proven that
\mathrm{d}\tau_{2}
does not involve second derivatives of
\dot{J}
or
\dot{A}_{0}
. In fact, by carrying out calculations similar to those made above

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\gamma_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\bigg(-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\bigg(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{4}\langle A,\nabla_{\bullet}A\rangle\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle+f^{\prime}\big(\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle+\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\nabla_{\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\big)\bigg)\ .

Because of the very similar expression of the 2-forms
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
it is easy to see, by going over the calculations already done, that the part involving second derivatives of
(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})
in
\eta_{2}
is exactly

(f-1)\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\circ J\big)-\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\mathrm{d}\langle A,\big(\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\big)J\rangle\ .

∎

The next step is to write down in coordinates the expressions found in Lemma
4.13
, so that, later, we will be able to explicitly deduce the principal symbol of the matrix of operators associated with the PDEs defining the subspace
W_{(J,A)}
. In order to do this, we need to recall the construction in coordinates for
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
(see
[
RT24
, §3.1]
), and then use the particular definition of
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
to infer that the same can be done, point-wise, in the genus
g\geq 2
case (see Remark
4.6
). Let
\rho_{0}:=\mathrm{d}x_{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}y_{0}
be the standard area form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
g_{J}^{0}:=\rho_{0}(\cdot,J\cdot)
be the associated scalar product, for some
J\in\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Recall from Section
4.1
that
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-equivariantly isomorphic to the holomorphic vector bundle of cubic differentials over Teichmüller space of the torus, denoted with
Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}(T^{2})\big)
. The latter can be identified with
\mathbb{H}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}
, where
\mathbb{H}^{2}
is a copy of
\mathcal{T}(T^{2})
and
\mathbb{C}
is isomorphic to the fibre over an oriented (almost) complex structure
J:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
z=x+iy
and
w=u+iv
be the complex coordinates on
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and
\mathbb{C}
, respectively. Then, we have the following correspondence

\mathbb{H}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}\ni(z,w)\longmapsto\big(j(z),C_{(z,w)}\big)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

where
C_{(z,w)}=\mathcal{R}e(q_{(z,w)})
with
q_{(z,w)}=\widebar{w}(\mathrm{d}x_{0}-\widebar{z}\mathrm{d}y_{0})^{3}
(see Corollary
[
RT24
, Lemma 3.18]
and
[
Tra18
, Lemma 5.2.1]
). Because of the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariance, one can compute the pair
(j(z),C_{(z,w)})
at points
(i,w)\equiv(0,1,u,v)
, for some
w\in\mathbb{C}
. Using the relation
A=(g_{J}^{0})^{-1}C
, one can deduce:

\dot{J}=\mathrm{d}_{i}j(\dot{x},\dot{y})=\begin{pmatrix}\dot{x}&-\dot{y}\\
-\dot{y}&-\dot{x}\end{pmatrix},\qquad A_{(i,w)}=\begin{pmatrix}u&v\\
v&-u\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}x_{0}+\begin{pmatrix}v&-u\\
-u&-v\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}y_{0}\ .

(\dot{A}_{0})_{(i,w)}=\begin{pmatrix}\dot{u}+u\dot{y}+v\dot{x}&-u\dot{x}+\dot{v}+v\dot{y}\\
-u\dot{x}+\dot{v}+v\dot{y}&-\dot{u}-u\dot{y}-v\dot{x}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}x_{0}+\begin{pmatrix}\dot{v}+2(v\dot{y}-u\dot{x})&-\dot{u}-2(u\dot{y}+v\dot{x})\\
-\dot{u}-2(u\dot{y}+v\dot{x})&-\dot{v}+2(u\dot{x}-v\dot{y})\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}y_{0}

(\dot{A}_{\tr})_{(i,w)}=\begin{pmatrix}-u\dot{y}-v\dot{x}&0\\
0&-u\dot{y}-v\dot{x}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}x_{0}+\begin{pmatrix}u\dot{x}-v\dot{y}&0\\
0&u\dot{x}-v\dot{y}\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{d}y_{0}\ .

Now, let us define the following matrix of smooth differential operators

(4.18)

\displaystyle\Lambda:T_{(J,A)}

\displaystyle D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\longrightarrow\Omega^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)\oplus\Omega^{2}\big(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})\big)

\displaystyle(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})\longmapsto\big((L_{1}+iL_{2})(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0}),S(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})\big)

where

\displaystyle L_{1}(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0}):=\mathrm{d}\bigg(\mathrm{div}_{g}\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\bigg)\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)\ ,

\displaystyle L_{2}(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0}):=\mathrm{d}\bigg(\mathrm{div}_{g}\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)\circ J+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}_{0}\circ J+\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{\bullet}A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\bigg)\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)\ ,

\displaystyle S(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\cdot,\cdot)-J(\divr\dot{J}\wedge A)(\cdot,\cdot)\in\Omega^{2}\big(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})\big)\ .

It is possible to define the principal symbol of a matrix of mixed-order differential operators as the matrix obtained by taking the principal symbols of each differential operator. The corresponding system of PDEs is called
elliptic
, if the symbol matrix has non-zero determinant (see
[
ADN64
]
and
[
Gru77
]
for more details).

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
(J,A)
be an arbitrary point in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. Then, for any
p\in\Sigma
and for any
0\neq\xi\in T^{*}\Sigma
, the symbol matrix
\sigma\big(\Lambda\big)_{p}(\xi)
has non-zero determinant.

Proof.

Let
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
be a
g_{J}
-orthonormal basis and let
\{e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}\}
be the dual basis, so that
\xi=\xi_{1}e_{1}^{*}+\xi_{2}e_{2}^{*}
. We first note that
\sigma(\Lambda)_{p}(\xi)
is a
4\times 4
matrix as any term in
\Lambda
, involving the tensors
(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})
, can be written in the coordinates
(\dot{x},\dot{y},\dot{u},\dot{v})
, for what explained above. Moreover, we have the following decomposition:

(4.19)

\sigma\big(\Lambda\big)_{p}(\xi)=\begin{pmatrix}\Theta&\Xi\\
\Gamma&\Delta\end{pmatrix}\ ,

where each block is a
2\times 2
matrix, and each entry in the first and second block-row is a homogeneous polynomial in
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
of degree two and one, respectively. After a fairly long computation in coordinates the final expression for
\sigma(\Lambda)_{p}(\xi)
is

\begin{pmatrix}-2(f-1)\xi_{1}\xi_{2}&(f-1)(\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2})+\frac{3}{2}|w|^{2}f^{\prime}|\xi|^{2}&-f^{\prime}u(\xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2})&-f^{\prime}v|\xi|^{2}\\
(f-1)(\xi_{2}^{2}-\xi_{1}^{2})-\frac{3}{2}|w|^{2}f^{\prime}|\xi|^{2}&-2(f-1)\xi_{1}\xi_{2}&-f^{\prime}v|\xi|^{2}&f^{\prime}u|\xi|^{2}\\
-3u\xi_{1}&-3v\xi_{1}&-\xi_{2}&\xi_{1}\\
-3v\xi_{1}&3u\xi_{1}&-\xi_{1}&-\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

where
|\xi|^{2}:=\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}
and the second column corresponds to the coefficient of
-\dot{y}
. We only show how to get block
\Theta
, as with a similar calculation one can obtain the remaining ones. To write down explicitly each entry of
\Theta
, we need to compute the principal symbol of
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, along directions
(\dot{x},-\dot{y},\dot{u},\dot{v})
with
\dot{u}=\dot{v}=0
. According to Lemma
4.13
,
\sigma_{2}(L_{1})
and
\sigma_{2}(L_{2})
depend, respectively, on
(f-1)\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})+\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\mathrm{d}\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle
and on
(f-1)\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\circ J)-\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\mathrm{d}\langle A,(\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0})J\rangle
. In particular,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(\xi,\xi)=\dot{x}(-2\xi_{1}\xi_{2})-\dot{y}(\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2})\ ,\qquad\mathrm{d}\big(\langle A,\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\big)(\xi,\xi)=-6|w|^{2}(\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2})\dot{y}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\circ J)(\xi,\xi)=\dot{x}(\xi_{2}^{2}-\xi_{1}^{2})-\dot{y}(-2\xi_{1}\xi_{2})\ ,\qquad\mathrm{d}\big(\langle A,(\nabla_{J\bullet}\dot{A}_{0})J\rangle\big)(\xi,\xi)=6|w|^{2}(\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2})\dot{x}\ ,

where all the above equality are to be intended up to lower order terms in
\xi
. In the end, the upper left block in
\sigma(\Lambda)_{p}(\xi)
is given by:

\Theta=\begin{pmatrix}-2(f-1)\xi_{1}\xi_{2}&(f-1)(\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2})+\frac{3}{2}|w|^{2}f^{\prime}|\xi|^{2}\\
(f-1)(\xi_{2}^{2}-\xi_{1}^{2})-\frac{3}{2}|w|^{2}f^{\prime}|\xi|^{2}&-2(f-1)\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ .

If
\xi\neq 0
, then the matrix
\Delta
is invertible as its determinant is equal to
|\xi|^{2}
. This allows us to use the determinant formula of block matrices to obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}\big(\sigma(\Lambda)_{p}(\xi)\big)

\displaystyle=|\xi|^{2}\mathrm{det}\big(\Theta-\Xi\Delta^{-1}\Gamma\big)

\displaystyle=|\xi|^{2}\Big(4\xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\big(1-f+\frac{3}{2}f^{\prime}|w|^{2}\big)^{2}+(\xi_{1}^{2}-\xi_{2}^{2})^{2}\big(1-f+\frac{3}{2}f^{\prime}|w|^{2}\big)^{2}\Big)\ .

Since
1-f+\frac{3}{2}f^{\prime}|w|^{2}
is strictly positive (Lemma
2.19
), requiring that last expression vanishes is equivalent to the conditions
\xi_{1}\xi_{2}=0
and
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
, which clearly is possible if and only if
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=0
.
∎

Theorem E
.

Let
(J,A)
be a point in
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, then

\mathrm{dim}W_{(J,A)}\geq 16g-16+2g\ .

Proof.

Notice that, the space
W_{(J,A)}
can be seen as the kernel of
\Lambda
, namely the matrix of smooth differential operators defined in (
4.18
). Let us consider the deformation
tA
, for some
t\in[0,1]
, and look at the corresponding smooth
1
-parameter family of matrices of differential operators:

\displaystyle\big\{\Lambda_{t}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}:T_{(J,tA)}

\displaystyle D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\to\Omega^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)\oplus\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J}))

\displaystyle(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})\longmapsto(D_{t}(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0}),S_{t}(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0}))\ ,

\displaystyle D_{t}(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0}):=

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bigg(\mathrm{div}_{g}\big((f_{t}-1)\dot{J}\big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}_{t}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}_{t}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}tA)J,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\bigg)

\displaystyle+i\mathrm{d}\bigg(\mathrm{div}_{g}\big((f_{t}-1)\dot{J}\circ J\big)+\mathrm{d}(\dot{f}_{0})_{t}\circ J+\frac{f^{\prime}_{t}}{6}\langle\nabla_{\bullet}tA,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\bigg)\ ,

S_{t}(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0}):=\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\cdot,\cdot)-tJ(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\wedge A)(\cdot,\cdot)\ ,

where
f_{t}:=f\big(t^{2}||A||_{0}^{2}\big),\dot{f}_{t}=t\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}}{4}\langle\dot{A}_{0},A\rangle
, and
(\dot{f}_{0})_{t}=-t\frac{f_{t}^{\prime}}{4}\langle\dot{A}_{0}J,A\rangle
. Observe that the matrix
\Lambda_{t}
is elliptic for any
t\in[0,1]
(in the sense explained above) as Lemma
4.14
holds for any
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. In particular, since
\Sigma
is closed the operator matrix
\Lambda_{t}
has a well-defined index for any
t\in[0,1]
. By definition,
\Lambda_{0}
associates (up to a sign), to each
(\dot{J},\dot{A}_{0})
, the element

\big(\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\big)+i\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\circ J\big),\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}\big)\ .

The homotopy invariance of the Fredholm index (see
[
Nic20
]
for example) implies the following chain of equalities:

\mathrm{ind}\big(\Lambda\big)=\mathrm{ind}\big(\Lambda_{1}\big)=\mathrm{ind}\big(\Lambda_{t}\big)=\mathrm{ind}\big(\Lambda_{0}\big)\ .

Since the differential equations obtained from the kernel of the matrix
\Lambda_{0}
are decoupled in
\dot{J}
and
\dot{A}_{0}
, we have the following index decomposition:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}\big(\Lambda_{0}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ind}\big(\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\cdot)+i\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\circ J)\big)+\mathrm{ind}\big(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ind}\big(\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\cdot)+i\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\circ J)\big)+10g-10\ .

where in the last step we used Lemma
4.12
. It is well-known (see
[
Tro12
]
for example) that the divergence operator
\mathrm{div}_{g}:T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\to\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)
is surjective and its kernel has real dimension equal to
6g-6
. In particular, for any
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)
there exists
\dot{J}\in T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
such that
\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}=\alpha
. Any such real
1
-form has a decomposition
\alpha=\alpha^{1,0}+\alpha^{0,1}
, with
\widebar{\alpha^{0,1}}=\alpha^{1,0}
. Thus,

\alpha+i\alpha\circ J=\alpha^{1,0}+\alpha^{0,1}+i\big(i\alpha^{1,0}-i\alpha^{0,1}\big)=2\alpha^{0,1}\ .

According to this last identity and the surjectivity of the divergence operator, it follows that the cokernel of
\mathrm{d}\big((\mathrm{div}_{g}\cdot)+i(\mathrm{div}_{g}\circ J)\big)
is isomorphic to

\mathrm{Coker}\big(\partial:\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\Omega^{1,1}(\Sigma)\big)\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\Omega^{1,1}(\Sigma)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathrm{Im}(\partial)$}\right.}=H^{1,1}_{\partial}(\Sigma)\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}\ ,

as there are no
(0,2)
-forms on
(\Sigma,J)
. In addition, the kernel of
\mathrm{d}\big((\mathrm{div}_{g}\cdot)+i(\mathrm{div}_{g}\circ J)\big)
is given by

\{\dot{J}\in T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)\ |\ \partial\big((\mathrm{div}_{g}\cdot)+i(\mathrm{div}_{g}\circ J)\big)=0\}\cong H^{0,1}_{\partial}(\Sigma)\times\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\cdot)\cong\mathbb{R}^{6g-6}\times\mathbb{R}^{2g}

using again the surjectivity of the divergence operator. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{ind}(\Lambda_{0})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ind}\big(\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\cdot)+i\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{div}_{g}\circ J)\big)+10g-10

\displaystyle=6g-6+2g-2+10g-10=16g-16+2g-2.

To conclude, we notice that all operators
D_{t}
take value into the subspace of complex exact
2
-forms, hence the dimension of the cokernel of
\Lambda_{t}
is at least equal to the dimension of
H^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}(\Sigma)\cong\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{dim}W_{(J,A)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{Ker}(\Lambda_{1}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ind}(\Lambda_{1})+\mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{Coker}(\Lambda_{1}))

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{ind}(\Lambda_{0})+2

\displaystyle=16g-16+2g-2+2=16g-16+2g\ .

∎

4.4.
The preferred subspace inside the tangent to
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)

In this section we prove Theorem
F
by using the theory developed so far. In particular, in Lemma
4.15
and Lemma
4.17
we prove the
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
and
\mathbf{I}
invariance of
W_{(J,A)}
, respectively. Then, we find a formula for the action of the almost-complex structure
\mathbf{I}
on tangent vectors to the
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
-orbit (Lemma
4.18
) and we study the operator associated with the first equation in (
HS
) (Lemma
4.19
). Finally, if
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\to\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
denotes the quotient projection, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is the quotient of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
by
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
, the injectivity of the map
\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}\pi:W_{(J,A)}\to T_{[J,A]}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is proven in Lemma
4.21
by using all the previous results. The only part of Theorem
F
that is left is the inclusion
W_{(J,A)}\subset T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, as it is necessary to explain first the connection between the system of differential equations (
1.2
) and the theory of symplectic reduction. For this reason its discussion is postponed to Section
5.3
. The results presented in this section follow closely the ones given for the anti-de Sitter case (
[
MST21
, §4.5]
), even though one of the two tensors we work with is of a different type.

Lemma 4.15
.

For every symplectomorphism
\phi
of
(\Sigma,\rho)
and for every
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in W_{(J,A)}
, we have
(\phi^{*}\dot{J},\phi^{*}\dot{A})\in W_{(\phi^{*}J,\phi^{*}A)}
. In other words, the distribution
\{W_{(J,A)}\}_{(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)}
is invariant under the action of
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
.

Proof.

The assumption that
\phi
is a symplectomorphism (
\phi^{*}\rho=\rho
) is crucial in order to prove that
g_{\phi^{*}J}
, the metric associated with the area form
\rho
and complex structure
\phi^{*}J
, is equal to the pull-back metric
\phi^{*}g_{J}=\phi^{*}\big(\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)\big)
. In other words, we are saying that
\phi:(\Sigma,g_{\phi^{*}J})\to(\Sigma,g_{J})
, is an isometry. In particular, for any endomorphism of the tangent bundle
B
we get

\phi^{*}\Big(\mathrm{div}_{g}B\Big)=\mathrm{div}_{\phi^{*}g}(\phi^{*}B)\ .

Moreover, the parts involving the scalar product between tangent vectors
\dot{J},\dot{J}^{\prime}\in T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
and
\dot{A},\dot{A}^{\prime}
are preserved by
\phi
(see Section
3.2
and Section
4.1
). As for the rest of the terms in the equations defining
W_{(J,A)}
, we see that they are preserved by
\phi
using the naturality of the action and the functoriality of the involved operators, such as the induced connection
\nabla
and the exterior covariant derivative
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}
on End
{}_{0}(\Sigma,g)
-valued
1
-form.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

Notice that the above lemma holds for any symplectomorphism
\phi
not necessarily Hamiltonian. This is a stronger result than what we need in Theorem
F
. In particular, with the same argument it is possible to prove the
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
-invariance of
\mathbf{I}
and
\mathbf{g}_{f}
.

Lemma 4.17
.

For any
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, the subspace
W_{(J,A)}
is preserved by
\mathbf{I}
.

Proof.

Recall that by definition
\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})=(-J\dot{J},-\dot{A}J-A\dot{J})=:(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})
. We only need to show that the pair
(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})
still satisfies the equations defining
W_{(J,A)}
. In fact,

(rel. (
3.14
))

\displaystyle(f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}(\dot{J}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}\big(-J\dot{J}\big)

\displaystyle=(f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\circ J\ .

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\langle\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime},A\rangle\Big)\circ J=\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\langle-\dot{A}_{0}J,A\rangle\Big)\circ J\ ,

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}\rangle=-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J,-\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle=\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{\bullet}A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\ ,

where in the last step we used relation (
4.4
). By using
\mathbf{I}^{2}=-\mathds{1}
, it follows that the element
\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})
satisfies the second equation in (
1.2
) as well. Regarding the last equation, notice that, according to Remark
4.8
, it is equivalent to
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,J\bullet)=\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}(\bullet)A(J\bullet)-\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}(J\bullet)A(\bullet)
. Therefore, for any
X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\big(\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}\big)(X,JX)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\big(\dot{A}_{0}J\big)(X,JX)

(
\nabla_{\bullet}J=0
)

\displaystyle=-\big(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}\big)(X,JX)J

(
A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J
)

\displaystyle=-(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(X)A(JX)J+(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(JX)A(X)J

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(X)A(X)+\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}(JX)A(X)J\ .

On the other hand,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}^{\prime})(X)A(JX)-(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}^{\prime})(JX)A(X)

\displaystyle=-(\mathrm{div}_{g}J\dot{J})(X)A(JX)+(\mathrm{div}_{g}J\dot{J})(JX)A(X)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(JX)A(JX)+(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(X)A(X)

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(JX)A(X)J+(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(X)A(X)\ ,

where we used relation (
3.14
) on the first step and
A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J
on the second one.
∎

Lemma 4.18
.

For every symplectic vector field
X
on
(\Sigma,\rho)
and for every
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, with
C(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)=g_{J}\big(A(\cdot)\cdot,\cdot\big)
equal to the real part of a holomorphic cubic differential on
(\Sigma,J)
, we have
\mathbf{I}\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)=\big(-\mathcal{L}_{JX}J,-g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{JX}C\big)
.

Proof.

For any vector field
V
on the surface, let use define the operator
M_{V}:\Gamma(T\Sigma)\to\Gamma(T\Sigma)
as
M_{V}(Y):=\nabla^{g}_{Y}V
, where
\nabla^{g}
is the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g\equiv g_{J}=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
. Then, for any
Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{V}J)Y

\displaystyle=[V,JY]-J([V,Y])

(
\nabla^{g}
is torsion-free)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g}_{V}(JY)-\nabla^{g}_{JY}V-J(\nabla^{g}_{V}Y)+J(\nabla^{g}_{Y}V)

(
\nabla^{g}_{\bullet}J=0
)

\displaystyle=J(\nabla^{g}_{V}Y)-M_{V}(JY)-J(\nabla^{g}_{V}Y)+JM_{V}(Y)

\displaystyle=(JM_{V}-M_{V}J)(Y)\ .

The above computation implies that

(4.20)

\mathcal{L}_{V}J=JM_{V}-M_{V}J\ .

Now since
C(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
is a
(0,3)
-tensor, for any
Y,Z,U\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
, its Lie derivative can be computed as follows

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{V}C)(Y,Z,U)=V\cdot C(Y,Z,U)-C([V,Y],Z,U)-C(Y,[V,Z],U)-C(Y,Z,[V,U])\ .

Moreover, using the relation

V\cdot C(Y,Z,U)=(\nabla^{g}_{V}C)(Y,Z,U)+C(\nabla^{g}_{V}Y,Z,U)+C(Y,\nabla^{g}_{V}Z,U)+C(Y,Z\nabla_{V}^{g}U)\ ,

we obtain that

(4.21)

(\mathcal{L}_{V}C)(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)=(\nabla^{g}_{V}C)(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)+C(M_{V}\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)+C(\cdot,M_{V}\cdot,\cdot)+C(\cdot,\cdot,M_{V}\cdot)\ .

In particular, by re-writing the last relation using the associated
(1,2)
-tensor defined as
A=g^{-1}C
and using the compatibility between
\nabla^{g}
and the metric
g
, we get

(4.22)

(g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C)(\cdot)=(\nabla^{g}_{V}A)(\cdot)+A(M_{V}\cdot)+A(\cdot)M_{V}+M_{V}^{*}A(\cdot)\ ,

where
M_{V}^{*}
denotes the
g
-adjoint operator of
M_{V}
. Now let us apply the almost-complex structure
\mathbf{I}
to the pair
(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C)
with
X
a
\rho
-symplectic vector field. Therefore,

\mathbf{I}\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)=\big(-J\mathcal{L}_{X}J,-(g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C)(\cdot)J-A(\cdot)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\big)\ .

Since
J
is
\nabla^{g}
-parallel then
M_{JX}=JM_{X}
, so that the first component of
\mathbf{I}\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)
is given by

(rel. (
4.20
) for
V=X
)

\displaystyle-J\mathcal{L}_{X}J

\displaystyle=-J(JM_{X}-M_{X}J)

\displaystyle=-(JM_{JX}-M_{JX}J)

(rel. (
4.20
) for
V=JX
)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{L}_{JX}J\ .

Regarding the second component of
\mathbf{I}\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)
, using relation (
4.20
) and (
4.22
) for
V=X
, we have

\displaystyle-(g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C)(\cdot)J-A(\cdot)\mathcal{L}_{X}J

\displaystyle=-(\nabla_{X}^{g}A)(\cdot)J-A(M_{X}\cdot)J-M^{*}_{X}A(\cdot)J-A(\cdot)M_{X}J

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -A(\cdot)JM_{X}+A(\cdot)M_{X}J

\displaystyle=-(\nabla_{X}^{g}A)(\cdot)J-A(M_{X}\cdot)J-A(\cdot)JM_{X}+M^{*}_{X}JA(\cdot)\ ,

where in the last equality we used
A(\cdot)J=-JA(\cdot)
. On the other hand, using relation (
4.22
) with
V=JX
, we get

\displaystyle-(g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{JX}C)(\cdot)

\displaystyle=-(\nabla_{JX}^{g}A)(\cdot)-A(M_{JX}\cdot)-A(\cdot)M_{JX}-M^{*}_{JX}A(\cdot)

(
M_{JX}=JM_{X}
)

\displaystyle=-(\nabla_{JX}^{g}A)(\cdot)-A(JM_{X}\cdot)-A(\cdot)JM_{X}-(JM_{X})^{*}A(\cdot)

(Theorem
2.7
)

\displaystyle=-(\nabla_{X}^{g}A)(J\cdot)-A(JM_{X}\cdot)-A(\cdot)JM_{X}-(JM_{X})^{*}A(\cdot)

\displaystyle=-(\nabla_{X}^{g}A)(J\cdot)-A(M_{X}\cdot)J-A(\cdot)JM_{X}+M_{X}^{*}JA(\cdot)\ ,

where in the last step we used
A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J
and
J^{*}=-J
. Finally, we conclude by observing that

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g}_{X}A)(JY)Z

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g}_{X}\big(A(JY)Z\big)-A\big(\nabla^{g}_{X}(JY)\big)Z-A(JY)\nabla^{g}_{X}Z

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g}_{X}\big(A(Y)JZ\big)-A\big(J\nabla^{g}_{X}Y\big)Z-A(Y)J\nabla^{g}_{X}Z

\displaystyle=\nabla^{g}_{X}\big(A(Y)JZ\big)-A\big(\nabla^{g}_{X}Y\big)JZ-A(Y)\nabla^{g}_{X}(JZ)

\displaystyle=(\nabla^{g}_{X}A)(Y)JZ,\qquad\forall X,Y,Z\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)\ .

∎

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
G:D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\to C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
be the operator defined as
G(J,A):=K_{h}+1-||q||_{h}^{2}
, where
h
is the metric in the conformal class of
g_{J}
with conformal factor
e^{F}
(see (
2.12
)), and
q
is a cubic differential whose real part is equal to
C=g_{J}A
. Suppose that
(J,A)
satisfies equations
\mathrm{(\ref{Gausscodazzi})}
and let
U
be a vector field on
\Sigma
. Then,

\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}G\big(\mathcal{L}_{U}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C\big)=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}\lambda+(1+2||q||_{h}^{2})\lambda\ ,

where
\lambda:=\mathrm{div}_{g_{J}}U\bigg(\frac{3}{2}||q||_{g_{J}}^{2}F^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||q||^{2}_{g_{J}}}{2}\Big)-1\bigg)
. In particular, if the element
\big(\mathcal{L}_{U}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C\big)
belongs to the kernel of
\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}G
, then
U
is symplectic.

Proof.

Let us denote with
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
the flow of
U
, and let
(J,C)
be a point in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. Consider the path
\{(J_{t},C_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}\subset D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
given by
(J_{t},C_{t})=(\psi^{*}_{t}J,\psi^{*}_{t}C)
so that
(J_{0},C_{0})=(J,C)
. In particular,

\mathcal{L}_{U}J=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\psi^{*}_{t}J\Big|_{t=0},\quad g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C=g^{-1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\psi^{*}_{t}C\Big|_{t=0},\quad g\equiv g_{J}\ .

The final goal will be to compute
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}G(J_{t},C_{t})|_{t=0}
. Let us first determine the Riemannian metric
g_{t}:=\rho(\cdot,J_{t}\cdot)
, where
J_{t}=\mathrm{d}\psi^{-1}_{t}\circ J\circ\mathrm{d}\psi_{t}
.

\displaystyle g_{t}

\displaystyle=\rho\big(\cdot,(\mathrm{d}\psi^{-1}_{t}\circ J\circ\mathrm{d}\psi_{t})\cdot\big)=\rho\big((\mathrm{d}\psi^{-1}_{t}\circ\mathrm{d}\psi_{t})\cdot,(\mathrm{d}\psi^{-1}_{t}\circ J\circ\mathrm{d}\psi_{t})\cdot\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{d}\psi^{-1}_{t})\circ\psi_{t}\big)\rho\big(\mathrm{d}\psi_{t}\cdot,(J\circ\mathrm{d}\psi_{t})\cdot\big)=\big(\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{d}\psi^{-1}_{t})\circ\psi_{t}\big)g\big(\mathrm{d}\psi_{t}\cdot,\mathrm{d}\psi_{t}\cdot\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{d}\psi^{-1}_{t})\circ\psi_{t}\big)\psi_{t}^{*}g\ .

In particular,
g_{t}
is conformal to
\psi^{*}_{t}g
with conformal factor given by
u_{t}:=\big(\mathrm{det}(\mathrm{d}\psi^{-1}_{t})\circ\psi_{t}\big)
. Now let
F:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be the function defined in Lemma
2.18
and consider the conformal change of metric
h=e^{F}g
, where
F
is evaluated at
||q||_{g}^{2}
divided by
2
. The next step is to determine the Riemannian metric

h_{t}:=e^{F\Big(\frac{||q_{t}||_{g_{t}}^{2}}{2}\Big)}g_{t}=e^{F\Big(\frac{||q_{t}||_{g_{t}}^{2}}{2}\Big)}u_{t}\cdot\psi_{t}^{*}g\ ,

where
q_{t}
is the
J_{t}
-holomorphic cubic differential whose real part is equal to
C_{t}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle h_{t}

\displaystyle=e^{F\Big(\frac{||q_{t}||_{g_{t}}^{2}}{2}\Big)}u_{t}\cdot\psi_{t}^{*}g=e^{F\Big(\frac{||q_{t}||^{2}_{g_{t}}}{2}\Big)}u_{t}\cdot e^{-F\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}\circ\psi_{t}}{2}\Big)}\psi^{*}_{t}h

\displaystyle=e^{F\Big(\frac{||q_{t}||^{2}_{g_{t}}}{2}\Big)-F\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}\circ\psi_{t}}{2}\Big)}u_{t}\cdot\psi_{t}^{*}h=v_{t}\cdot\psi_{t}^{*}h\ .

Again, the metric
h_{t}
is conformal to
\psi_{t}^{*}h
with conformal factor
v_{t}
(notice that
v_{0}=u_{0}\equiv 1
). Using the formula of curvature by conformal change of metric, we get

\displaystyle K_{h_{t}}

\displaystyle=K_{v_{t}\psi_{t}^{*}h}

\displaystyle=v_{t}^{-1}\Big(K_{\psi_{t}^{*}h}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{\psi_{t}^{*}h}\ln v_{t}\Big)

\displaystyle=v_{t}^{-1}\Big(K_{h}\circ\psi_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\big(\Delta_{h}\ln(v_t\circ\psi_t^{-1})\big)\circ\psi_{t}\Big)\ ,

where in the last equality we used the functoriality of the Gaussian curvature and of the Laplacian, namely

K_{\psi_{t}^{*}h}=\psi_{t}^{*}(K_{h}),\quad\Delta_{\psi_{t}^{*}h}\ln v_{t}=\psi_{t}^{*}\Big(\Delta_{h}\ln(v_t\circ\psi_t^{-1})\Big)\ .

The last term we need to determine in
G(J_{t},C_{t})
is the one involving the norm of the cubic differential
q_{t}
.

(4.23)

||q_{t}||_{h_{t}}^{2}=||q_{t}||_{v_{t}\psi_{t}^{*}h}^{2}=v_{t}^{-3}||\psi_{t}^{*}q||_{\psi_{t}^{*}h}^{2}=v_{t}^{-3}||q||_{h}^{2}\circ\psi_{t}\ .

We can finally deduce an expression for the term

K_{h_{t}}-||q_{t}||_{h_{t}}^{2}=v_{t}^{-1}\Big(K_{h}\circ\psi_{t}-\frac{1}{2}\big(\Delta_{h}\ln(v_t\circ\psi_t^{-1})\big)\circ\psi_{t}-v_{t}^{-2}||q||_{h}^{2}\circ\psi_{t}\Big)\ ,

and compute the first order variation of operator
G
along the path
t\mapsto(J_{t},C_{t})
, obtaining

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big(K_{h_{t}}-||q||_{h_{t}}^{2}+1\big)\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=-\dot{v}\big(K_{h}-||q||_{h}^{2}\big)+U(K_{h})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}\dot{v}+2\dot{v}||q||_{h}^{2}-U\big(||q||_{h}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\dot{v}\big(1+2||q||_{h}^{2}\big)-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}\dot{v}\ ,

where in the last line we used that
(J,C)
satisfies
G(J,C)=0
. At this point, it only remains to compute
\dot{v}
, i.e. the first order variation of
v_{t}

(
v_{0}\equiv 1
)

\displaystyle\dot{v}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}v_{t}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}=\frac{\mathrm{d}(v_{t}\circ\psi_{t}^{-1})}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}e^{F\Big(\frac{||q_{t}||^{2}_{g_{t}}\circ\psi_{t}^{-1}}{2}\Big)-F\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\Big)}u_{t}\circ\psi_{t}^{-1}\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\dot{u}+\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\Big)\frac{\mathrm{d}\big(||q_{t}||_{g_{t}}^{2}\circ\psi_{t}^{-1}\big)}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\ .

By imitating the steps performed for relation (
4.23
), we deduce that

||q_{t}||_{g_{t}}^{2}=u_{t}^{-3}||q||^{2}_{g}\circ\psi_{t}\ .

Since
(\psi_{t})
represents the flow of
U
, the first order variation of the conformal factor
u_{t}
is given by

\dot{u}=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(\det(\mathrm d\psi_t^{-1})\circ\psi_{t}\Big)\Big|_{t=0}=-\mathrm{div}_{g}U\ .

To conclude, we have

\displaystyle\dot{v}

\displaystyle=\dot{u}-\frac{3}{2}F^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\Big)\dot{u}||q||_{g}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{div}_{g}U\Big(\frac{3}{2}F^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\Big)||q||_{g}^{2}-1\Big)\ ,

hence the first order variation of operator
G
along the path
t\mapsto(J_{t},C_{t})
is

\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}G\big(\mathcal{L}_{U}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C\big)=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}\lambda+(1+2||q||_{h}^{2})\lambda,\quad\lambda:=\mathrm{div}_{g}U\Big(\frac{3}{2}F^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\Big)||q||_{g}^{2}-1\Big)\ .

Regarding the second part of the statement, observe that the following inequality holds

T(\lambda):=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}\lambda+(1+2||q||_{h}^{2})\lambda\geq-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{h}\lambda+\lambda=:S(\lambda)\ .

Since the linear operator
S
is known to be self-adjoint and positive, hence injective, over
L^{2}(\Sigma,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{a}_{h})
, so is the linear operator
T
. Therefore, if
(\mathcal{L}_{U}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C)
lies inside the kernel of
\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}G
, then the function
\lambda=\mathrm{div}_{g}U\Big(\frac{3}{2}F^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\Big)||q||_{g}^{2}-1\Big)
is send to
0
by the operator
T
. At this point, we would conclude by saying that the divergence of the vector field
U
is zero (see (
3.15
)), which is obviously true if
A=0
, i.e.
q=0
. As for the first order variation of the operator
G
at points where
q\neq 0
, using Lemma
2.19
with
t=\frac{||q||_{J}^{2}}{2}
, we get that the function
\frac{3}{2}F^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\Big)||q||_{g}^{2}-1
is strictly negative. In particular,
\mathrm{div}_{g}U\Big(\frac{3}{2}F^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||q||_{g}^{2}}{2}\Big)||q||_{g}^{2}-1\Big)
is zero if and only if
\mathrm{div}_{g}U=0
.
∎

Remark 4.20
.

In order to conclude the proof of the Theorem
F
, one of the remaining results to show is the inclusion of the subspace
W_{(J,A)}
inside the tangent space to the infinite-dimensional space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
. In order to show this inclusion, it is necessary to explain how the differential equations defining
W_{(J,A)}
are related to the process of infinite-dimensional symplectic reduction. In view not to overextending the discussion too much, during the proof of the last lemma that follows, we will use a result presented and proved in Section
5.3
.

Lemma 4.21
.

For every
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, we have

W_{(J,A)}\cap T_{(J,A)}\Big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\Big)=\{0\}\ .

In particular, the natural quotient projection
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\to\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
induces a linear isomorphism

\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}\pi:W_{(J,A)}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}T_{[J,A]}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)\ .

Proof.

Let
X
be a Hamiltonian vector field on
\Sigma
with Hamiltonian function
H
, and suppose that
(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C)
belongs to
W_{(J,A)}
. Thus, according to Lemma
4.18
and the
\mathbf{I}
-invariance of
W_{(J,A)}
, the same has to hold for
\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C)=(-\mathcal{L}_{JX}J,-g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{JX}C)
. Since
W_{(J,A)}
is contained in
T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
(see Proposition
5.15
), the differential of operator
G
considered in Lemma
4.19
has to send the pair
(-\mathcal{L}_{JX}J,-g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{JX}C)
to zero. By the second part of Lemma
4.19
, we deduce that
JX
is
\rho
-symplectic, namely
\mathrm{d}(\iota_{JX})=0
. This implies that the
1
-form
-\mathrm{d}H\circ J=-(\iota_{X}\rho)\circ J=\iota_{JX}\rho
is closed, and therefore the function
H
is
g
-harmonic (since
\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{d}H\circ J\big)=-\Delta_{g}H\rho
). The only harmonic functions on a closed manifold are the constants, hence we deduce that the vector field
X
is equal to zero, which proves the first part of the statement. Regarding the second one, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
be the quotient of the infinite-dimensional space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
by the group
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
and consider the quotient projection
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\to\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
. By definition, the kernel of
\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}\pi
coincides with
T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)
. Hence, by the first part of the statement, the map
\mathrm{d}_{(J,A)}\pi
is injective. Moreover, since
\dim W_{(J,A)}\geq 16g-16+2g
(Theorem
E
) and
\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)=16g-16+2g
, this is actually an isomorphism.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

The above lemma shows a major difference with the
\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
case (
[
MST21
, Lemma 4.21]
), where the authors were able to obtain a similar result for the group of symplectomorphisms of the surface not necessarily Hamiltonian. This forces us to perform an additional (finite-dimensional) quotient to obtain the Hitchin component, and thus produces additional analytical difficulties carried out in Section
6.1
.

4.5.
The circle action on
\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}(\Sigma)

Recall that the space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
consists of pairs
(J,A)
, where
J
is an almost-complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
A
is a
1
-form with values in the trace-less and
g_{J}^{0}
-symmetric endomorphisms bundle of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J
and
A(X)Y=A(Y)X,\ \forall X,Y\in T\mathbb{R}^{2}
(see Section
4.1
for more details). In particular, there is
\text{MCG}(T^{2})\cong\SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
-equivariant isomorphism between
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and the holomorphic vector bundle
Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}(T^{2})\big)
of cubic differentials over Teichmüller space of the torus (
[
RT24
, Corollary 3.3]
). In fact, if
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
then the
(0,3)
-tensor
C(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)=g_{J}^{0}\big(A(\cdot)\cdot,\cdot\big)
is the real part of a
J
-holomorphic cubic differential
q
on
(T^{2},J)
. The natural
S^{1}
-action on
Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}(T^{2})\big)
given by
(J,q)\mapsto(J,e^{-i\theta}q)
, can be induced on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and results in the following formula

\displaystyle\widehat{\Psi}_{\theta}\ :

\displaystyle\ D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\longrightarrow D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))

\displaystyle(J,A)\mapsto(J,\cos\theta A(\cdot)-\sin\theta A(\cdot)J)\ .\

It is clear from the definition that
\widehat{\Psi}_{\theta}
preserves the
0
-section in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
(seen as a vector bundle over
\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cong\mathcal{T}(T^{2})
), hence it induces an
S^{1}
-action on
\mathcal{B}_{0}(T^{2})
which will still be denoted by
\widehat{\Psi}_{\theta}
by abuse of notation.

Lemma 4.23

(
[
RT24
]
)
.

The
S^{1}
-action on
\mathcal{B}_{0}(T^{2})
is Hamiltonian with respect to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}
and it satisfies

\widehat{\Psi}_{\theta}^{*}\widehat{\mathbf{g}}_{f}=\widehat{\mathbf{g}}_{f}\ ,

for any choice of a smooth function
f:[0,+\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
such that:
f(0)=0,f^{\prime}(t)<0
for any
t>0
and
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}f(t)=-\infty
. Moreover, the Hamiltonian function is given by

\widehat{H}\big(J,A\big)=\frac{2}{3}f\Big(\frac{||q||^{2}_{J}}{2}\Big)\ ,

where
q
is the
J
-holomorphic cubic differential whose real part is equal to
C=g_{J}A
.

Moving on to the case of genus
g\geq 2
, we still have an
S^{1}
-action on
Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)\big)
given by
([J],q)\mapsto([J],e^{-i\theta}q)
, which can be induced on the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component using the parametrization

\Phi:\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}Q^{3}\big(\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{c}}(\Sigma)\big)

found by Labourie and Loftin (see Section
2.4
). Thanks to Proposition
2.9
and to the construction explained in Section
2.5
, we know that
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
is diffeomorphic to the following space

\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left\{(J,C)\ \Bigg|\ \parbox{190.00029pt}{$J$ is an (almost) complex structure on $\Sigma$ \\
$C$ is the real part of a $J$-cubic differential \\
$\big(h:=e^{F\big(\frac{||q||^{2}_{g_{J}}}{2}\big)}g_{J},A:=g_{J}^{-1}C\big)$ satisfy (\ref{Gausscodazzi})}\right\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\Symp_{0}(\Sigma)$}\right.}\ ,

where
F:[0,+\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is the smooth function defined in Lemma
2.18
. In particular, we can then describe the induced
S^{1}
-action by the following formula:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\theta}\ :

\displaystyle\ \mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)\longrightarrow\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle(J,A)\mapsto(J,\cos\theta A(\cdot)-\sin\theta A(\cdot)J)\ .\

Theorem C
.

Let
\rho
be a fixed area form on
\Sigma
, then the circle action on
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
is Hamiltonian with respect to
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
and it satisfies:

\Psi_{\theta}^{*}\mathbf{g}_{f}=\mathbf{g}_{f},\quad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{R}\ .

The Hamiltonian function is given by:

H(J,q):=\frac{2}{3}\int_{\Sigma}f\Big(\frac{||q||^{2}_{g_{J}}}{2}\Big)\rho,

where
f:[0,+\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
is the smooth function defined by (
2.13
).

The proof of Theorem
C
is simply an adaptation of the proof made in the torus case (
[
RT24
]
and Lemma
4.23
). In fact, as already explained in Remark
4.6
, identities valid for elements in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
can be interpreted as point-wise identities for smooth sections in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, and, according to the construction explained in Section
4.1
, the Hitchin component
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
can be seen a subset of
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
.

5.
The infinite dimensional symplectic reduction

In this section we present the process that led us to the definition of the pseudo-Kähler structure on the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component and the characterization of its tangent space as described in Remark
4.9
. The main theorem we will use was proved by Donaldson (see
[
Don03
]
).

5.1.
Donaldson’s construction

In the following discussion, we present a (much more general) construction done in
[
Don03
, §2]
and adapted to our case of interest.

Since we will be using a lot of notation from Section
4.1
, let us briefly recall the construction of the infinite-dimensional space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. It has been defined as the space of smooth sections of the bundle

P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P\times D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\SL(2,\mathbb{R})$}\right.}\longrightarrow\Sigma\ ,

where
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
acts diagonally on two factors. In particular, each element in
