D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
can be described as a pair
(J,A)
, with
J
an almost-complex structure on
\Sigma
, and
A
a
1
-form with values in the trace-less and
g_{J}
-symmetric endomorphisms of
T\Sigma
such that
A(J\cdot)=A(\cdot)J
and
A(X)Y=A(Y)X,\ \forall X,Y\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
. Moreover, a tangent vector
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
, where
\dot{A}:=g_{J}^{-1}\dot{C}
, at
(J,A)
can be considered as the data of:

•

a section
\dot{J}
of End
(T\Sigma)
such that
\dot{J}J+J\dot{J}=0
, namely
\dot{J}
is a
g_{J}
-symmetric and trace-less endomorphism of
T\Sigma
;

•

an End
(T\Sigma,g_{J})
-valued
1
-form
\dot{A}
such that

\dot{A}=\dot{A}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\tr(JA\dot J)\mathds{1}\ ,

where
\mathds{1}
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix and
\dot{A}_{0}
is the trace-less part of
\dot{A}
. In particular, the trace-part
\dot{A}_{\text{tr}}
of
\dot{A}
is uniquely determined by
\dot{J}
.

Let us denote with
s=(J,A)
an element in
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, and with
\dot{s}
the corresponding tangent vector. Recall there is a natural
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
(see (
4.7
)). Given an
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariant symplectic form
\widehat{\omega}
on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, there is an induced symplectic structure on each vertical subspace of
P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
, denoted with
\widehat{\omega}_{s(x)}
for
x\in\Sigma
. In particular, given two tangent vectors
\dot{s},\dot{s}^{\prime}\in T_{s}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, we can define

(5.1)

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}(\dot{s},\dot{s}^{\prime}):=\int_{\Sigma}\widehat{\omega}_{s}(\dot{s},\dot{s}^{\prime})\rho\ .

This gives rise to a formal symplectic structure on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
which is invariant by the action of
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
. Now, suppose for a moment that the
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
we introduced in (
4.7
) is Hamiltonian with respect to the symplectic form
\widehat{\omega}
, and with moment map
\widehat{\mu}:D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\to\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})^{*}
. Given any section
s\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, the moment map
\widehat{\mu}
induces a section
\widehat{\mu}_{s}
of the bundle End
{}_{0}(T\Sigma)^{*}
. Then, the following result holds

Theorem 5.1

(
[
Don03
, Theorem 9]
)
.

Let
\rho
be an area form on
\Sigma
and let
\nabla
be any torsion-free connection on
T\Sigma
satisfying
\nabla\rho=0
. Define the map
\boldsymbol{\mu}:D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\to\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)
as follows:

\boldsymbol{\mu}(s):=\widehat{\omega}\big(\nabla_{\bullet}s,\nabla_{\bullet}s\big)+\langle\widehat{\mu}_{s}\ |\ R^{\nabla}\rangle-\mathrm{d}\big(c(\nabla_{\bullet}\widehat{\mu}_{s})\big)\ .

Then,

(1)

\boldsymbol{\mu}(s)
is a closed
2
-form for any
s\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
;

(2)

\boldsymbol{\mu}
is equivariant with respect to the action of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
;

(3)

Given a vector field
V\in\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)
, and
\gamma_{V}
a primitive of
\iota_{V}\rho
, the differential of the map

\displaystyle D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle s\mapsto\int_{\Sigma}\gamma_{V}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mu}(s)

equals

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{s}(\dot{s},\mathcal{L}_{V}s)=\int_{\Sigma}\widehat{\omega}_{s}(\dot{s},\mathcal{L}_{V}s)\rho\ .

Before moving on, the meaning of each term in the definition of the moment map must be explained. First notice that
\nabla_{\bullet}s
is a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes s^{*}\big(T^{\text{vert}}P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)\big)
, hence we set
\widehat{\omega}\big(\nabla_{\bullet}s,\nabla_{\bullet}s\big)
to be the
2
-form on
\Sigma
given by:

\widehat{\omega}\big(\nabla_{\bullet}s,\nabla_{\bullet}s\big)(U,V):=\widehat{\omega}\big(\nabla_{U}s,\nabla_{V}s\big),\ \text{for}\ U,V\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)\ ,

where in the RHS of last equality we apply the symplectic form
\widehat{\omega}
on
T^{\text{vert}}P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
and the wedge product on the
1
-form part. Moreover, the covariant derivative
\nabla_{\bullet}\widehat{\mu}_{s}
is a section of
T^{*}\Sigma\otimes\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma)^{*}
and we get a
1
-form by performing the following contraction:

c\big(\nabla_{\bullet}\widehat{\mu}_{s}\big)(v):=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\langle\nabla_{e_{j}}\widehat{\mu}_{s}\ |\ (v\otimes e^{*}_{j})_{0}\rangle,\ \text{for}\ v\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)\ ,

where
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
is a local orthonormal frame of
T\Sigma
and
\{e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}\}
is the associated orthonormal dual frame. Finally, the curvature tensor
R^{\nabla}
of the torsion-free connection
\nabla
is defined as:

R^{\nabla}\big(U,V\big)W:=\nabla_{V}\nabla_{U}W-\nabla_{U}\nabla_{V}W-\nabla_{[V,U]}W\ ,

for any
U,V,W\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
. Because of the anti-symmetry in the first two entries of
R^{\nabla}
, it can be considered as a section of
\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma)
. For this reason we can contract the endomorphism part of
R^{\nabla}
with
\widehat{\mu}_{s}
and obtain the
2
-form on
\Sigma
denoted with
\langle\widehat{\mu}_{s}\ |\ R^{\nabla}\rangle
. Let us recall the following technical result that will be useful later in the construction of our moment map.

Lemma 5.2

(
[
Don03
, Lemma 13]
)
.

There exists a closed
2
-form
\widehat{\omega}_{P(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})))}
on
P\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\big)
such that, for any section
s\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, the following holds:

s^{*}\widehat{\omega}_{P(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2})))}=\widehat{\omega}\big(\nabla_{\bullet}s,\nabla_{\bullet}s\big)+\langle\widehat{\mu}_{s}\ |\ R^{\nabla}\rangle\ .

In particular, since
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is contractible, the de-Rham cohomology class of
\boldsymbol{\mu}(s)
in
H^{2}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
does not depend on the chosen section.

5.2.
The moment map on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))

Before proceeding with the genus
g\geq 2
case, as occurred before, we need to recall some results proved in the torus case. In Section
4.1
we introduced an
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on elements
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, given by

P\cdot(J,A)=(PJP^{-1},PA(P^{-1}\cdot)P^{-1})\ ,

where
A(P^{-1}\cdot)
has to be interpreted as the action of
P\in\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
via pull-back on the
1
-form part of
A
.

Lemma 5.3

(
[
RT24
]
)
.

The
\SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-action on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is Hamiltonian with respect to
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}
with moment map
\widehat{\mu}:D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\to\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})^{*}
given by

(5.2)

\widehat{\mu}_{(J,A)}(X)=\bigg(1-f\Big(\frac{||q||^{2}_{J}}{2}\Big)\bigg)\tr(JX)\ ,

for all
X\in\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R})
and for any choice of a smooth function
f:[0,+\infty)\to(-\infty,0]
such that:
f(0)=0,f^{\prime}(t)<0
for any
t>0
and
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}f(t)=-\infty
. Moreover,
q
is the
J
-holomorphic cubic differential whose real part is equal to
C=g_{J}A
.

Moving on to the genus
g\geq 2
case, in Section
4.1
we defined a (formal) family of pseudo-Kähler structures
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, depending on a choice of a smooth function
f
as in Lemma
5.3
. If
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}
denotes the symplectic form induced on each fibre by an area-preserving isomorphism between
T_{x}\Sigma
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then

(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big)=\int_{\Sigma}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{f}\big((\dot{J},\dot{A}),(\dot{J}^{\prime},\dot{A}^{\prime})\big)\rho

is obtained from relation (
5.1
) by integrating fibre-wise the family of symplectic forms introduced in the torus case. Moreover, according to Donaldson’s construction of previous section, the group
\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
acts on
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
preserving
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
and the action of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
is Hamiltonian.

Theorem 5.4
.

The moment map found by Donaldson for the action of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
on
\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)),\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big)
can be expressed as:

\boldsymbol{\mu}(J,A)=-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)\big(||\bar{\partial}\tau||^{2}-||\partial\tau||^{2}\big)\rho+2K_{J}\bigg(f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}_{J}}{2}\Big)-1\bigg)\rho+2i\bar{\partial}\partial f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)\ ,

where
\tau
is the complex cubic differential whose real part is equal to
C=g_{J}A
and where
\bar{\partial}=\bar{\partial}_{J},\partial=\partial_{J}
.

Proof.

We will determine the expression for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
using Theorem
5.1
, hence starting from the explicit description of
\widehat{\mu}
given in Lemma
5.3
. As a torsion-free connection
\nabla
we can choose the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g_{J}=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
, which clearly satisfies
\nabla\rho=0
. Similar computations can be found in
[
Tra18
]
, where the functions
f
and
F
are chosen to have different properties.

The term
\widehat{\omega}(\nabla_{\bullet}s,\nabla_{\bullet}s)
:

Since
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection for
g_{J}
, we have
\nabla_{V}J=0
, and the element
\nabla_{V}A
is still an End
{}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})
-valued
1
-form for any
V\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
. Now let
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
be a local
g_{J}
-orthonormal frame of
T\Sigma
and let
\{e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}\}
be the dual frame. Then, we get

\displaystyle(\widehat{\omega}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((0,\nabla_{e_{1}}A),(0,\nabla_{e_{2}}A)\big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}f^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle\ .

According to the above observation, the tensors
\nabla_{e_{1}}A
and
\nabla_{e_{2}}A
can be written as

\nabla_{e_{1}}A=(A_{1})^{1}e^{*}_{1}+(A_{2})^{1}e^{*}_{2},\qquad\nabla_{e_{2}}A=(A_{1})^{2}e^{*}_{1}+(A_{2})^{2}e^{*}_{2}\ ,

where
A_{j}:=A(e_{j})
for
j=1,2
and

(A_{1})^{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}a^{1k}_{11}&a^{1k}_{12}\\
a^{1k}_{12}&-a^{1k}_{11}\end{pmatrix},\qquad(A_{2})^{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}a^{2k}_{11}&a^{2k}_{12}\\
a^{2k}_{12}&-a^{2k}_{11}\end{pmatrix},\ \text{for}\ k=1,2\ .

Using the relation
A_{2}=A_{1}J
and
\nabla_{\bullet}J=0
, we get

(A_{2})^{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}a^{1k}_{12}&-a^{1k}_{11}\\
-a^{1k}_{11}&-a^{1k}_{12}\end{pmatrix}\ .

Moreover, by (
4.3
), we have

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle=\tr\Big((A_1)^1(A_1)^2J+(A_2)^1(A_2)^2J\Big)

\displaystyle=4(a_{11}^{11}a_{12}^{21}-a_{12}^{11}a_{11}^{21})\ .

Recalling that
A=g_{J}^{-1}C
, last formula can be written in terms of
C(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
by using the following relation:

a_{lm}^{kj}=g_{J}\big((\nabla_{e_{k}}A)(e_{j})\cdot e_{l},e_{m}\big)=(\nabla_{e_{k}}C)(e_{j},e_{l},e_{m})=:(\nabla_{e_{k}}C)_{jlm}\ .

In the end, we obtain

\displaystyle(\widehat{\omega}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((0,\nabla_{e_{1}}A),(0,\nabla_{e_{2}}A)\big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||\tau||_{J}^{2}}{2}\Big)\Big((\nabla_{e_{1}}C)_{111}(\nabla_{e_{2}}C)_{112}-(\nabla_{e_{1}}C)_{112}(\nabla_{e_{2}}C)_{111}\Big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||\tau||_{J}^{2}}{2}\Big)\Big((\nabla_{e_{1}}C)_{222}(\nabla_{e_{2}}C)_{111}-(\nabla_{e_{1}}C)_{111}(\nabla_{e_{2}}C)_{222}\Big)\ ,

where in the last step we used
C(J\cdot,J\cdot,J\cdot)=-C(J\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)=-C(\cdot,J\cdot,\cdot)=-C(\cdot,\cdot,J\cdot)
. The action of the operators
\partial
and
\bar{\partial}
on
\tau
are defined as follows:

(\partial\tau)(v,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\nabla_{v}\tau-i\nabla_{Jv}\tau\Big),\qquad(\bar{\partial}\tau)(v,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\nabla_{v}\tau+i\nabla_{Jv}\tau\Big)\ .

With a fairly long calculation in local coordinates we deduce

||(\bar{\partial}\tau)(e_{1},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)||^{2}-||(\partial\tau)(e_{1},\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)||^{2}=(\nabla_{e_{1}}C)_{222}(\nabla_{e_{2}}C)_{111}-(\nabla_{e_{1}}C)_{111}(\nabla_{e_{2}}C)_{222}\ .

Finally, we get

\widehat{\omega}(\nabla_{\bullet}(J,A),\nabla_{\bullet}(J,A))=(\widehat{\omega}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((0,\nabla_{e_{1}}A),(0,\nabla_{e_{2}}A)\big)=-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\Big(\frac{||\tau||_{J}^{2}}{2}\Big)\big(||\bar{\partial}\tau||^{2}-||\partial\tau||^{2}\big)\rho\ ,

where
||\partial\tau||^{2}=||\partial\tau(v,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)||^{2}
and
||\bar{\partial}\tau||^{2}=||\bar{\partial}\tau(v,\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)||^{2}
for some unit vector
v
(the norm is independent of such vector).

The term
\langle\widehat{\mu}_{s}\ |\ R^{\nabla}\rangle
:

Since
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection for
g_{J}
, the tensor
R^{\nabla}
coincides with the Riemann tensor of
g_{J}
. A classical computation using a local orthonormal frame shows that
R^{\nabla}=K_{J}\rho J
, where
K_{J}
is the Gaussian curvature of
g_{J}
. From Lemma
5.3
we have
\widehat{\mu}_{(J,A)}(\cdot)=\bigg(1-f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}_{J}}{2}\Big)\bigg)\tr(J\cdot)
, therefore

\langle\widehat{\mu}_{s}\ |\ R^{\nabla}\rangle=2K_{J}\bigg(f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}_{J}}{2}\Big)-1\bigg)\rho\ .

The term
\mathrm{d}\big(c(\nabla_{\bullet}\widehat{\mu}_{s})\big)
:

Notice that, for any
B\in\mathfrak{sl}(T\Sigma,\rho)\equiv\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma)
and for any
v\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
, we have

\nabla_{v}\widehat{\mu}_{(J,A)}\big(B\big)=-\mathrm{d}f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}_{J}}{2}\Big)(v)\tr(JB)\ ,

where we used again
\nabla_{\bullet}J=0
. If
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
denotes a local
g_{J}
-orthonormal frame for
T\Sigma
and
\{e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}\}
denotes its dual frame, we obtain

\displaystyle c(\nabla_{\bullet}\widehat{\mu}_{(J,A)})(v)

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}\widehat{\mu}_{(J,A)}\ |\ (v\otimes e_{1})^{*}_{0}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{e_{2}}\widehat{\mu}_{(J,A)}\ |\ (v\otimes e_{2})^{*}_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)(e_{1})\tr\big(J(v\otimes e_1)^*_0\big)-\mathrm{d}f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)(e_{2})\tr\big(J(v\otimes e_2)^*_0\big)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)(e_{1})e^{*}_{1}\big(Jv\big)-\mathrm{d}f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)(e_{2})e^{*}_{2}\big(Jv\big)

\displaystyle=-\bigg(\mathrm{d}\Big(f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)\Big)\circ J\bigg)(v)\ .

In other words
c(\nabla_{\bullet}\widehat{\mu}_{(J,A)})=-\mathrm{d}\Big(f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)\Big)\circ J
. It is not difficult to show that, for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, the following relation holds:

\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{d}\psi\circ J\big)=-\Delta_{g_{J}}\psi=-2i\partial_{J}\bar{\partial}_{J}\psi=2i\bar{\partial}_{J}\partial_{J}\psi\ .

In the end, we get

\mathrm{d}\big(c(\nabla_{\bullet}\widehat{\mu}_{(J,A)})\big)=-\mathrm{d}\bigg(\mathrm{d}\Big(f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)\Big)\circ J\bigg)=-2i\bar{\partial}\partial f\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}}{2}\Big)\ .

∎

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
\rho
be a fixed area form on
\Sigma
, and let
c:=\frac{2\pi\chi(\Sigma)}{\mathrm{Area}(\Sigma,\rho)}
. Then, the map

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:

\displaystyle D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\longrightarrow B^{2}(\Sigma)\subset\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)^{*}

\displaystyle(J,A)\longmapsto\boldsymbol{\mu}(J,A)+2c\rho

is a moment map for the action of
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
on
\big(D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)),\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big)
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
5.2
, the de-Rham cohomology class of the closed
2
-form
\boldsymbol{\mu}(J,A)
does not depend on the choice of the section, and the same is true for its integral over the surface. Hence, if
A=0
, by Gauss-Bonnet Theorem we get

\int_{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\mu}(J,0)=-2\int_{\Sigma}K_{J}\rho=-4\pi\chi(\Sigma)\ .

In particular, the integral of the
2
-form
\boldsymbol{\mu}(J,A)+2c\rho
is equal to zero. This implies that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
takes values in the space of exact
2
-forms
B^{2}(\Sigma)
, which is contained in
\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)^{*}
(see Section
3.1
). Finally, the properties
(i)
and
(ii)
in Definition
3.5
continue to hold for
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
since the additional term
2c\rho
does not depend on the chosen section.
∎

In the remaining part of this section we show how, if we assume the additional hypothesis
\bar{\partial}_{J}\tau=0
, the moment map
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
we found is directly related to Wang’s equation for hyperbolic affine spheres in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
(see Section
2.3
). The idea of proof of the following result is similar to that used in
[
Tra18
]
for a slightly different moment map.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
and suppose that
A=g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{R}e(\tau)
with
\bar{\partial}_{J}\tau=0
, then

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(J,A)=-2e^{F\Big(\frac{||\tau||^{2}_{J}}{2}\Big)}\Big(K_{h}-||\tau||_{h}^{2}+1\Big)\rho\ ,\qquad\text{where}\ \ h:=e^{F\Big(\frac{||\tau||_{J}^{2}}{2}\Big)}g_{J}\ .

Proof.

If
A=0
, the statement is immediate. Suppose that
A\neq 0
and define
\lambda
to be the function
\frac{||\tau||^{2}_{J}}{2}
. Then, outside the zeroes of
A
it is easy to show that:

-\frac{i}{\lambda}\bar{\partial}\lambda\wedge\partial\lambda=||\partial\tau||^{2}_{J}\rho\ ,\qquad K_{J}\rho=-\frac{i}{3}\bar{\partial}\partial\log(\lambda)\ .

Let us assume for a moment that the following identity holds:

(5.3)

2i\bar{\partial}\bigg(\Big(f^{\prime}(\lambda)-\frac{f(\lambda)}{3\lambda}\Big)\partial\lambda\bigg)=-\frac{2i}{3\lambda}f^{\prime}(\lambda)\bar{\partial}\lambda\wedge\partial\lambda+2f(\lambda)K_{J}\rho+2i\bar{\partial}\partial f(\lambda)\ .

According to Theorem
5.4
we can write
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(J,A)
as follows:

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(J,A)=-\frac{2i}{3\lambda}f^{\prime}(\lambda)\bar{\partial}\lambda\wedge\partial\lambda+\frac{2i}{3}\big(1-f(\lambda)\big)\bar{\partial}\partial\log(\lambda)+2i\bar{\partial}\partial f(\lambda)+2c\rho

In particular, we obtain the following sequence of identities:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(J,A)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2i}{3\lambda}f^{\prime}(\lambda)\bar{\partial}\lambda\wedge\partial\lambda+\frac{2i}{3}\big(1-f(\lambda)\big)\bar{\partial}\partial\log(\lambda)+2i\bar{\partial}\partial f(\lambda)+2c\rho

(rel.
(\ref{relationformomentmap})
)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2i}{3\lambda}f^{\prime}(\lambda)\bar{\partial}\eta\wedge\partial\lambda+\frac{2i}{3}\big(1-f(\lambda)\big)\big(-\frac{3}{i}K_{J}\rho)+2i\bar{\partial}\partial f(\lambda)+2c\rho

(Lemma
2.19
)

\displaystyle=2i\bar{\partial}\bigg(\Big(\frac{f(\lambda)}{3\lambda}-f^{\prime}(\lambda)\Big)\partial\lambda\bigg)-2(K_{J}-c)\rho

\displaystyle=-2i\bar{\partial}F^{\prime}(\lambda)\wedge\partial\lambda-2(K_{J}-c)\rho

\displaystyle=-2i\bar{\partial}\partial F(\lambda)-2(K_{J}-c)\rho

\displaystyle=-2\Big(K_{J}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g_{J}}F(\lambda)-c\Big)\rho\ .

Now, if
h
denotes the Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
conformal to
g_{J}
with conformal factor equal to
e^{F(\lambda)}
, we get

K_{h}=e^{-F(\lambda)}\Big(K_{J}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g_{J}}F(\lambda)\Big)\ .

On the other hand, using the functional equation (
2.12
) satisfied by
F
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(J,A)

\displaystyle=-2\Big(K_{J}-\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{g_{J}}F(\lambda)-c\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=-2\Big(e^{F(\lambda)}K_{h}-c\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=-2e^{F(\lambda)}\big(K_{h}-||\tau||^{2}_{J}e^{-3F(\lambda)}+1\big)

\displaystyle=-2e^{F(\lambda)}\big(K_{h}-||\tau||^{2}_{h}+1\big)\rho\ .

In order to finish the proof, it only remains to show that relation (
5.3
) holds, which stems from the following identities:

2fK_{J}\rho=\frac{2i}{3\lambda}f\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda}\bar{\partial}\wedge\partial\lambda-\bar{\partial}\partial\lambda\Big),\quad 2i\bar{\partial}\partial f=2i\Big(f^{\prime\prime}\bar{\partial}\lambda\wedge\partial\lambda+f^{\prime}\bar{\partial}\partial\lambda\Big)\ .

This ends the proof outside the zeroes of
A
, which is a finite set in
\Sigma
. Thus, the statement follows by continuity of the expression.
∎

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
. Then
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(J,A)=0
and
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0
if and only if
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
.

Proof.

Recall from Section
2.5
that
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
is the space of pairs
(J,A)
such that
\big(h=e^{F}g_{J},A\big)
satisfies (
HS
) (see also Remark
2.8
). By Theorem
2.7
we know that, up to contraction with the metric,
A
is the real part of a
J
-holomorphic cubic differential
\tau
. Finally, the above theorem implies that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(J,A)=0
if and only if
K_{h}-||\tau||^{2}_{h}=-1
.
∎

5.3.
The symplectic quotient

Here we explain how the use of symplectic reduction allows us to determine (in part) the system of differential equations (
1.2
) defining
W_{(J,A)}
, hence those characterizing the tangent space to the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component (see Remark
4.9
). Following in parallel the construction done for Teichmüller space in Section
3.2
, the idea is to induce our symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
from the ambient space
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
to the quotient of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1}(0)
by the group
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
. On the other hand, there are two major differences with the case of
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
: the first is that the infinite-dimensional space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
is cut by two equations (see Corollary
5.7
) and, only one of them, has an interpretation as a moment map. In particular, we have to look at the space
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1}(0)\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
modulo
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
; the second is that once we induce the symplectic form on the quotient, the pairing
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}(\mathbf{I}\cdot,\cdot)=\mathbf{g}_{f}
gives rise to a pseudo-Riemannian metric, and this generates additional difficulties since one is intent to identify the space
W_{(J,A)}
with the
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to the
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-orbit.

Our moment map
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
has values in the space of exact
2
-forms on the surface, which is contained in the dual Lie algebra of the Hamiltonian group (see Corollary
5.5
). After recalling two technical lemmas, in Proposition
5.10
we compute a primitive of the differential of the moment map (still with values in the exact 2-forms). This will allow us, in Proposition
5.12
, to perform a highly non-trivial integration by parts, which will be useful later in discussing the (possible) presence of degenerate vectors for the pseudo-metric away from the Fuchsian locus. Then,
with Proposition
5.15
we prove the inclusion of
W_{(J,A)}
inside the tangent to
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, the discussion of which had been left hanging by Section
4.4
. Finally, inspired by the Kähler reduction of Teichmüller space, in Theorem
H
we are able to characterize
W_{(J,A)}
as the largest subspace in
T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
that is both
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to the orbit and invariant under the action of the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
.

The statements and the proofs of Proposition
5.10
and Proposition
5.12
are inspired by the analogous counterparts in the anti-de Sitter case (
[
MST21
, Proposition 6.10 and 6.12]
). Despite that, the presence of the
1
-form part in the tensor
A
created additional problems during the development of the proofs, which will be highlighted throughout. We first recall two technical lemmas that will be useful further on.

Lemma 5.8

(
[
MST21
, Lemma 4.16]
)
.

Let
B
be a trace-less endomorphism of
T\Sigma
, then

(\nabla_{X}B)Y-(\nabla_{Y}B)X=(\mathrm{div}_{g}B)(Y)X-(\mathrm{div}_{g}B)(X)Y\ .

Lemma 5.9

(
[
MST21
, Lemma 4.15]
)
.

Let
\dot{J}\in T_{J}\mathcal{J}(\Sigma)
be an infinitesimal variation of a complex structure on
\Sigma
. If
\dot{\nabla}
denotes the first order variation of the Levi-Civita connection of
g_{J}=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
along
\dot{J}
, then the following holds:

(5.4)

\dot{\nabla}_{X}Y=-\frac{1}{2}\big((\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(X)JY+J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Y\big)\ ,

for every tangent vector fields
X,Y
on
\Sigma
.

Proposition 5.10
.

For every
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
such that
A=g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{R}e(\tau)
with
\bar{\partial}_{J}\tau=0
, and for every tangent vector
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
we have

(5.5)

\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A})=\mathrm{d}\Big((f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}f\circ\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)\ ,

where
f,f^{\prime},\dot{f}
are evaluated at
\frac{||\tau||_{J}^{2}}{2}
and
\beta
is the
1
-form defined as
\beta(V):=\langle\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{V}A)J\rangle
.

Proof.

The most intricate part of the proof is encompassed in showing the following identity:

(5.6)

\bigg(-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\big(||\bar{\partial}\tau||^{2}-||\partial\tau||^{2}\big)\rho\bigg)^{\prime}=-\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)-2\dot{f}K_{J}\rho+\mathrm{d}(f-1)\wedge\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\ ,

where the derivative is taken with respect to
(J,A)
along tangent directions
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
. Let us assume for a moment that (
5.6
) holds and let us prove the formula stated in the theorem. In fact, the other terms in
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(see Theorem
5.4
) are easier to handle

(Proposition
3.10
)

\displaystyle\big(2(f-1)K_{J}\rho\big)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2\dot{f}K_{J}\rho+2(f-1)\mathrm{d}K_{J}(\dot{J})\rho

\displaystyle=2\dot{f}K_{J}\rho+(f-1)\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\big)\ .

Moreover,

\big(2i\bar{\partial}\partial f\big)^{\prime}=-\big(\Delta_{g_{J}}f\big)^{\prime}\rho=\mathrm{d}\big((\mathrm{d}f\circ J)^{\prime}\big)=\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J+\mathrm{d}f\circ\dot{J}\big)\ .

Combining these formulas, we get the desired expression for the moment map

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A})

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)-2\dot{f}K_{J}\rho+\mathrm{d}f\wedge\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J+\mathrm{d}f\circ\dot{J}\big)+2\dot{f}K_{J}\rho+(f-1)\mathrm{d}\big(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Big((f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}f\circ\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)\ .

Now let us focus on proving relation (
5.6
). As was shown in Theorem
5.4
, we know that:

(5.7)

-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\big(||\bar{\partial}\tau||^{2}-||\partial\tau||^{2}\big)=(\widehat{\omega}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((0,\nabla_{e_{1}}A),(0,\nabla_{e_{2}}A)\big)\ ,

for any choice of a local frame
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
such that
\rho(e_{1},e_{2})=1
. Therefore, we can compute the following derivative:

\displaystyle\bigg(-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\big(||\bar{\partial}\tau||^{2}-||\partial\tau||^{2}\big)\bigg)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bigg((\widehat{\omega}_{f})_{(J,A)}\big((0,\nabla_{e_{1}}A),(0,\nabla_{e_{2}}A)\big)\bigg)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\bigg(-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle\bigg)^{\prime}\ ,

where in the second step we used that, for any
i=1,2
, the endomorphism part of
\nabla_{e_{i}}A
is trace-less and
g_{J}
-symmetric. In order to simplify the computation of the derivative, let us make some preliminary observations. Since equation (
5.7
) is true for any unit volume local frame
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
, we can further assume that it is
g_{J}
-orthonormal and does not change as
J
varies along tangent directions. Moreover, the terms corresponding to variations
\dot{J}
make no contributions as
\tr(\nabla_{e_1}A\nabla_{e_2}A\dot J)=0
(see (
4.3
)). This allows us to reduce the study of the derivative to only the following terms:

(5.8)

\displaystyle\bigg(-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\big(||\bar{\partial}\tau||^{2}-||\partial\tau||^{2}\big)\bigg)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{24}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle+

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)^{\prime},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)^{\prime}J\rangle\ ,

and we expressed the first order variation of
f^{\prime}
as
\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{4}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle
(see Lemma 3.22 in
[
RT24
]
). At this point, using Lemma
5.9
, we can obtain an expression for
(\nabla_{X}A)^{\prime}
. In fact,

(\nabla_{X}A)^{\prime}=\dot{\nabla}_{X}A+\nabla_{X}A^{\prime}

and we can compute

\displaystyle(\dot{\nabla}_{X}A)(Y)Z

\displaystyle=\dot{\nabla}_{X}\big(A(Y)Z\big)-A(\dot{\nabla}_{X}Y)Z-A(Y)\dot{\nabla}_{X}Z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(-(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(X)JA(Y)Z-J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)Z+(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(X)A(JY)Z+

\displaystyle\ \ \ +A\big(J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Y\big)Z+(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(X)A(Y)JZ+A(Y)J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Z\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big(3(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(X)A(Y)JZ+A\big(J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Y\big)Z+A(Y)J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Z-J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)Z\Big)

where we used
A(JY)Z=A(Y)JZ
and
A(Y)JZ=-JA(Y)Z
. As for the term involving the derivative of
A
, we first notice that
A^{\prime}=J\dot{J}A+\dot{A}
, hence

\displaystyle(\nabla_{X}A^{\prime})

\displaystyle=J\nabla_{X}\dot{J}A+J\dot{J}\nabla_{X}A+\nabla_{X}\dot{A}_{0}+\nabla_{X}\dot{A}_{\text{tr}}

\displaystyle=J\nabla_{X}\dot{J}A+J\dot{J}\nabla_{X}A+\nabla_{X}\dot{A}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\tr(\nabla_X\dot JJA)+\tr(\dot JJ\nabla_XA)\Big)\ .

Now, choosing
X=e_{1}
and observing that the two trace terms in
(\nabla_{e_{1}}A^{\prime})
and the four elements
AJ\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J},J\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}A,J\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}A,J\dot{J}\nabla_{e_{1}}A
are zero once they pair with
(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J
using the scalar product (
4.3
), we get

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)^{\prime},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(e_{1})\langle AJ,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{f^{\prime}}{12}\langle A\big(\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}\cdot\big)J,\nabla_{e_{2}}AJ\rangle\ .

Moreover, since
\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)^{\prime}J\rangle=-\langle(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)^{\prime}\rangle
, performing a similar computation as above, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)^{\prime},(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(e_{2})\langle AJ,(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle+\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{f^{\prime}}{12}\langle A\big(\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{J}\cdot\big)J,\nabla_{e_{1}}AJ\rangle\ .

Combining everything together in (
5.8
), we have

(5.9)

\displaystyle\Big(-\frac{2}{3}f^{\prime}\big(||\bar{\partial}\tau||^{2}-||\partial\tau||^{2}\big)\Big)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{24}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle+\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\Big((\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(e_{2})\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,A\rangle

\displaystyle-(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(e_{1})\langle AJ,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle\Big)-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\Big(\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{A}_{0})J)\rangle\Big)+\frac{f^{\prime}}{12}\Big(\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,A\big(\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{J}\cdot\big)\rangle

\displaystyle-\langle A\big(\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}\cdot\big),\nabla_{e_{2}}A\rangle\Big)\ ,

where we used, again, the symmetry and the compatibility of the scalar product with
J
(see (
4.4
)). Regarding the divergence term found in (
5.9
), it can be elaborated as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\Big((\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(e_{2})\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,A\rangle-(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(e_{1})\langle A,\nabla_{e_{2}}A\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=-\big(\mathrm{div}\dot{J}\wedge\mathrm{d}f\big)(e_{1},e_{2})

\displaystyle=\big(\mathrm{d}(f-1)\wedge\mathrm{div}\dot{J}\big)(e_{1},e_{2})\ ,

where we used
\mathrm{d}f=\frac{f^{\prime}}{4}\langle A,\nabla_{\bullet}A\rangle
. Comparing relation (
5.6
) with (
5.9
), the proof is complete if we show that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{(i)}\ -\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)(e_{1},e_{2})=

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{24}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle+2\dot{f}K_{J}+

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\Big(\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle+\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{A}_{0})J)\rangle\Big)\ ,

\boldsymbol{(ii)}\ \ \langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,A\big(\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{J}\cdot\big)\rangle-\langle A\big(\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}\cdot\big),\nabla_{e_{2}}A\rangle=0\ .\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad

Proof of relation
\boldsymbol{(i)}

First notice that if
A=0
then the relation is clearly satisfied. Suppose
A
is not identically zero, then

-\mathrm{d}\Big(\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)=-\frac{1}{6}\mathrm{d}f^{\prime}\wedge\beta-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\mathrm{d}\beta=-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{24}\langle A,\nabla_{\bullet}A\rangle\wedge\beta-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\mathrm{d}\beta\ .

Regarding the differential of
\beta(\bullet)=\langle\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J\rangle
we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\beta(e_{1},e_{2})

\displaystyle=e_{1}\cdot\big(\langle\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle\big)-e_{2}\cdot\big(\langle\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle\big)-\langle\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{[e_{1},e_{2}]}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle-\langle\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle+\langle\dot{A}_{0},\big(\nabla_{e_{1}}\nabla_{e_{2}}A-\nabla_{e_{2}}\nabla_{e_{1}}A-\nabla_{[e_{1},e_{2}]}A\big)J\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle-\langle\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle-3K_{J}\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\ ,

where the last equality follows from
R^{\nabla}(e_{1},e_{2})A=3K_{J}AJ
since
R^{\nabla}(e_{1},e_{2})=\nabla_{e_{1}}\nabla_{e_{2}}-\nabla_{e_{2}}\nabla_{e_{1}}-\nabla_{[e_{1},e_{2}]}
. Thus,

(5.10)

-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\mathrm{d}\beta(e_{1},e_{2})=-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\Big(\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle-\langle\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle\Big)+2\dot{f}K_{J}\ .

Concerning the other therm, we need to prove that

\big(\langle A,\nabla_{\bullet}A\rangle\wedge\beta\big)(e_{1},e_{2})=\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle\ .

Notice that, for any
p\in\Sigma
outside the zeroes of
A
, the elements
(A_{1})_{p}:=\big(A(e_{1})\big)_{p}
and
(A_{1}J)_{p}:=\big(A(e_{1})J\big)
form a basis for the space of
g_{J}
-symmetric and trace-less endomorphisms of
T_{p}\Sigma
. In particular, using the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
we can write

\dot{A}_{0}=\frac{1}{||A||^{2}}\Big(\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle A+\langle AJ,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle AJ\Big),\quad\nabla_{e_{1}}A=\frac{1}{||A||^{2}}\Big(\langle A,\nabla_{e_{1}}A\rangle A+\langle AJ,\nabla_{e_{1}}A\rangle AJ\Big)\ .

Replacing these identities in the previous equation, we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\langle A,\nabla_{\bullet}A\rangle\wedge\beta\big)(e_{1},e_{2})

\displaystyle=\langle A,\nabla_{e_{1}}A\rangle\langle\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle-\langle A,\nabla_{e_{2}}A\rangle\langle\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle A,\nabla_{e_{1}}A\rangle}{||A||^{2}}\Big(\langle\dot{A}_{0},A\rangle\langle A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle+\langle\dot{A}_{0},AJ\rangle\langle AJ,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{\langle A,\nabla_{e_{2}}A\rangle}{||A||^{2}}\Big(\langle\dot{A}_{0},A\rangle\langle A,(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle+\langle\dot{A}_{0},AJ\rangle\langle AJ,(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\dot{A}_{0},A\rangle}{||A||^{2}}\Big(\langle A,\nabla_{e_{1}}A\rangle\langle A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle-\langle A,\nabla_{e_{2}}A\rangle\langle A,(\nabla_{e_{1}}A)J\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\langle\dot{A}_{0},A\rangle}{||A||^{2}}\cdot\big\langle\langle A,\nabla_{e_{1}}A\rangle A+\langle AJ,\nabla_{e_{1}}A\rangle AJ;(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle A,\dot{A}_{0}\rangle\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,(\nabla_{e_{2}}A)J\rangle\ .

Since the relation is true on the complement of a finite set in
\Sigma
(the zeroes of
A
), it extends on the whole surface by continuity of the expression.

Proof of relation
\boldsymbol{(ii)}

As explained at the beginning of the section, the presence of the
1
-form part in the tensor
A
generates further difficulties. In fact, one has to deal with terms of the form
A\big(\nabla_{e_{i}}\dot{J}\cdot\big)
which do not appear in the anti-de Sitter case. First of all notice that if
A
is identically zero, then the relation is clearly satisfied. Hence, let us assume that this is not the case. In the following, we will use the notations introduced in the proof of Theorem
5.4
. Namely,

\nabla_{e_{1}}A=(A_{1})^{1}e^{*}_{1}+(A_{2})^{1}e^{*}_{2},\qquad\nabla_{e_{2}}A=(A_{1})^{2}e^{*}_{1}+(A_{2})^{2}e^{*}_{2}\ ,

where
A_{j}:=A(e_{j})
for
j=1,2
and since
A=g_{J}^{-1}C
, we have

\displaystyle A_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}C_{111}&C_{112}\\
C_{112}&-C_{111}\end{pmatrix},\qquad A_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}C_{112}&-C_{111}\\
-C_{111}&-C_{112}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle(A_{1})^{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}(\nabla_{k}C)_{111}&(\nabla_{k}C)_{112}\\
(\nabla_{k}C)_{112}&-(\nabla_{k}C)_{111}\end{pmatrix},\quad(A_{2})^{k}:=\begin{pmatrix}(\nabla_{k}C)_{112}&-(\nabla_{k}C)_{111}\\
-(\nabla_{k}C)_{111}&-(\nabla_{k}C)_{112}\end{pmatrix},\ k=1,2

\displaystyle(\nabla_{k}C)_{jlm}:=(\nabla_{e_{k}}C)(e_{j},e_{l},e_{m})=g_{J}\big((\nabla_{e_{k}}A)(e_{j})\cdot e_{l},e_{m}\big)\ .

By assumption,
C
is the real part of a holomorphic cubic differential and, this is equivalent (see Theorem
2.7
), to require that
(\nabla_{JX}A)(\cdot)=(\nabla_{X}A)(J\cdot)
for any vector field
X
on the surface. In particular, we obtain the following additional relations

(5.11)

(\nabla_{2}C)_{111}=(\nabla_{1}C)_{112},\qquad(\nabla_{2}C)_{112}=-(\nabla_{1}C)_{111}\ .

The next step is to write explicitly, in a similar way, the tensors

A\big(\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}\cdot\big)=(\widetilde{A}_{1})^{1}e_{1}^{*}+(\widetilde{A}_{2})^{1}e_{2}^{*},\qquad A\big(\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{J}\cdot\big)=(\widetilde{A}_{1})^{2}e_{1}^{*}+(\widetilde{A}_{2})^{2}e_{2}^{*}\ .

For any
p\in\Sigma
outside the zeroes of
A
, the elements
A_{1}
and
A_{2}=A_{1}J
form a basis for the space of
g_{J}
-symmetric and trace-less endomorphisms of
T_{p}\Sigma
. In particular, both
\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}
and
\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{J}
can be written in this basis as

\displaystyle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}=\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(\nabla_{e_1}\dot JA_1)A_{1}+\tr(A_1J\nabla_{e_1}\dot J)A_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{J}=\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(\nabla_{e_2}\dot JA_1)A_{1}+\tr(A_1J\nabla_{e_2}\dot J)A_{2}\Big)\ .

This new form of the endomorphisms allows us to compute their values on the
g_{J}
-orthonormal basis of the tangent to the surface

\displaystyle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}\cdot e_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(\nabla_{e_1}\dot JA_1)C_{111}+\tr(A_1J\nabla_{e_1}\dot J)C_{112}\Big)e_{1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(\nabla_{e_1}\dot JA_1)C_{112}-\tr(A_1J\nabla_{e_1}\dot J)C_{111}\Big)e_{2}

\displaystyle\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}\cdot e_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(\nabla_{e_1}\dot JA_1)C_{112}-\tr(A_1J\nabla_{e_1}\dot J)C_{111}\Big)e_{1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(\nabla_{e_1}\dot JA_1)C_{111}+\tr(A_1J\nabla_{e_1}\dot J)C_{112}\Big)e_{2}

and the same calculation can be done for
\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{J}
. In particular, we obtain

\displaystyle(\widetilde{A}_{1})^{k}=\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(\nabla_{e_k}\dot JA_1)\big(C_{111}A_{1}+C_{112}A_{2}\big)+\tr(A_1J\nabla_{e_k}\dot J)\big(C_{112}A_{1}-C_{111}A_{2}\big)\Big),

\displaystyle(\widetilde{A}_{2})^{k}=\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(\nabla_{e_k}\dot JA_1)\big(C_{112}A_{1}-C_{111}A_{2}\big)-\tr(A_1J\nabla_{e_k}\dot J)\big(C_{111}A_{1}+C_{112}A_{2}\big)\Big)\ .

To conclude, we notice that
\tr(A_1^2)=2\big(C_{111}^{2}+C_{112}^{2}\big)
, hence

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{e_{1}}A,A\big(\nabla_{e_{2}}\dot{J}\cdot\big)\rangle-\langle A\big(\nabla_{e_{1}}\dot{J}\cdot\big),\nabla_{e_{2}}A\rangle

\displaystyle=\tr\Big((A_1)^1(\widetilde A_1)^2+(A_2)^1(\widetilde A_2)^2\Big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\tr\Big((A_1)^2(\widetilde A_1)^1-(A_2)^2(\widetilde A_2)^1\Big)

\displaystyle=0\ .

Since the relation is true on the complement of a finite set in
\Sigma
(the zeroes of
A
), it extends on the whole surface by continuity of the expression.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

In analogy with what happens for the
\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{P}\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
case (see
[
MST21
, Remark 6.11]
), we fix a primitive of
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
found in Proposition
5.10
and we consider the linear map
L_{(J,A)}:T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\to\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)/B^{1}(\Sigma)\subset\mathfrak{S}(\Sigma,\rho)^{*}
which associates to each tangent vector
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
the above primitive (modulo exact
1
-forms). With an abuse of notation we will denote this primitive by
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\equiv L_{(J,A)}(\dot{J},\dot{A})
.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, then for every
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
and for every symplectic vector field
V
, we have

(5.12)

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big((\mathcal{L}_{V}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C);(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)=-\langle\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\ |\ V\rangle_{\mathfrak{S}}

Proof.

Before we begin the proof of the formula stated in the proposition, let us make some preliminary remarks. For any vector field
X
on the surface, let use define the operator
M_{X}:\Gamma(T\Sigma)\to\Gamma(T\Sigma)
as
M_{X}(Y):=\nabla^{g}_{Y}X
, where
\nabla^{g}
is the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g\equiv g_{J}=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
. The endomorphism
M_{X}
can be decomposed as

M_{X}=\frac{\tr(M_X)}{2}\mathds{1}-\frac{\tr(JM_X)}{2}J+M_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}\ ,

where the first term is the trace part, the second one is the
g
-skew-symmetric part, and the third one is the
g
-symmetric and trace-less part. If
X=V
is a
\rho
-symplectic vector field, then the trace part of
M_{V}
vanishes. Since
J
is
\nabla^{g}
-parallel, we have
M_{JV}=JM_{V}
and its decomposition is given by

(5.13)

M_{JV}=JM_{V}=\frac{\tr(JM_V)}{2}\mathds{1}+0+JM_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}\ .

In particular, the
g
-skew-symmetric part of
M_{JV}
vanishes and
JM_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}=M_{JV}^{\mathrm{s}}
. Recall that (see (
4.22
)) we found the following formula for the Lie derivative of
C
expressed in terms of the tensor
A

(g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C)(\cdot)=(\nabla_{V}A)(\cdot)+A(M_{V}\cdot)+A(\cdot)M_{V}+M_{V}^{*}A(\cdot)\ ,

which can be re-written using the decomposition of
M_{V}
found above

(5.14)

(g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C)(\cdot)=\underbrace{(\nabla_{V}A)(\cdot)-\frac{3}{2}\tr(JM_V)AJ+A\big(M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big)}_{\text{trace-less}}+\underbrace{AM_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}+M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}A}_{\text{trace part}}\ .

At this point, we can compute the symplectic form

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})\big((\mathcal{L}_{V}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C),(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big((f-1)\langle\mathcal{L}_{V}J,J\dot{J}\rangle-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle(g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C)_{0},(\dot{A}J+A\dot{J})_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{f^{\prime}}{12}\langle(g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C)_{\text{tr}},(\dot{A}J+A\dot{J})_{\text{tr}}\rangle\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big((f-1)\langle\mathcal{L}_{V}J,J\dot{J}\rangle-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{V}A-\frac{3}{2}\tr(JM_V)AJ,\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\big(\langle A\big(M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle AM_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}+M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}A,(\dot{A}J+A\dot{J})_{\text{tr}}\rangle\big)\Big)\rho

In order to simplify the third and fourth term in the integral, we make us of the following identity which will be proven at the end

(5.15)

\langle A\big(M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\langle AM_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}+M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}A,(\dot{A}J+A\dot{J})_{\text{tr}}\rangle=0\ .

Regarding the first term in the symplectic form, we use Lemma
3.9
and we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}(f-1)\langle\mathcal{L}_{V}J,J\dot{J}\rangle\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big((1-f)(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(V)+(f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}(\dot{J}V)\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big((1-f)(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(V)-\mathrm{d}f(\dot{J}V)+\mathrm{div}_{g}\big((f-1)\dot{J}V\big)\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}\Big((f-1)(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(V)+\mathrm{d}f(\dot{J}V)\Big)\rho\ .

Moving on to the second term in the symplectic form

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\nabla_{V}A-\frac{3}{2}\tr(JM_V)AJ,\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle\rho

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}\Big(-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta(V)-\dot{f}\mathrm{div}_{g}(JV)\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}\Big(-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta(V)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}(JV)-\mathrm{div}_{g}(\dot{f}JV)\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}\Big(-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta(V)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}(JV)\Big)\rho\ .

In the end, combining the above two relations with (
5.15
), we obtain

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})\big((\mathcal{L}_{V}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C),(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}\Big((f-1)(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J})(V)+\mathrm{d}f(\dot{J}V)-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta(V)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}(JV)\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\iota_{V}\Big((f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}f\circ\dot{J}-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J\Big)\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big((f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}f\circ\dot{J}-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J\Big)\wedge\iota_{V}\rho

\displaystyle=-\langle\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\ |\ V\rangle_{\mathfrak{S}}\ ,

where in the third step we used equation (
3.6
).

Proof of relation (
5.15
)

Once again, the presence of the
1
-form part in
A
makes the analysis more difficult. In fact, there is an additional term which does not appear in the anti-de Sitter case. If
A=0
the identity is clearly satisfied. Suppose
A\neq 0
, then for any
p\in\Sigma
outside the zeroes of
A
the elements
A_{1}
and
A_{2}=A_{1}J
form a basis for the space of
g_{J}
-symmetric and trace-less endomorphisms of
T_{p}\Sigma
. Let
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
be a
g_{J}
-orthonormal basis and let
\{e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*}\}
be its dual. Following the approach used to prove Proposition
5.10
, we have

\displaystyle A\big(M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(M_V^\mathrm sA_1)\big(C_{111}A_{1}+C_{112}A_{2}\big)+\tr(A_1JM_V^\mathrm s)\big(C_{112}A_{1}-C_{111}A_{2}\big)\Big)e_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(\tr(M_V^\mathrm sA_1)\big(C_{112}A_{1}-C_{111}A_{2}\big)+\tr(A_1JM_V^\mathrm s)\big(C_{111}A_{1}+C_{112}A_{2}\big)\Big)e_{2}^{*}\ ,

\displaystyle AM_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}+M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}A=\tr(M_V^\mathrm sA_1)\mathds{1}e_{1}^{*}+\tr(A_1JM_V^\mathrm s)\mathds{1}e_{2}^{*},

\displaystyle(\dot{A}J+A\dot{J})_{\text{tr}}=\frac{1}{2}\tr(\dot A_2)\mathds{1}e_{1}^{*}-\frac{1}{2}\tr(\dot A_1)\mathds{1}e_{2}^{*},\qquad\dot{A}_{0}J=(\dot{A}_{1})_{0}Je_{1}^{*}+(\dot{A}_{2})_{0}Je_{2}^{*}

In particular, we can write the two terms in (
5.15
) as follows:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\langle AM_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}+M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}A,(\dot{A}J+A\dot{J})_{\text{tr}}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\tr(\dot A_2)\tr(A_1M_V^\mathrm s)-\tr(\dot A_1)\tr(A_1M_{JV}^\mathrm s)\Big),

\displaystyle\langle A\big(M_{V}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),\dot{A}_{0}J\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\tr(A_1M_V^\mathrm s)}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(C_{111}\tr(A_1(\dot A_1)_0J)+C_{112}\tr(A_2(\dot A_1)_0J)\Big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{\tr(A_1M_V^\mathrm s)}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(C_{112}\tr(A_1(\dot A_2)_0J)-C_{111}\tr(A_2(\dot A_2)_0J)\Big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{\tr(A_1M_{JV}^\mathrm s)}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(C_{112}\tr(A_1(\dot A_1)_0J)-C_{111}\tr(A_2(\dot A_1)_0J)\Big)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{\tr(A_1M_{JV}^\mathrm s)}{\tr(A_1^2)}\Big(C_{111}\tr(A_1(\dot A_2)_0J)+C_{112}\tr(A_2(\dot A_2)_0J)\Big)\ .

Finally, writing
\dot{A}
in term of the variations of the tensor
C
, namely

\dot{A}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}\dot{C}_{111}&\dot{C}_{112}\\
\dot{C}_{112}&\dot{C}_{122}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\dot{A}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\dot{C}_{112}&\dot{C}_{122}\\
\dot{C}_{122}&\dot{C}_{222}\end{pmatrix}

and using that
\tr(A_i(\dot A_k)_0J)=\tr(A_i\dot A_kJ)
for any
i,j=1,2
, a direct computation shows the desired equality. Since (
5.15
) holds on the complement of a finite set in
\Sigma
, it holds everywhere by continuity of the expression.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

It is crucial to emphasize the importance of the result just proved. From the general theory of moment maps (see Definition
3.5
) we know that (
5.12
) follows from Corollary
5.5
if
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is paired with Hamiltonian vector fields. The point is that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
can not be promoted to a moment map for the action of
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
, which still preserves
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
. In particular, the formula showed above is far from being obvious when computed for a symplectic vector field, which decomposes as the sum of a harmonic and a Hamiltonian vector field (see (
3.2
)).

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, then the kernel of the linearized Codazzi-like equation
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0
is given by

\big\{(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\ |\ \mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0\big\}\ .

Proof.

Recall that, for any vector fields
X,Y,Z\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
, we have

(5.16)

(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A)(X,Y)Z=(\nabla_{X}A)(Y)Z-(\nabla_{Y}A)(X)Z\ .

Therefore, we need to compute the derivative of (
5.16
) with respect to variations of
(J,A)
. For instance,

\displaystyle\Big((\nabla_{X}A)(Y)Z-(\nabla_{Y}A)(X)Z\Big)^{\prime}=(\dot{\nabla}_{X}A)(Y)Z-(\dot{\nabla}_{Y}A)(X)Z+(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A^{\prime})(X,Y)Z\ ,

where
A^{\prime}=J\dot{J}A+\dot{A}
. The part involving the variation of the connection has already been computed in the proof of Proposition
5.10

\displaystyle(\dot{\nabla}_{X}A)(Y)Z=\frac{1}{2}\Big(3(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(X)A(Y)JZ+A\big(J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Y\big)Z+A(Y)J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Z-J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)Z\Big).

Subtracting the term
(\dot{\nabla}_{Y}A)(X)Z
from the last expression and using Lemma
5.8
on
A\big((\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Y-(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})X\big)
, we get

\displaystyle(\dot{\nabla}_{X}A)(Y)Z-(\dot{\nabla}_{Y}A)(X)Z

\displaystyle=J\Big((\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(Y)A(X)-(\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(X)A(Y)\Big)Z+\frac{1}{2}JA(X)(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})Z

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{2}J\Big((\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})A(X)-A(Y)(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})-(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)\Big)Z

\displaystyle=-J(\mathrm{div}\dot{J}\wedge A)(X,Y)Z+\frac{1}{2}JA(X)(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})Z

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{2}J\Big((\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})A(X)-A(Y)(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})-(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)\Big)Z\ .

Regarding the term with the exterior covariant derivative of
A^{\prime}
, we have

(5.17)

(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A^{\prime})(X,Y)Z=\underbrace{(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}(J\dot{J}A))(X,Y)Z}_{\text{term}\ (a)}+\underbrace{(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{\text{tr}})(X,Y)Z}_{\text{term}\ (b)}+(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0})(X,Y)Z\ .

The term
(a)
is easy to handle since
\nabla_{\bullet}J=0
and
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0
,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}(J\dot{J}A))(X,Y)Z

\displaystyle=\nabla_{X}(J\dot{J}A)(Y)Z-\nabla_{Y}(J\dot{J}A)(X)Z

\displaystyle=J\Big((\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)-(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})A(X)\Big)Z\ .

As for the term
(b)
, recall that
\dot{A}_{\text{tr}}=\frac{1}{2}\tr(\dot JJA)\mathds{1}
, hence

\displaystyle(\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{\text{tr}})(X,Y)Z

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{X}\dot{A}_{\text{tr}})(Y)Z-(\nabla_{Y}\dot{A}_{\text{tr}})(X)Z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\tr(\nabla_X\big(\dot JJA(Y)\big))Z-\frac{1}{2}\tr(\nabla_Y\big(\dot JJA(X)\big))Z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\tr((\nabla_X\dot J)JA(Y)-(\nabla_Y\dot J)JA(X))Z\ .

We conclude if we show that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tr((\nabla_X\dot J)JA(Y)-(\nabla_Y\dot J)JA(X))Z

\displaystyle=-J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)Z+J(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})A(X)Z

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{1}{2}JA(X)(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})Z-\frac{1}{2}J(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})A(X)Z

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{2}JA(Y)(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})Z+\frac{1}{2}J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)Z\ ,

which follows from the fact that the elements
JA(X)\nabla_{Y}\dot{J}-J(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})A(X)
and
J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)-JA(Y)\nabla_{X}\dot{J}
are both trace-term, and they can be written as

\displaystyle JA(X)\nabla_{Y}\dot{J}-J(\nabla_{Y}\dot{J})A(X)=-\tr(J(\nabla_Y\dot J)A(X))\mathds{1},

\displaystyle J(\nabla_{X}\dot{J})A(Y)-JA(Y)\nabla_{X}\dot{J}=-\tr(JA(Y)\nabla_X\dot J)\mathds{1}\ .

∎

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
and consider the space
W_{(J,A)}
defined by the system of equations (
1.2
). Then,

W_{(J,A)}\subset T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\ .

Proof.

According to Corollary
5.7
, the infinite-dimensional space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
can be seen as the intersection of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1}(0)
with
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}:=\{(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\ |\ \mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0\}
. In particular, the tangent space to the pre-image of the zero locus of the moment map is identified with
\mathrm{Ker}\big(\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\big)
. On the other hand, Proposition
5.10
and Lemma
5.14
together implies that

(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\iff\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\Big((f-1)\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}f\circ\dot{J}+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\dot{A}_{0},(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J\rangle\Big)=0\\
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0\end{cases}

Looking again at the equations (
1.2
) defining the space
W_{(J,A)}
, it is clear that

W_{(J,A)}\subset T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\ .

∎

At this point, it must be noted that the subspace we are interested in can be described as

(5.18)

W_{(J,A)}=\left\{(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\ \bigg|\ \parbox{150.00023pt}{$(\dot{J},\dot{A}),\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}})$ \\
$\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0$}\right\}

which clarifies the connection of the first two equations in (
1.2
) with symplectic reduction theory.

Theorem H
.

For any
(J,A)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
, the vector space
W_{(J,A)}
is the largest subspace in
T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
that is:

\bullet

invariant under the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
;

\bullet

\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to the orbit
T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)
.

Proof.

Recall from Corollary
5.7
that the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
can be identified with
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{-1}(0)\cap\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}:=\{(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\ |\ \mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0\}
. Let us denote with
\widetilde{W}
the largest subspace in
T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
that is
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to
T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)
and
\mathbf{I}
-invariant. Suppose that
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in\widetilde{W}
, hence the same is true for
\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})
by
\mathbf{I}
-invariance. In particular, both
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
and
\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})
lie in
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
. We now note that
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
is
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to the
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
-orbit if and only if
\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})
lies in
\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}})
thanks to the following computation

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{f}\big((\mathcal{L}_{V}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C),(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{g}_{f}\big((\mathcal{L}_{V}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C),\mathbf{I}^{2}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big((\mathcal{L}_{V}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{V}C),\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\big(\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)\ |\ V\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}},\quad\forall\ V\in\mathfrak{H}(\Sigma,\rho)\ .

by definition of moment map. This implies that an element
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
belongs to
\widetilde{W}
if and only if

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A})=0\\
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\big(\mathbf{I}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)=0\\
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\mathrm{div}_{g}\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0\ ,\end{cases}

which is equivalent to the system of partial differential equations (
1.2
) defining the subspace
W_{(J,A)}
(see Proposition
5.10
).
∎

6.
Final properties

In this final section of the paper we discuss about the non-degeneracy of the pseudo-metric over the entire
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component, and we study the relation of
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
with Goldman’s symplectic form on the Fuchsian locus, both along horizontal and vertical directions.

6.1.
The finite-dimensional quotient

Although the main part of the results have been shown, it still remains to prove Theorem
G
, namely the identification of
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
with the finite dimensional quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)/H
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is the smooth manifold of real dimension
16g-16+2g
isomorphic to the quotient of the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
by the group
\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
(see Theorem
F
), and
H:=\Symp_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
is isomorphic to
H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
(see Lemma
3.2
). The tangent space
T_{[J,A]}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is identified with the vector space
W_{(J,A)}
which is defined as the space of solutions to the following system of differential equations

\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}\big(\divr\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\big)=0\\
\mathrm{d}\big(\divr\big((f-1)\dot{J}\big)\circ J+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}_{0}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\circ J\big)=0\\
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\divr\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0\end{cases}

Let us denote with
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
the
1
-forms in the above system whose differential is zero and let us introduce the vector space

(6.1)

V_{(J,A)}:=\left\{(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)\ \bigg|\ \parbox{150.00023pt}{$\alpha_{1}+i\alpha_{2}$ is exact \\
$\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}(\bullet,\bullet)-J(\divr\dot{J}\wedge A)(\bullet,\bullet)=0$}\right\}

It is not difficult to see, following the lines of the proof of Lemma
4.15
and Lemma
4.17
, that
V_{(J,A)}
is invariant under the action of
\Symp(\Sigma,\rho)
and the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
. In what follows, although we will use the term "symplectic form" to denote
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
, we do not yet know whether on the spaces we are considering
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
is actually non-degenerate. In any case, with abuse of terminology, the results we are about to present still apply.

Proposition 6.1
.

There is a
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
-orthogonal decomposition

W_{(J,A)}=V_{(J,A)}\overset{\perp_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}}}{\oplus}S_{(J,A)}\ ,

where
S_{(J,A)}:=\{\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)\ |\ X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma),\ \mathrm{d}(\iota_{X}\rho)=\mathrm{d}(\iota_{JX}\rho)=0\}\cong T_{(J,A)}\big(H\cdot(J,A)\big)
is the tangent space to the harmonic orbit.

Proof.

Recall that, according to (
5.12
), for any symplectic vector field
X
on the surface and for any
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in T_{(J,A)}D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
, we have

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big((\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C);(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)=-\langle\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A})\ |\ X\rangle_{\mathfrak{S}}\ ,

where
\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A})
denotes the primitive found in Proposition
5.10
(see also Remark
5.11
). In particular, if
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in V_{(J,A)}
such a primitive equals the
1
-form
\alpha_{1}
considered in (
6.1
), hence it is exact. Using the non-degenerate symplectic pairing (
3.5
), we get

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big((\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C);(\dot{J},\dot{A})\big)=-\langle\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\dot{J},\dot{A}),X\rangle_{\mathfrak{S}}=0\ ,

for any symplectic vector field
X
and for any
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in V_{(J,A)}
. In other words,
V_{(J,A)}
is
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
-orthogonal to the symplectic orbit and it coincides with the
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
-orthogonal to
S_{(J,A)}
inside
W_{(J,A)}
. For this reason, we can conclude if we show that

V_{(J,A)}\cap S_{(J,A)}=\{0\}\ .

Suppose there exists a harmonic vector field
X
such that
(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C)\in V_{(J,A)}
. By definition of
V_{(J,A)}
, the
1
-form

\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}:=\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\langle\big(g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)_{0},(\nabla_{\bullet}A)J\rangle

is exact. Therefore,

(rel. (
3.6
))

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}\wedge\iota_{U}\rho

\displaystyle=-\langle\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big),U\rangle_{\mathfrak{S}}

\displaystyle=0,\quad\forall U\in\mathfrak{S}(\Sigma,\rho)\ .

Since
X
is harmonic, we can choose
U=JX
and obtain

(rel. (
3.6
))

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)\wedge\iota_{JX}\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)+\mathrm{d}\dot{f}\circ J-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)\big(JX\big)\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)(JX)\rho-\int_{\Sigma}(\mathrm{d}\dot{f})(X)\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)(JX)\rho-\int_{\Sigma}\big(\divr(\dot{f}X)-\dot{f}\divr(X)\big)\rho

(
X
is harmonic)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)-\frac{f^{\prime}}{6}\beta\Big)(JX)\rho\ .

Now let us compute the term

(Theorem
2.7
)

\displaystyle\beta(JX)

\displaystyle=\langle\big(g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)_{0},(\nabla_{JX}A)J\rangle

(rel. (
5.14
))

\displaystyle=-\langle\big(g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)_{0},\nabla_{X}A\rangle

(
JX
is symplectic)

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla_{X}A-\frac{3}{2}\tr(JM_X)AJ+A\big(M_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),\nabla_{X}A\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla_{X}A+A\big(M_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),\nabla_{X}A\rangle

(Theorem
2.7
)

\displaystyle=-||\nabla_{X}A||^{2}-\langle A\big(M_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),\nabla_{X}A\rangle

(
\nabla_{\bullet}J=0
)

\displaystyle=-||\nabla_{X}A||^{2}+\langle A\big(M_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),(\nabla_{JX}A)J\rangle

\displaystyle=-||\nabla_{X}A||^{2}+\langle A\big(M_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),(\nabla_{JX}A)J+A\nabla_{JX}J\rangle\ .

Applying equation (
5.15
) to the last term with
\dot{A}_{0}=\nabla_{JX}A
and
\dot{J}=\nabla_{JX}J
, we get

\displaystyle\beta(JX)

\displaystyle=-||\nabla_{X}A||^{2}+\langle A\big(M_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\big),(\nabla_{JX}A)J+A\nabla_{JX}J\rangle

\displaystyle=-||\nabla_{X}A||^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\langle AM_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}+M_{X}^{\mathrm{s}}A,\Big((\nabla_{JX}A)J+A\nabla_{JX}J\Big)_{\text{tr}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-||\nabla_{X}A||^{2}\ ,

where we used that the endomorphism part of
(\nabla_{JX}A)J

is trace-less. In order to study the divergence term, let us first make some preliminary observations. Let
L:\Gamma(T\Sigma)\to\mathrm{End}_{0}(T\Sigma,g_{J})
be the Lie derivative operator. It can be shown that its
L^{2}
-adjoint is
L^{*}(\dot{J})=-J(\divr_{g_{J}}\dot{J})^{\#}
(
[
Tro12
]
), where
\#:\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)\to\Gamma(T\Sigma)
is the musical isomorphism induced by the metric
g_{J}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}\Big(\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)\Big)(JX)\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)^{\#},JX\rangle\rho

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma}\langle J\Big(\divr\Big((f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J\Big)\Big)^{\#},X\rangle\rho

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}\langle(f-1)\mathcal{L}_{X}J,\mathcal{L}_{X}J\rho\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma}(f-1)||\mathcal{L}_{X}J||^{2}\rho\ .

Referring back to the term we are interested in, we conclude

\int_{\Sigma}(f-1)||\mathcal{L}_{X}J||^{2}\rho+\frac{1}{6}\int_{\Sigma}f^{\prime}||\nabla_{X}A||^{2}\rho=0

and, since
f,f^{\prime}
are both strictly negative, this is possible if and only if
\mathcal{L}_{X}J=\nabla_{X}A=0
. Given that on a Riemann surface
(\Sigma,J)
of genus
g\geq 2
there are no non-zero biholomorphism isotopic to the identity, it follows that
X=0
.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

The vector space
S_{(J,A)}
is a complex-symplectic subspace of
\big(W_{(J,A)},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big)
isomorphic to
H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Requiring
S_{(J,A)}
to be a complex subspace of
\big(W_{(J,A)},\mathbf{I}\big)
is equivalent to say that it is preserved by the action of the complex structure. For instance, if
(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C)\in S_{(J,A)}
then
\mathbf{I}\big(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C\big)=\big(-\mathcal{L}_{JX}J,-g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{JX}\big)
(see Lemma
4.18
). Since
X
is harmonic, i.e.
X
and
JX
are symplectic vector field, the element
\big(-\mathcal{L}_{JX}J,-g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{JX}\big)
belongs to
S_{(J,A)}
as
\mathrm{d}\big(\iota_{J^{2}X}\rho\big)=-\mathrm{d}\big(\iota_{X}\rho\big)=0
. Moreover, according to Proposition
6.1
, we have

S_{(J,A)}\cap\big(S_{(J,A)}\big)^{\perp_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}}}=\{0\}\ ,

which implies that
S_{(J,A)}
is a symplectic subspace of
\big(W_{(J,A)},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big)
endowed with the restricted symplectic form.

Now if
(\mathcal{L}_{X}J,g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{X}C)\in S_{(J,A)}
, then
\mathrm{d}(\iota_{X}\rho)=\mathrm{d}(\iota_{JX}\rho)=0
. In particular,

0=\mathrm{d}(\iota_{JX}\rho)=-\mathrm{d}(\iota_{X}\rho\circ J)

and since
\iota_{X}\rho\circ J=\ast_{J}(\iota_{X}\rho)
, we conclude that
\iota_{X}\rho
is a harmonic
1
-form. This gives a well-defined map from
S_{(J,A)}
to the space of harmonic
1
-forms on the surface, which is isomorphic to
H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
by Hodge theory. The map is an isomorphism since for any cohomology class
[\gamma]\in H^{1}_{\text{dR}}(\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
there exists a unique harmonic representative, which is of the form
\iota_{X}\rho
, for some harmonic vector field
X
on the surface (see Lemma
3.1
). ∎

Remark 6.3
.

It should be noted that the decomposition of Proposition
6.1
is also orthogonal with respect to
\mathbf{g}_{f}
. In fact,
\mathbf{g}_{f}(\cdot,\cdot)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}(\mathbf{I}\cdot,\cdot)
and using the
\mathbf{I}
-invariance of
S_{(J,A)}
it follows that

V_{(J,A)}=\big(S_{(J,A)}\big)^{\perp_{\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}}}=\big(S_{(J,A)}\big)^{\perp_{\mathbf{g}_{f}}}\subset W_{(J,A)}\ .

In Section
3.2
, we discussed how to obtain Teichmüller space by means of symplectic reduction theory and we argued how the symplectic form is actually part of a Kähler metric. If
\mu
denotes the moment map of Theorem
3.6
, the quotient space
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)=\mu^{-1}(0)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
is a smooth manifold of dimension
6g-6+2g
with a natural
H
-action. In particular, since the action is free and proper, the quotient map
p:\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is an
H
-principal bundle. On the other hand, there is a
\mathrm{MCG}(\Sigma)
-equivariant projection map
\widetilde{\pi}:\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)\to\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
which allows us to lift the
H
-action from
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma)
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
. By a standard argument, the
H
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is free and proper as well (see
[
Lab08
, Proposition 6.3.3]
).
In the end, the quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)/H
results in an identification with
\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
so that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}(\Sigma){}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}(\Sigma){}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\pi:\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{T}(\Sigma)
is the
\mathrm{MCG}(\Sigma)
-equivariant holomorphic vector bundle map given by Theorem
2.16
, and
p^{\prime}:\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{B}(\Sigma)
is the quotient projection. According to Proposition
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
, the orbits
H
-orbits in
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
are complex-symplectic submanifolds, therefore there is a well-defined complex structure
\mathbf{I}
and symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
on the quotient (see
[
Tra18
, Lemma 4.4.9]
), giving rise to a pseudo-Kähler metric on the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component. In other words, we proved the following

Theorem G
.

The
H
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\Sigma)
is free and proper, with complex and symplectic
H
-orbits. Moreover, the pseudo-Kähler structure
(\mathbf{g}_{f},\mathbf{I},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})
descend to the quotient which is identified with
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
. Finally, the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
induced on the
\SL(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin component coincides with the one found by Labourie and Loftin.

6.2.
The pseudo-metric is non-degenerate on the orbit

Here we want to study the set
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}=\{(J,A)\in D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))\ |\ \mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0\}
, namely the subspace of
D^{3}(\mathcal{J}(\Sigma))
where the Codazzi-like equation for hyperbolic affine spheres (see (
HS
)) is satisfied. The main result of this section is contained in Corollary
6.6
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
(J,A)
be a point in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
, then

T_{(J,A)}\big(\Diff(\Sigma)\cdot(J,A)\big)\subset T_{(J,A)}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}\ .

Moreover, the tangent space
T_{(J,A)}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
admits the following decomposition:

V_{(J,A)}\overset{\perp_{\mathbf{g}_{f}}}{\oplus}S_{(J,A)}\overset{\perp_{\mathbf{g}_{f}}}{\oplus}T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\overset{\perp_{\mathbf{g}_{f}}}{\oplus}\mathbf{I}\Big(T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\Big)\ .

Proof.

If
(J,A)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
, then
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0
where
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g_{J}=\rho(\cdot,J\cdot)
. In particular,
A=g_{J}^{-1}C=g_{J}^{-1}\mathcal{R}e(q)
where
q
is a
J
-complex cubic differential on
(\Sigma,J)
so that equation
\mathrm{d}^{\nabla}A=0
is equivalent to
\bar{\partial}_{J}q=0
(see Theorem
2.7
). Now let
X\in\Gamma(T\Sigma)
and consider its flow
\{\phi_{t}\}\subset\Diff(\Sigma)
, namely
X=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\phi_{t}|_{t=0}
and
\phi_{0}=\mathrm{Id}
. Let us define

J_{t}:=\mathrm{d}\phi_{t}^{-1}\circ J\circ\mathrm{d}\phi_{t},\qquad C_{t}:=C(\mathrm{d}\phi_{t}\cdot,\mathrm{d}\phi_{t}\cdot,\mathrm{d}\phi_{t}\cdot),\qquad q_{t}:=\phi_{t}^{*}q\ .

It is not difficult to show that
q
is holomorphic with respect to
J
if and only if
q_{t}
is holomorphic with respect to
J_{t}
. Therefore, to conclude the proof of the first part of the statement, we only need to show that
\mathcal{R}e(C_{t})=q_{t}
. This last identity can be proven with a computation in coordinates. In fact, let
\{x,y\}
be isothermal coordinates on the surface, so that
g_{J}=e^{u}(\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\mathrm{d}y^{2})
and
q=(P+iQ)\mathrm{d}z^{3}
, with
P+iQ
a
J
-holomorphic function. Then, we get

C=P\mathrm{d}x^{3}-3P\mathrm{d}x\odot\mathrm{d}y^{2}-3Q\mathrm{d}x^{2}\odot\mathrm{d}y+Q\mathrm{d}y^{3}\ ,

where
\odot
denotes the symmetric product. Plugging in the action of the flow
(\phi_{t})
on the expressions above for
q
and
C
gives the claim. Regarding the decomposition, we already know by Lemma
3.1
that
T_{(J,A)}\big(\Diff(\Sigma)\cdot(J,A)\big)
splits as a direct sum

T_{(J,A)}\big(H\cdot(J,A)\big)\oplus T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\oplus\mathbf{I}\Big(T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\Big)\ ,

where
H:=\Symp_{0}(\Sigma\,\rho)/\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)
. In particular, by Lemma
6.2
the tangent to the harmonic orbit is identified with
S_{(J,A)}
. Let
U
be a Hamiltonian vector field on the surface. The
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonality follows from the following computation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{f}\big((\mathcal{L}_{U}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C);\mathbf{I}(\mathcal{L}_{U}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C)\big)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}\big((\mathcal{L}_{U}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C);(\mathcal{L}_{U}J,g^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{U}C)\big)=0\ ,

and by
\mathbf{I}
-invariance of
S_{(J,A)}
, which is contained in the largest subspace in
T_{(J,A)}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}\mathcal{S}}_{0}(\Sigma,\rho)
that is
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to the Hamiltonian orbit (see Theorem
H
). Finally,
V_{(J,A)}
is
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal to the symplectic orbit by Proposition
5.12
and to the space
\mathbf{I}\Big(T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\Big)
by
\mathbf{I}
-invariance.
∎

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
(J,A)
be a point in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
. Then, the pseudo-metric
\mathbf{g}_{f}
is non-degenerate when restricted to the following subspaces:

S_{(J,A)},\ \ T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big),\ \ \mathbf{I}\Big(T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\Big).

Proof.

The pseudo-metric
\mathbf{g}_{f}
is non-degenerate on the Hamiltonian orbit as a consequence of Lemma
4.21
and Theorem
H
, indeed they imply together the following condition

T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\cap\Big(T_{(J,A)}\big(\Ham(\Sigma,\rho)\cdot(J,A)\big)\Big)^{\perp_{\mathbf{g}_{f}}}=\{0\}\ .

Moreover, the same is true on the Hamiltonian orbit after applying the complex structure
\mathbf{I}
since
\mathbf{g}_{f}(\mathbf{I}\cdot,\mathbf{I}\cdot)=\mathbf{g}_{f}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Regarding the subspace
S_{(J,A)}
, we get the thesis directly from the proof of Lemma
6.2
(see also Remark
6.3
).
∎

Corollary 6.6
.

Let
(J,A)
be a point in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
. Then, the following are equivalent:

\bullet

\mathbf{g}_{f}
is non-degenerate on
T_{(J,A)}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
;

\bullet

\mathbf{g}_{f}
is non-degenerate when restricted to
V_{(J,A)}

Proof.

The tangent space
T_{(J,A)}\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}
decomposes in the
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal direct sum of four subspaces (Lemma
6.4
). Thanks to Proposition
6.5
we know that the metric
\mathbf{g}_{f}
is non-degenerate on three out of four spaces, and the one not counted is exactly
V_{(J,A)}
. Using that the decomposition is
\mathbf{g}_{f}
-orthogonal, the thesis follows directly.
∎

6.3.
A new result regarding Goldman’s symplectic form

In
[
Gol90a
]
, Goldman gave a gauge theoretic formula for a symplectic form
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}
on the Hitchin component, which carries his name: if we identify
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
with the moduli space of flat
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-connections, by associating to
\rho\in\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
the Blaschke connection
\nabla
of the unique
\rho
-equivariant affine sphere, we can interpret tangent vectors to the Hitchin component as variations
\dot{\nabla}
of the connection. Then the pairing

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\dot{\nabla},\dot{\nabla}^{\prime})=\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\dot{\nabla} \wedge\dot{\nabla}')-\frac{1}{3}\tr(\dot{\nabla})\wedge\tr(\dot{\nabla}')

induces a non-degenerate symplectic form on
\mathrm{Hit}_{3}(\Sigma)
. The techniques we developed in Section
5.2
allows us to explicitly compute this form at the Fuchsian locus along
all
variations, tangent or transverse to the Fuchsian locus.

Recall that the Blaschke connection
\nabla
of the affine sphere with embedding data
(J,A)
can be written as
\nabla=\nabla^{h}+\sqrt{2}A
, where
\nabla^{h}
denotes the Levi-Civita connection of the Blaschke metric
h
. See also Remark
2.11
and Remark
4.5
that justify the presence of the factor
\sqrt{2}
. By the discussion in Section
2.5
, we can write
h=e^{F(\|A\|_{0}^{2})}g_{J}
, where
F
is the function introduced in Lemma
2.18
. Therefore, by standard Riemannian geometry, we have

\nabla^{h}_{X}Y=\nabla^{J}_{X}Y+\frac{1}{2}\left(X(F)Y+Y(F)X-g_{J}(X,Y)\mathrm{grad}_{J}(F)\right)\ ,

where
\nabla^{J}
and
\mathrm{grad}_{J}
denote the Levi-Civita connection and the gradient for the metric
g_{J}
, respectively. On the Fuchsian locus
A=0
, the Blaschke metric
h
is the unique hyperbolic metric in the conformal class of
J
by Equation (
HS
) and, by our choice of the area form
\rho
on
\Sigma
with volume
-2\pi\chi(\Sigma)
,
F
is constantly equal to
0
on
\Sigma
. Thus for any
(\dot{J},\dot{A})\in V_{(J,0)}
the induced variation on the Blaschke connection is

\displaystyle\dot{\nabla}_{X}Y

\displaystyle=\dot{\nabla}^{J}_{X}Y+\frac{1}{2}\big(X(\dot{F})Y+Y(\dot{F})X-\dot{g}_{J}(X,Y)\mathrm{grad}_{J}(F)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ -g_{J}(X,Y)\dot{\mathrm{grad}}_{J}(F)-g_{J}(X,Y)\mathrm{grad}_{J}(\dot{F})\big)+\dot{A}(X)Y

(
\dot{F}=0
, see Lemma 3.22 in
[
RT24
]
)

\displaystyle=\dot{\nabla}^{J}_{X}Y+\sqrt{2}\dot{A}(X)Y

(by Lemma
5.9
)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\big((\mathrm{div}\dot{J})(X)JY+J(\nabla^{J}_{X}\dot{J})Y\big)+\sqrt{2}\dot{A}(X)Y

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}J(\nabla^{J}_{X}\dot{J})Y+\sqrt{2}\dot{A}_{0}(X)Y

because the trace part of
\dot{A}
vanishes identically (see Lemma
4.3
) and the tangent vector
(\dot{J},\dot{A})
is a solution to the system (
4.15
), which on the Fuchsian locus simply says that
\dot{J}
is divergence free and
\dot{A}_{0}
is the real part of a holomorphic cubic differential. Since on the Fuchsian locus
\nabla^{J}
is equal to the Blaschke connection
\nabla
, we will suppress the superscript
J
from now on.

Proof of Theorem
D
.

We show that
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
coincide at a point on the Fuchsian locus
(J,0)
along any pair of tangent vectors. The reader may refer to Equation (
4.10
) for the explicit formula of
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
that we will use throughout the proof. Let us start with vertical variations, i.e. tangent vectors of the form
(0,\dot{A}_{0}),(0,\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime})\in V_{(J,0)}
. From the discussion above, the corresponding variations of the Blaschke connection are
\dot{\nabla}=\sqrt{2}\dot{A}_{0}
and
\dot{\nabla}^{\prime}=\sqrt{2}\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}
. Therefore,

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\dot{\nabla},\dot{\nabla}^{\prime})=2\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\dot{A}_{0} \wedge\dot{A}_{0}')=2\langle\dot{A}_{0},\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}J\rangle

because, since
\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}
is the real part of a holomorphic cubic differential, in an orthonormal basis for
g_{J}
, we have
\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}=(\dot{A}_{0})_{1}e_{1}^{*}+(\dot{A}_{0})_{1}Je_{2}^{*}
. The last expression in the above formula equals exactly
(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})_{(J,0)}((0,\dot{A}_{0}),(0,\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}))
because
f^{\prime}(0)=-3
by direct computation.

Let us now consider the pairing between a horizontal variation
(\dot{J},0)\in V_{(J,0)}
and a vertical one
(0,\dot{A}_{0})
. We need to check that, if we denote again by
\dot{\nabla}
and
\dot{\nabla}^{\prime}
the induced variations of the Blaschke connection, we have

\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\dot{\nabla},\dot{\nabla}^{\prime})=(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})_{(J,0)}((\dot{J},0),(0,\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}))=0\ .

Now, we know that
\dot{\nabla}=-\frac{1}{2}J(\nabla\dot{J})
and
\dot{\nabla}^{\prime}=\sqrt{2}\dot{A}_{0}^{\prime}
, therefore

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\dot{\nabla},\dot{\nabla}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(J\nabla\dot{J} \wedge\dot{A}_{0}')

(
\nabla J=0
)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\nabla J\dot{J} \wedge\dot{A}_{0}')

(integrating by parts)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(J\dot{J} \wedge d^{\nabla}\dot{A}_{0}')

(Equation (
4.15
) with
A=0
)

\displaystyle=0\ .

We are left with the case of two horizontal tangent vectors
(\dot{J},0),(\dot{J}^{\prime},0)\in V_{(J,0)}
, which induce the variations of Blaschke connection
\dot{\nabla}=-\frac{1}{2}J(\nabla\dot{J})
and
\dot{\nabla}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2}J(\nabla\dot{J}^{\prime})
. We compute

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\dot{\nabla},\dot{\nabla}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(J\nabla\dot{J} \wedge J\nabla\dot{J}')

(
\nabla J=0
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\nabla J\dot{J} \wedge\nabla J\dot{J}')

(integrating by parts)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(J\dot{J} \wedge d^{\nabla}(\nabla J\dot{J}'))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(J\dot{J} \wedge[-R^{\nabla}, J\dot{J}'])

because
d^{\nabla}\circ\nabla
is the curvature operator of the connection on the bundle
\mathrm{End}(T\Sigma)
, which coincides with the adjoint action of the Riemann tensor
R^{\nabla}
. The minus sign in the above formula comes from our conventions in the definition of the Riemann tensor (see page 52). Since
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of the hyperbolic metric compatible with
J
, we have
R^{\nabla}=-J\otimes\rho
, hence

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}(\dot{\nabla},\dot{\nabla}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(J\dot{J} \wedge[-R^{\nabla}, J\dot{J}'])

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(J\dot{J}[J, J\dot{J}'])\rho

(
J\dot{J}^{\prime}=-\dot{J}^{\prime}J
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(J\dot{J}\dot{J}')\rho

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\tr(\dot{J}J\dot{J}')\rho

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f})_{(J,0)}((\dot{J},0),(\dot{J}^{\prime},0))\ .

Note that this last computation is compatible with the fact that Goldman symplectic form on Teichmüller space is equal to four times the Weil-Petersson symplectic form.

By bilinearity, we conclude that
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{G}
coincides with
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
on the Fuchsian locus along all pairs of tangent vectors.
∎

Theorem
D
implies that the pairing between Goldman symplectic form on the Hitchin component and the Labourie-Loftin complex structure cannot give rise to a positive definite Kähler structure. Moreover, it is reasonable to conjecture that Goldman symplectic form coincides with our
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{f}
at all points.
